Cusp forms and parabolic cohomology classes for symmetric spaces of rank one
Roelof Bruggeman, YoungJu Choie, Roberto Miatello, Anke Pohl

Abstract
. For any rank-one Riemannian symmetric space S S of non-compact type and any discrete, cofinite, non-cocompact, torsion-free group Γ \Gamma of orientation-preserving Riemannian isometries on S S , we develop a cohomological interpretation for the cusp forms of Γ \Gamma . To that end, we identify certain Γ \Gamma -submodules of smooth semi-analytic vectors in the spherical principal series representation with spectral parameter ν \nu as well as certain subspaces of parabolic cohomology spaces of Γ \Gamma of degree dim S − 1 \dim S-1 with these Γ \Gamma -submodules. We provide explicit isomorphisms between the spaces of cusp forms of spectral parameter ν \nu and these specific cohomology subspaces. The isomorphisms from cusp forms to cohomology are given by an integral transform, and the explicit form of the inverse isomorphism takes advantage of a certain reproducing property of the integral transform. The result is uniform for all these symmetric spaces and does not rely on their classification.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 11F75, 32N15; Secondary: 22E30, 22E40

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 11F75, 32N15; Secondary: 11F55, 22E40

1.
Introduction

Let
S
be a Riemannian symmetric space of non-compact type and of rank
one, and let
\Gamma
be a discrete group of orientation-preserving
Riemannian isometries of
S
that is torsion-free and cofinite, but not
cocompact. In a nutshell, our main result is a cohomological interpretation of cusp forms for
\Gamma
.
In what follows, we will provide a more detailed description of this result. By its very nature, the proof of this result is rather lengthy and involved. For the convenience of the reader we will therefore first deliver a reduced explanation that allows us to provide, with Theorem
A
, a partial statement of our main result that can be stated and surveyed (but not proved) with a reduced number of entities used for intermediate steps. We will then deepen this explanation by surveying the key steps in our approach, therefore giving a glimpse on some of the additional entities used, and finally state, with Theorem
B
, the full version of our main result. Thereafter we will discuss our motivation and provide a brief survey of the relation between our main result to previous results in this research area. Afterwards we will give an overview of the structure of this article.

1.1.
Main result

Throughout let
S
denote an arbitrary rank-one
Riemannian symmetric space of non-compact type.

According to É. Cartan’s magnificent classification of Lie groups
and symmetric spaces, there are four types of rank-one
(irreducible) Riemannian symmetric spaces of non-compact type, namely the three families of the hyperbolic spaces
over the real numbers, the complex numbers, and the quaternions, and
the one exceptional space, often considered to be the two-dimensional
hyperbolic space over the Cayley numbers.

For our purposes here, we will use the presentation of Riemannian
symmetric spaces as quotients of connected Lie groups by certain
compact subgroups. We refer to Section
2
for details. In our
situation it means that we may and shall identify
S
with the
Riemannian symmetric space
G/K
, where
G
is the connected component
of the identity element of the group of Riemannian isometries on
S
,
and
K
is the stabilizer group in
G
of an arbitrarily chosen point

\text{{{e}}}\in S
. Then
G
is the group of orientation-preserving Riemannian isometries of
S
and a connected simple Lie group of real rank one
with trivial center,
K
is a maximal compact subgroup of
G
, and the
Riemannian structure on
G/K
as a symmetric space is essentially
unique. Using this presentation, our approach and our proofs do not rely on the classification of Riemannian symmetric spaces mentioned above.

Throughout, we let
\Gamma
be a discrete, torsion-free subgroup of
G

that is cofinite, but not cocompact, and we let
\Delta
denote the (positive) Laplacian on
S
. The cusp forms for
\Gamma
are the
\Gamma
-invariant eigenfunctions of
\Delta
that have quick decay at each cusp of
\Gamma
. (Of course, we could define these forms as being the eigenfunctions of the Laplacian on the Riemannian locally symmetric space
\Gamma\backslash S=\Gamma\backslash G/K
with quick decay towards each end. However, working with “lifted” functions and asking for invariance has some advantages in our considerations.)

For each cusp form
u
we parametrize its
\Delta
-eigenvalue
\lambda=\lambda(u)
as
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
, where
\rho
is
essentially half the sum of the positive roots of
S
. More precisely, for convenience, we deviate here slightly from standard conventions and define
\rho
to be the positive half-integer that corresponds to the half sum of the positive roots using standard normalizations (see Section
2.2
). The element
\nu
is a complex number depending on
u
, unique only up to sign. Each choice of
\nu
is called a
spectral parameter
of
u
. We denote the space of cusp forms for
\Gamma
with spectral parameter
\nu
by
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
.

For the cohomological interpretation of the cusp forms, the set of cusps
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
of
\Gamma
, considered as a set of points in the geodesic boundary
\partial S
of
S
, plays a prominent role. Using the resolution
F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}
with
F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!j}=\mathbb{C}\bigl[{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}^{j+1}\bigr]
for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the parabolic cohomology space
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
of degree
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module
V
arises in the same manner as the standard cohomology spaces of
\Gamma
arise from the standard resolution. See Section
4.3
. However, as the action of
\Gamma
on
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
is not free, parabolic cohomology spaces are in general not isomorphic to the standard cohomology spaces. For our purposes we require a realization of the parabolic cohomology spaces using a resolution based on a
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation of
S^{\!\ast}=S\cup{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
into cells of different dimensions such that the boundary components of higher dimensional cells are made of cells of lower dimensions in a highly controlled way.
With this somewhat refined resolution, the geometry of
S^{\!\ast}
and the action of
\Gamma
on
S^{\!\ast}
can be sufficiently tracked via the resolution and the parabolic cohomology spaces. We refer to Sections
4.2
-
4.6
, and in particular to Proposition
4.13
, for detailed discussions. In what follows, the tessellation is denoted
{\mathcal{T}}=(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i})_{i=0}^{{\mathrm{d}}}
with
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
being the collection of
i
-dimensional cells for
i=0,\ldots,{\mathrm{d}}
where
{\mathrm{d}}=\dim S
, and the associated resolution is denoted
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
.

For the choice of suitable
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-modules
V
, we take advantage of the relation between cusp forms for
\Gamma
and principal series representations. We initially use the
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module
\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
of smooth semi-analytic vectors in the spherical principal series representation with spectral parameter
\nu
. We realize these vectors as (germs of) smooth, semi-analytic functions on the geodesic boundary
\partial S
of
S
.
The points of (potential) non-analyticity of elements of
\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
are contained in
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. See Section
6
for a full definition.

We develop a certain differential form on
S
defined in detail in (
6.14
), which is denoted here
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,\cdot)
, depending on
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
,
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and
b\in\partial S
. It is built by means of a generalization of Green’s form and of a kernel function on
(\partial S)\times G
of Poisson-type defined in (
6.2
), where the latter is based on a real-analytic vector in the spherical principal series representation.
For
f
being a cusp form (i.e., for
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
) the form
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,\cdot)
is closed. Using the absolutely convergent integral

(1.1)

I(f,X;b)=\int_{x\in X}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,x)

for
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
,
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
and
b\in\partial S
, the map

(1.2)

\psi^{f}\colon\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\to\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)\,,\quad X\mapsto I(f,X;\cdot)\,,

assigns to
f
the cocycle
\psi^{f}
in the space
Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)\bigr)
. Here,
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
denotes the
G
-module of smooth vectors on
\partial S
in the principal series.
The assignment in (
1.2
) descends to the linear map

(1.3)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)\,,\quad f\mapsto[\psi^{f}]\,,

where
[\psi^{f}]
denotes the cohomology class of
\psi^{f}
. See Section
6
.

The map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
typically is not bijective, and hence we are interested in better understanding its image and under which conditions it is injective. We first observe that the strong decay of cusp forms and their derivatives towards the cusps of
\Gamma
yields that for any
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
, the cocycle
\psi^{f}
maps into the sheaf of smooth semi-analytic vectors
\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
in the specific manner that

\psi^{f}(X)\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus(X\cap{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)})\bigr)

for each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
.
Thus, the points of potential non-analyticity of
\psi^{f}(X)
are precisely those cusps that are “seen” by
X
. We say that cocycles with this property satisfy the
boundary condition

{\mathrm{bdc}}
. Thus, the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in (
1.3
) descends to the map

(1.4)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,,\quad f\mapsto[\psi^{f}]\,,

for any
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. See Theorem
6.6
. We emphasize that we continue to denote all specifications of
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
from (
1.3
) by the same symbol.

Furthermore, at least for spectral parameters
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
obeying
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
and
\re\nu>-\rho
,
the regularity of cusp forms or, equivalently, of smooth semi-analytic vectors in combination with the contraction property of the action of
\Gamma
towards cusps yields that the cocycles
\psi^{f}
for
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
extend real-analytically and with highly controlled growth to the part in
S
of so-called excised neighborhoods of
(\partial S)\smallsetminus E
, where
E
is the finite set of cusps of non-analyticity of
\psi^{f}
. We refer to Section
9.3
, in particular to Definitions
9.11
and
9.12
as well as Proposition
9.4
, for details.
We denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
the
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module (or the sheaf) of elements in
\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
with these additional extension properties. Then the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in (
1.4
) restricts to the map

(1.5)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,,\quad f\mapsto[\psi^{f}]\,.

We emphasize that in (
1.5
) we changed our initial choice of the
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module
V
being
\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
to its submodule
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
.

With further restrictions on the values for
\nu
, we can show that the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in (
1.5
) is indeed an isomorphism, as stated in the following partial statement of our main theorem.

Theorem A

(partial statement of main theorem)
.

Let
S
be a rank-one Riemannian symmetric of
non-compact type and let
\Gamma
be a cofinite, torsion-free discrete
group of orientation-preserving Riemannian isometries of
S
that is
not cocompact. For any (spectral) parameter

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
with
|\re\nu|<\rho
, the map

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,,\quad f\mapsto[\psi^{f}]\,,

is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

For the proof of Theorem
A
we employ several further entities and establish intermediate isomorphisms, as surveyed in what follows. In this way, we prove a stronger version of Theorem
A
in the sense that we provide an explicit formulation of the inverse isomorphism.

To establish the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
as in (
1.4
), i.e., as a map into
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
, we proceed via a careful analysis of the properties of cusp forms and the integral in (
1.1
). Additional entities are used for determining the image of
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
, i.e., establishing it as the map in (
1.5
), and showing that it is an isomorphism for a suitable restricted regime for
\nu
.

In a first step, we relate the sheaf
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
to
\nu
-analytic boundary germs. In a nutshell, the space of boundary germs with spectral parameter
\nu
forms a sheaf on
\partial S
, which is the direct limit of smooth Laplace eigenfunctions with spectral parameter
\nu
. A
\nu
-analytic boundary germ on an open subset
I
of
\partial S
is a boundary germ that extends analytically across
\partial S
in the following sense: We use an analytic extension
\hat{S}
of
S
in which
\partial S
, and hence
I
, is in the interior. The
\nu
-analytic boundary germ is then represented by a smooth Laplace eigenfunction
u
on
U\cap S
for some open neighborhood
U
of
I
in
\hat{S}
with the additional property that there is an analytic function
A_{u}
on
U
such that

u(x)=t(x)^{-(\rho+\nu)}A_{u}(x)

for all
x\in U\cap S
, where
t(x)
is, in a certain sense, the vertical distance of
x
from
\partial S
. For details see Section
8
. We denote by
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)
the space of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs on
I
. The restriction map
\rho_{\nu}\colon u\mapsto A_{u}
is a
G
-equivariant sheaf morphism
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
for all
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
; for
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
it is an isomorphism. See Theorem
8.5
. These properties are preserved for the extension to
\nu
-semi-analytic entities and induce morphisms
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}\to\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
as well as
\mathcal{W}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\to\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
(isomorphisms for
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
), both denoted
\rho_{\nu}
.

For
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, the isomorphism
\rho_{\nu}
between
\mathcal{W}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
and
\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}
in combination with the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in (
1.4
) yields the map

(1.6)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}=\rho_{\nu}^{-1}\circ\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,,\quad f\mapsto[\rho_{\nu}^{-1}(\psi^{f})]\,.

For
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in this “lifted” formulation, a thorough analysis of the preimage of the differential form
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;\cdot,x)
and the integral
I(f,X;\cdot)
from (
1.1
) under
\rho_{\nu}
allows us to detect that the image of
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is indeed in
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
, at least for
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
obeying
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
and
\re\nu>-\rho
. See Section
9
, in particular Propositions
9.4
and
9.13
. As
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
is, by definition, the preimage of
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
under the isomorphism
\rho_{\nu}
, at this stage we have established the cohomology space in the image of
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in Theorem
A
.

In the next step, to show that the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is an isomorphism between
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
and
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
obeying
|\re\nu|<\rho
, we work again with the (isomorphic) map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
and show that it is an isomorphism between
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
and
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
. To that end we construct an explicit inverse map to
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
obeying
|\re\nu|<\rho
.

We start with providing, for

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
with
|\re\nu|<\rho
, a map

(1.7)

\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}\colon H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)

that is left inverse to
\mathbf{i}\circ\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
, where
\mathbf{i}
is the inclusion map from
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
to
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
, and where
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
denotes the sheaf of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs that satisfy the extension property mentioned above but for which the extension map is not necessarily smooth at the points of non-analyticity.

To establish this map we provide a specific construction that assigns to each cocycle class
[\psi]\in H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
a Laplace eigenfunction
u_{\psi}
with spectral parameter
\nu
that is defined on all of
S
, not only on some subset of
S
as
a priori
requested by the definition of boundary germs, such that the
\mathcal{W}_{\!\!}
-version of the integral transform
I
in (
1.1
) is locally reproducing for
u_{\psi}
. See Proposition
10.5
. This is the most crucial result for building the inverse map. The definition of
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is constructive, rather explicit and takes advantage of the realization of the cohomology spaces using a tessellation of
S^{\!\ast}
. Further, under our restrictions on
\nu
, the map
u_{\psi}
is indeed in
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
, and if
[\psi]
arises from an element
u\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
, then
u_{\psi}=u
. See Propositions
10.5
and
10.8
.

While these results show that
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is surjective and a left-inverse to
\mathbf{i}\circ\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
, it still admits the possibility that two different cocycle classes in
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
, say
[\psi_{1}],[\psi_{2}]
with
[\psi_{1}]\not=[\psi_{2}]
, are mapped to the same element in
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
, thus
u_{\psi_{1}}=u_{\psi_{2}}
. At this point in the argumentation, we cannot even rule out this possibility for cocycle classes in
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
. Hence, this possibility still prevents us from concluding if
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is an isomorphism or not. Therefore, in Section
11
we explicitly determine the kernel of
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
for
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
,
|\re\nu|<\rho
, satisfying
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. See Proposition
11.3
.

By means of sheaf cohomology we relate this kernel, which uses a certain space of global representatives for the
\nu
-analytic boundary germs with sufficient regularity properties, to a cohomology space with a certain module,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
resp.
{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
, of Laplace eigenfunctions. See (
11.15
) and Proposition
11.9
. In Section
12
we show that under the additional condition that
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, the kernel is indeed trivial. See Theorem
12.1
. Combining these results with the isomorphism
\rho_{\nu}
gives the full statement of our main result, which we can state now. We emphasize that (

i

) in this theorem handles more values of
\nu
than (

ii

).

Theorem B

(full statement of main result)
.

Let
S
be a rank-one Riemannian symmetric of
non-compact type and let
\Gamma
be a cofinite, torsion-free discrete
group of orientation-preserving Riemannian isometries of
S
that is
not cocompact. Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
,
|\re\nu|<\rho
.

\mathrm{(i)}

If
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, then the map

\beta_{\nu}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

from (
1.5
) induces a linear isomorphism between
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
and the quotient

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\bigm/H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\rho_{\nu}{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,.

\mathrm{(ii)}

If
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, then
\beta_{\nu}

is a linear bijection.

1.2.
Motivation and relation to previous results

Our investigations in this article are a natural continuation of the studies of cohomological interpretations of Maass cusp forms and more general automorphic forms in, e.g.,
[
12
,
30
,
4
,
8
,
6
,
5
,
7
]
. We refer the reader to the introductional sections of these references for a survey of the development of this research field and for further references, and we restrict ourselves here to a brief explanation of the relation of the present article to this previous results. The results in
[
12
,
30
,
4
,
8
,
6
,
5
]
are for the situation of Fuchsian groups and hyperbolic orbisurfaces, whereas
[
7
]
is the (to our knowledge) first result beyond hyperbolic orbisurfaces, namely for Jacobi Maass cusp forms. In the present article we vastly go beyond hyperbolic orbisurfaces (hence two-dimensional spaces) and study arbitrary rank-one locally symmetric Riemannian symmetric spaces of non-compact type that are non-compact but of finite volume. In view of the length of this article, we here restrict to torsion-free fundamental groups to avoid to spend space on additional, necessary discussions.

In our approach, we follow as much as possible the approach in
[
4
]
. However, the presence of more dimensions in the considered spaces as well as the more involved geometry in particular for hyperbolic spaces over the complex numbers or the quaternions as well as for the exceptional space yield new obstacles to overcome and require to adapt several steps.

In the situation of the hyperbolic plane and Fuchsian groups, as handled in
[
4
]
and also in
[
12
]
, the passage from (Maass) cusp forms to cocycle classes is given by an integral transform for which the considered cusp form is integrated in a suitably chosen differential form against a suitably chosen kernel function along a well-chosen path (here, a geodesic) with a certain geometric meaning. See, in particular,
[
12
, §2.1]
and
[
4
, §5]
. This structure is also visible in
[
30
]
(also a cohomological interpretation is discussed in there) and it is highly present in essentially all further articles discussing cohomological interpretations of automorphic forms. We could preserve this structure also in the much more general rank-one situations that we consider here by developing a suitable kernel and exchanging the geodesics used as path of integration by tesselation elements of dimension
{\mathrm{d}}-1
.

Moreover, the cohomology space in the image of the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is more involved than in dimension
{\mathrm{d}}=2
. See Theorem
B
.
In particular, for
{\mathrm{d}}=2
, the kernel of the map
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
in (
1.7
) is trivial for all
\nu\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
whereas for higher dimensions this is not necessarily the case. This issue leads to a much more involved argumentation than in the case of hyperbolic surfaces. In Section
13
we provide a more elaborate comparison.

1.3.
Outline of article

In Section
2
we provide a survey of the necessary background material on Lie theory and symmetric spaces. In Section
3
we discuss the class of discrete groups that we consider here and we present the notion of automorphic forms and cusp forms that we will use. With Section
4
the main bulk of work in this article starts. In Section
4
we present the necessary concept of subgroup-invariant tessellation and develop parabolic cohomology based on a tessellation. In Section
5
we discuss the spherical principal series and their realization on
\partial S
. These preparations allow us to develop, in Section
6
, a kernel function that generalizes the kernel function from
[
4
]
and further to construct an integral transform that associates a cocycle with values in the spherical principal series to any given cusp form. This provides the passage from cusp forms to cocycle classes. In Sections
7
–
12
we study the image of this map and discuss its inverse map under the restrictions on the spectral parameter also presented above.
In the concluding Section
13
we comment on our approach and its relation to
[
4
]
and discuss as well some potential continuations of this work.

1.4.
Acknowledgements

YJC was partially supported by the grants NRF-2022R1A2B5B0100187111 and BSRI-2021R1A6A1A1004294412. Further, we would like to thank the Mathematisches Forschungsinstitut Oberwolfach (MFO) for the 2025 workshop “Cohomology theories for Automorphic forms and Enumerative Algebra” and its hospitality, which provided us with an opportunity to meet in person for an intensive period of research time.

2.
Lie groups and symmetric spaces

In this section we provide a review of background information on
Riemannian symmetric spaces and Lie groups needed for our purpose. For
proofs and further details, we usually refer to
[
38
]
.
Alternative standard references are, e.g.,
[
22
,
20
,
11
]
.

We start with a survey of the most essential concepts. We then provide
further discussion of technical facts concerning the Riemannian metric
as well as of notations and methods that we will use.

2.1.
Lie groups and symmetric spaces of rank
one

By de Rham decomposition, any Riemannian symmetric space of
non-compact type decomposes into a direct product of irreducible
Riemannian symmetric spaces of non-compact type. Any irreducible
Riemannian symmetric space
S
of non-compact type can be presented as a
quotient space
G/K
with a non-compact connected simple real Lie group

G
and a maximal compact subgroup
K
. For any given
S
, the choices
of
G
and
K
are not unique, not even up to isomorphisms of Lie
groups. However, this non-uniqueness is highly controlled. Namely, the
Lie group
G
may have a nontrivial center, which is then automatically
contained in
K
. By choosing
G
connected and with a
trivial center, which we may and shall do here,
G
as well as
K
are
unique up to isomorphisms of Lie groups.

Regarding this choice,
G
is isomorphic to the group of
orientation-preserving isometries of
S\cong G/K
, and it is also
isomorphic to a subgroup of
\GL({\mathfrak{g}})
for the Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
of
G
. The rank of the Riemannian symmetric
space
S
coincides with the real rank of the Lie group
G
. Thus,
throughout we will choose
G
to be a non-compact simple connected real
Lie group of real rank one with trivial center.

We will take advantage of several standard decompositions of
G
, which
we will survey in what follows.

The Iwasawa decomposition of
G
.
We fix an

Iwasawa decomposition

G=NAK
, with
N
maximal unipotent in
G
,
K

maximal compact in
G
and
A\cong\mathbb{R}^{\ast}_{>0}
. All Iwasawa
decompositions are conjugate to each other. See,
e.g.,
[
38
, §2.1]
.

The
symmetric space
of
G
is the
quotient
G/K=:S
. The group
G
acts on
S
by
multiplication on the left. We denote its real dimension by

{\mathrm{d}}
.

The dimension of
A
is the real rank of
G
. In this paper, this rank
is
1
, and
A
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}_{>0}
. We
choose a continuous group homomorphism

\mathrm{a}\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\ast}\rightarrow A
. Then
there is a unique element
{\mathbf{H}}_{0}
in the Lie
algebra
{\mathfrak{a}}
of
A
such that
\mathrm{a}(e^{x})=\exp(x{\mathbf{H}}_{0})
.

The unipotent group
N
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
as an analytic
manifold. For many groups
G
of rank one the subgroup
N
is not
abelian. Then
N
is not isomorphic to the additive group

\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
. In all cases, the commutator subgroup of
N
is equal to
the center of
N
.

An important subgroup is
M=\bigl\{m\in K\;:\;mam^{-1}=a\text{ for all }a\in A\bigr\}
.
We can choose an element
\mathrm{w}\in K
such that

\mathrm{w}\mathrm{a}(t)\mathrm{w}^{-1}=\mathrm{a}(1/t)
for all
t>0
. We can
and shall arrange the choice in
\mathrm{w}M
such that
\mathrm{w}^{-1}=\mathrm{w}
. See

[
21
, Lemma 1.5]
.

The Iwasawa decomposition is unique: it gives a diffeomorphism

N\times A\times K\rightarrow G\colon(n,a,k)\mapsto nak\,.

Two more decompositions for
G
.
The
Cartan decomposition

G=KAK
leads to a
diffeomorphism
(K/M)\times A^{+}\times K\cong G\smallsetminus K
with

A^{+}=\bigl\{\mathrm{a}(t)\;:\;t>1\bigr\}
. See

[
38
, § 2.4.2]
for these facts.

For rank one groups the
Bruhat
decomposition
takes the form

(2.1)

G=NAM\sqcup N\mathrm{w}MAN\,.

See
[
38
, Theorem 2.2.10]
. We note that
M
and
A
commute, and
that both normalize
N
. So we have
MAN=NAM=NMA
.

Analytic structure.
A function on
G
is
analytic
if it can locally be given as an analytic function of the
matrix entries in the realization of
G\subset\GL({\mathfrak{g}})
(with a
choice of a basis). We use ‘analytic’ to mean ‘real-analytic’: locally
given by power series in analytic coordinates. This provides
G
with
the structure of an analytic manifold.

This induces an analytic structure on closed subgroups of
G
. For
instance the coordinate function
t\leftrightarrow\mathrm{a}(t)
on
A
is
analytic. The diffeomorphism
S\cong NA
determines an analytic
structure on
S
.

The symmetric space and its boundary.
We denote by

\text{{{e}}}\in S
the image of the unit element

e\in G
.

The map
na\mapsto na\,\text{{{e}}}
(based on the Iwasawa decomposition)
gives a diffeomorphism
NA\rightarrow S
. Similarly the Cartan
decomposition gives the map
(k,t)\mapsto k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
, giving a
diffeomorphism
(K/M)A^{+}\rightarrow S\smallsetminus\{\text{{{e}}}\}
. As

t\uparrow\infty
the point
k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
approaches the boundary

\partial S=K/M

of
S
.
We name two special points of
\partial S
:

(2.2)

{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\text{{{o}}}=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{w}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}=\mathrm{w}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathrm{a}(t)\text{{{e}}}\,.

We use the boldface version
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
for the particular point of

\partial S
fixed by
MAN
; by
\infty
we mean the standard infinity
in the two-point compactification of
\mathbb{R}
.

The Iwasawa decomposition can be inverted to give the opposite Iwasawa
decomposition
G=KAN
. This implies that
\partial S=K/M
is
isomorphic to
G/MAN
. The subgroup of
G
fixing
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
is
MAN
. The
Bruhat decomposition (
2.1
) shows that

(2.3)

\partial S=\bigl\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}\sqcup N\,\text{{{o}}}\,.

Action of
G
on functions on
G
and on

S
.
The group
G
acts on itself by left and
by right translations. That induces representations of
G
in

C^{\infty}(G)
:

(2.4)

\displaystyle R(g_{1})f

\displaystyle\colon g\mapsto f(gg_{1})\,,

\displaystyle L(g_{1})f

\displaystyle\colon g\mapsto f(g_{1}^{-1}g)\,.

The inverse in the definition of
L
is necessary if one works with left
representations, which satisfy
L(g_{1}g_{2})=L(g_{1})L(g_{2})
.

Action of the Lie algebra.
The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, which will be
discussed in §
2.2
in more detail, acts on functions in

C^{\infty}(G)
by left and right differentiation: For each

{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
there is a group homomorphism

\mathbb{R}\rightarrow G\colon t\mapsto\exp(t{\mathbf{X}})
that has at
t=0
the
direction corresponding to
{\mathbf{X}}
. We denote the corresponding derived
actions of
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
by the same symbols
R
and
L
:

(2.5)

\displaystyle R({\mathbf{X}})f

\displaystyle\colon g\mapsto\partial_{t}f\bigl(g\exp(t{\mathbf{X}})\bigr)\bigm|_{t=0}\,,

\displaystyle L({\mathbf{X}})f

\displaystyle\colon g\mapsto\partial_{t}f\bigl(\exp(-t{\mathbf{X}})g\bigr)\bigm|_{t=0}\,.

These actions extend
\mathbb{C}
-linearly to elements of the complex Lie
algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{c}=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{g}}
. We note
that the actions
R
by right translation and
L
by left translation
commute.

Functions
f
on
S
correspond to functions on
G
that satisfy

R(k)f=f
for all
k\in K
. Thus, we can use
L
to denote the action
of
G
on functions on
S
and the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}
on differentiable
functions on
S
.

A function
f\in C^{\infty}(G)
is
right

K
-finite
if the functions
R(k)f
with

k\in K
generate a finite-dimensional space, which is then

R(K)
-invariant. By
C^{\infty}(G)_{K}
we
denote the subspace of right
K
-finite functions in
C^{\infty}(G)
.
This subspace is invariant under
R({\mathbf{X}})
for all
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
, but
not under
R(g)
for all
g\in G
.

The Riemannian structure of
S
.
The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of

G
can be defined as the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{\!e}G
at the unit element

e
of
G
, and the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
at
\text{{{e}}}\in S
can be identified with a
linear subspace of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The Lie algebra structure provides

\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
with a positive definite bilinear form, which can be
transported by the action of
G
on
S
to all tangent spaces

\mathrm{T}_{\!x}S
, thus providing
S
with a
G
-invariant linear Riemannian
structure. We will specify it in (
2.41
).

Laplace operator:

The Riemannian structure
determines the Laplace operator. This is a second order differential
operator on
S
. It commutes with the action of
G
:

(2.6)

\Delta L(g)f=L(g)\Delta f

for
g\in G
and
f\in C^{2}(U)
with
U\subset S
open.

The operator
\Delta
is an elliptic differential operator with analytic
coefficients. Hence its eigenfunctions are analytic.

Distance function:

The Riemannian metric determines a distance
function

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\colon S\times S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

which turns
S
into a metric space. It satisfies

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(gx,gy)={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,y)
for all
g\in G
,
x,y\in S
.

Volume form:

The Riemannian structure also determines a
{\mathrm{d}}
-form

{\zeta}_{S}
on
S
that satisfies
{\zeta}_{S}\circ l_{g}={\zeta}_{S}

for all
g\in G
, where
l_{g}:x\mapsto g\,x
. By

f\mapsto\int_{S}f\,{\zeta}_{S}
,
f\in C_{c}(S)
, we obtain an invariant
positive measure
d\mu
on
S
that
is invariant under
f\mapsto L(g)f
for all
g\in G
.

Some references.
The facts mentioned above concern the common
structure of Lie groups of real rank one, as discussed in
[
38
]
,

[
25
]
,
[
26
]
and also
[
22
]
. One may consult
also
[
9
,
10
]
, and
[
24
]
for alternative approaches.

2.2.
The Lie algebra of
G

The
tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{\!e}G={\mathfrak{g}}
has the structure of a Lie algebra,
given by a bilinear operation

{\mathfrak{g}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}:({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}})\mapsto[{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}]
. To
closed subgroups correspond Lie subalgebras, which we denote by the
corresponding lower case fraktur letter:
{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathrm{Lie}(A)
,

{\mathfrak{n}}=\mathrm{Lie}(N)
, etc.
The Iwasawa decomposition implies
that the vector space
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the direct sum

{\mathfrak{n}}\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}\oplus{\mathfrak{k}}
of vector spaces.

The choice of the isomorphism
\mathrm{a}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\ast}_{>0}\rightarrow A

determines an element
{\mathbf{H}}_{0}\in{\mathfrak{a}}
. The operator

\ad({\mathbf{H}}_{0}):{\mathbf{X}}\mapsto[{\mathbf{H}}_{0},{\mathbf{X}}]
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
can be brought into
diagonal form, with eigenvalues in
\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
. This corresponds
to the fact that
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})
has a one-dimensional root system of
the form
\{\alpha,0,-\alpha\}
or
\{2\alpha,\alpha,0,-\alpha,-2\alpha\}
, where
\alpha

is the linear form on
{\mathfrak{a}}
that satisfies
\alpha({\mathbf{H}}_{0})=1
. For any

j\in\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2\}
, the root space
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j\alpha}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}

consists of the elements
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
such that

\bigl[{\mathbf{H}},{\mathbf{X}}\bigr]=j\,\alpha({\mathbf{H}}){\mathbf{X}}
for all
{\mathbf{H}}\in{\mathfrak{a}}
. This
gives a root space decomposition

(2.7)

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-2\alpha}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-\alpha}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}\,.

The simple root
\alpha
is a
linear form on the one-dimensional space
{\mathfrak{a}}
. Each
\nu\in\mathbb{C}

determines a character of
A
given by

(2.8)

\mathrm{a}(t)\mapsto\mathrm{a}(t)^{\nu\alpha}\coloneqq t^{\nu}\,.

We have
{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}
. If
N
is abelian then

{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}=\{0\}
, otherwise
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}
is the Lie algebra of
the center
Z_{N}
of
N
. The
Weyl group
of
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})
is a group of order two; the
non-trivial element can be represented by
\mathrm{w}
. This element
\mathrm{w}

normalizes the group
M
. We have

\Ad(\mathrm{w}){\mathfrak{g}}_{j\alpha}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-j\alpha}
. The sum

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-\alpha}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2\alpha}
is the Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{n}}^{-}=\Ad(\mathrm{w}){\mathfrak{n}}
of the opposite unipotent group
N^{-}
, which
is equal to
\mathrm{w}N\mathrm{w}^{-1}
. Furthermore,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{a}}\oplus{\mathfrak{m}}
.

We put

(2.9)

\rho=\rho_{G}=\Bigl(\frac{p}{2}+q\Bigr)\,,\qquad p=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}\,,\quad q=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}\,.

The linear form
\rho\alpha
on
{\mathfrak{a}}
is half the sum of the positive
roots (with multiplicities).

2.2.1.
Killing form and choice of basis

For
each
{\mathbf{Y}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
we have the operator

\ad({\mathbf{Y}}):{\mathbf{X}}\mapsto[{\mathbf{Y}},{\mathbf{X}}]

in
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The Killing form is the bilinear form on
{\mathfrak{g}}
given by

({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}})\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}\bigl(\ad({\mathbf{X}})\ad({\mathbf{Y}})\bigr)
. For the
semisimple group
G
this form is non-degenerate. We multiply it by a
positive quantity such that
({\mathbf{H}}_{0},{\mathbf{H}}_{0})\mapsto 1
, and denote this

normalized Killing form

by
B
.

A
Cartan involution

{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}
is an involution of
{\mathfrak{g}}
that preserves the
Lie algebra structure. See
[
38
, §2.1.1, §2.1.4]
. The choice
of a Cartan involution corresponds to the choice of a maximal compact
subgroup, on which the Cartan involution has eigenvalue
1
. We use the
Cartan involution
\theta
such that

{\mathfrak{k}}
is the
1
-eigenspace of
\theta
. By
{\mathfrak{p}}
we denote the

(-1)
-eigenspace. It contains
\mathbb{R}{\mathbf{H}}_{0}={\mathfrak{a}}
. We can find a basis
of
{\mathfrak{g}}

(2.10)

\bigl\{{\mathbf{H}}_{0},{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{X}}_{p+q},\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{p+q},{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{U}}_{\dim({\mathfrak{m}})}\bigr\}

with the following properties

\mathrm{(i)}

{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{X}}_{p}
span
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathfrak{n}}
, and

{\mathbf{X}}_{p+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{X}}_{p+q}
span
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}=\mathrm{Lie}(Z_{N})
;

\mathrm{(ii)}

\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{p}
span
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-\alpha}\subset{\mathfrak{n}}^{-}
,
and
\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{p+1},\ldots,\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{p+q}
span

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2\alpha}=\mathrm{Lie}(Z_{N^{-}})
;

\mathrm{(iii)}

{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{U}}_{\dim({\mathfrak{m}})}
span
{\mathfrak{m}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
;

\mathrm{(iv)}

the basis in (
2.10
) and the basis

(2.11)

\bigl\{{\mathbf{H}}_{0},-\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,-\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{p+q},-{\mathbf{X}}_{1},\ldots,-{\mathbf{X}}_{p+q},-{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\ldots,-{\mathbf{U}}_{\dim({\mathfrak{m}})}\bigr\}

are dual with respect to
B
. (This means that
B(a,b)=1
if
a
and
b

have the same position in both bases, and
B(a,b)=0
otherwise.)

\mathrm{(v)}

\bigl[{\mathbf{X}}_{i},\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\bigr]=\begin{cases}-{\mathbf{H}}_{0}&\text{ for }1\leq i\leq p\,,\\
-2{\mathbf{H}}_{0}&\text{ for }p+1\leq i\leq p+q\,.\end{cases}

Based on the choice of the basis in (
2.10
) we define for

i=1,\ldots,p+q

(2.12)

{\mathbf{V}_{\!i}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}-\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i})\,,\qquad{\mathbf{W}_{\!i}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\mathbf{X}}_{i}+\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i})\,.

The elements
{\mathbf{V}_{\!1}},\ldots,{\mathbf{V}_{\!p+q}},{\mathbf{H}}_{0}
form an orthonormal basis for
the positive definite bilinear form
(X,Y)\mapsto-B(X,\theta Y)
on

{\mathfrak{p}}
. The elements

(2.13)

{\mathbf{W}_{\!1}},\ldots,{\mathbf{W}_{\!p+q}},{\mathbf{U}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{U}}_{\dim({\mathfrak{m}})}

form an orthonormal basis of
{\mathfrak{k}}
for the positive definite bilinear
form
-B
.

The fact that
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}
corresponds to the splitting in
eigenspaces of the Cartan involution
\theta
for the eigenvalues
1
and

-1
, implies that
[{\mathbf{Z}},{\mathbf{X}}]\in{\mathfrak{p}}
for
{\mathbf{Z}}\in{\mathfrak{k}}
and

{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{p}}
. In other words
{\mathbf{Z}}\mapsto\ad({\mathbf{Z}})
determines a Lie
algebra representation of
{\mathfrak{k}}
on
{\mathfrak{p}}
. The form
B
satisfies

B\bigl(\ad({\mathbf{Z}}){\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}\bigr)=-B\bigl({\mathbf{X}},\ad({\mathbf{Z}}){\mathbf{Y}}\bigr)
, and
hence

(2.14)

-B\bigl({\mathbf{X}},\ad({\mathbf{Z}}){\mathbf{Y}}\bigr)-B\bigl(\ad({\mathbf{Z}}){\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}\bigr)=0\qquad\text{ for }{\mathbf{Z}}\in{\mathfrak{k}},\;{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}\in{\mathfrak{p}}\,.

See, eg,
[
20
, Chap II, §6, (1)]
. This shows that the Lie
algebra representation
\ad
of
{\mathfrak{k}}
on
{\mathfrak{p}}
preserves the
Hilbert space structure of
{\mathfrak{p}}
corresponding to
-B
, and that the
corresponding representation
\Ad
of
K
on
{\mathfrak{p}}
is orthogonal for

-B
.

2.3.
Coordinates on
S

We consider
two systems of coordinates on
S
and give their relation.

Polar coordinates.
The Cartan
decomposition gives a diffeomorphism between
S\smallsetminus\{\text{{{e}}}\}
and

(K/M)\times(1,\infty)
.

Coordinates on
N
.
Let us take coordinates

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})
on
N
such that the derivation

(\partial_{x_{i}})_{e}
at the unit element
e
is equal to the
derivation
\sqrt{2}\,R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})_{e}
. By
\mathrm{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})
we
denote the corresponding element of
N
. We put

(2.15)

x^{(1)}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})\,,\qquad x^{(2)}=(x_{p+1},\ldots,x_{p+q})\,,

and write
\mathrm{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
. The coordinates can
be chosen such that

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{a}(t)\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(t)^{-1}=\mathrm{n}(tx^{(1)},t^{2}x^{(2)})\,,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\,\mathrm{n}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})=\mathrm{n}\bigl(x^{(1)}+u^{(1)},x^{(2)}+u^{(2)}+b(x^{(1)},u^{(1)})\bigr)\,,

where
b
is a bilinear map
\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q}
.
This can be arranged by letting

(2.18)

\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})=\exp\Bigl(\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p+q}x_{i}\,{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\Bigr)\,.

This can also be written as
\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})=\exp({\mathbf{X}})\exp({\mathbf{Y}})

with
{\mathbf{X}}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p}x_{i}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}
and

{\mathbf{Y}}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}x_{j}{\mathbf{X}}_{j}
. (Note that
\exp({\mathbf{X}})
and

\exp({\mathbf{Y}})
commute.)
A consequence is

(2.19)

\mathrm{n}(-x^{(1)},-x^{(2)})=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})^{-1}\,.

Horospherical coordinates.
We take coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q},y)
on
S
such
that

(2.20)

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q},y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\;\leftrightarrow\;\mathrm{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\in S\,.

We call these coordinates
normalized horospherical coordinates
.

We call
\bigl((x^{\prime})^{(1)},(x^{\prime})^{(2)}\bigr)

horospherical coordinates
if
(x^{\prime})^{(1)}
,
respectively
(x^{\prime})^{(2)}
, depends on
x^{(1)}
, respectively on

x^{(2)}
, by a bijective linear transformation. The properties
(
2.16
) and (
2.17
) stay valid for general horospherical
coordinates.

The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
can be viewed as the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{\!e}G
at
the unit element
e
. Under the natural projection
G\rightarrow G/K=S
the subspace
{\mathfrak{k}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
is mapped to
zero, and
{\mathfrak{p}}
is mapped isomorphically onto the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
. We use that
{\mathbf{V}_{\!i}}=\sqrt{2}\,{\mathbf{X}}_{i}-{\mathbf{W}_{\!i}}
to see that for

f\in C^{\infty}(G/K)
, on which
R({\mathfrak{k}})
acts trivially,

R({\mathbf{V}_{\!i}})f=\sqrt{2}\,R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})f-R({\mathbf{W}_{\!i}})f=\sqrt{2}\,R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})f\,.

We have arranged the definitions so that the derivation
R({\mathbf{V}_{\!i}})_{\text{{{e}}}}

at
e
corresponds to
\partial_{x_{i}}

at the point
(0,\cdots,0,1)
in normalized horospherical coordinates.
This explains the use of
\sqrt{2}
in the introduction of the
coordinates on
N
. The derivation
R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})_{\text{{{e}}}}
corresponds to

\partial_{y}
at the point
(0,\cdots,0,1)
.

Relation between coordinatizations.

Proposition 2.1
.

Projection functions for Iwasawa
decomposition

\mathrm{(i)}

There are unique analytic maps

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow N\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{t}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow(0,\infty)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow K\,,

such that
g=\mathbf{n}(g)\,\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}(g)\bigr)\mathbf{k}(g)
for all
g\in G
.
There are also unique analytic maps for the opposite Iwasawa
decomposition

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{o}}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow N\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow(0,\infty)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow K\,,

such that
g=\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(g)\,\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g)\bigr)\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{o}}(g)
for all
g\in G
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

The following explicit expression does not depend on the choice of

\mathrm{w}
:

(2.23)

\mathbf{t}\bigl(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)=\frac{y}{\sqrt{\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}}}\,.

By
\|\cdot\|
we denote the norm
\|x\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}
on

\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
c
is a positive constant.

Proof.

The existence of the functions in part (

i

) is a
direct consequence of the Iwasawa decomposition. We note that

(2.24)

\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(g)=\mathbf{k}(g^{-1})^{-1}\,,

and analogously for
\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}
and
\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{o}}
.

Part (

ii

) depends on
[
21
, Theorem 1.14]

\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(\mathrm{n}^{-}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\bigr)=\sqrt{(1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}}\,.

Helgason describes the element
\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
as

\exp({\mathbf{X}})\exp({\mathbf{Y}})
with
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}
,
{\mathbf{Y}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}
.
It turns out that
|{\mathbf{X}}|=2\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}
and
|{\mathbf{Y}}|=2\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}
.
So our
c
differs from the constant given by Helgason. Conjugation of

\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
by elements of
M
and taking the inverse does
not change the norms
\|x^{(j)}\|
.

To get the relation between
\mathrm{n}^{-}(x)
and
\mathrm{n}(x)
we write these
elements as
\exp({\mathbf{X}}_{-})
and
\exp({\mathbf{X}})
with
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{n}}
and

{\mathbf{X}}_{-}=\theta{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{n}}^{-}
. We have chosen
\mathrm{w}\in K
such that

\mathrm{w}\mathrm{a}(t)\mathrm{w}^{-1}=\mathrm{a}(1/t)
. Conjugation by
\mathrm{w}
gives an
isomorphism
N\cong N^{-}
as well. The element

\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{w}^{-1}
has the form

\mathrm{n}^{-}(\tilde{x}^{(1)},\tilde{x}^{(2)})
with orthogonal
transformations
x^{(j)}\mapsto\tilde{x}^{(j)}
. So for formulas
involving only these norms on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we can work with

\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{w}^{-1}
as well as with
\mathrm{n}^{-}(x)
.

In the following computation we use that
\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g)
is invariant under

g\mapsto kg
with
k\in K
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}\bigl(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})^{-1}\mathrm{w}^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})^{-1}\mathrm{w}^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(\mathrm{n}^{-}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}=\Bigl((1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-1/2}\,;

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}\bigl(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t}\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y)^{-1}\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(y^{-1}x^{(1)},y^{-2}x^{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=y^{-1}\,\Bigl(\bigl(1+y^{-2}\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4cy^{-4}\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=y\,\Bigl(\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-1/2}\,.

This is relation (
2.23
).
∎

Relation in
\partial S
.
We have

N\,\text{{{o}}}\subset K/M=\partial S
with

(2.25)

n\mathrm{w}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=n\,\text{{{o}}}=\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(n\mathrm{w})\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\quad\text{ for }n\in N\,.

Hence the embedding
N\,\text{{{o}}}\rightarrow\partial S
is given by

(2.26)

\mathrm{n}(x)\,\text{{{o}}}\mapsto\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(x)w\bigr)\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\,.

2.4.
Riemannian structure of
S

We
recall that the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
can be viewed as the tangent space

\mathrm{T}_{\!e}G
at the unit element
e
.
The action of
K
by conjugation preserves this tangent space, and is
orthogonal on
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
for the scalar product induced by

({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}})=-B({\mathbf{X}},\theta Y)
.

The subspace
{\mathfrak{p}}
can be identified with the tangent space

\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
. It inherits the scalar product, and
K
acts
orthogonally on
\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
. The derivations

\sqrt{2}\,R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})_{\text{{{e}}}}
,
1\leq i\leq p+q
, and
R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})_{\text{{{e}}}}

form an orthonormal basis of
\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
. In normalized horospherical
coordinates these derivations correspond to
(\partial_{x_{i}})_{\text{{{e}}}}

and
(\partial_{y})_{\text{{{e}}}}
.

We transport the scalar product on
\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
to
\mathrm{T}_{\!x}S
for all

x\in S
by the translation
L(g)
with
g\in G
such that

g\text{{{e}}}=x
. Since the scalar product on
\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
is preserved by
the action of
K
, any choice of
g
in
gK
determines the same scalar
product. The image of an orthonormal basis is an orthonormal basis
which does depend on the choice of
g
.

Let
g=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)
(normalized horospherical
coordinates). Since

g\,\mathrm{a}(e^{\xi})=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(ye^{\xi})\,,

the tangent vector
(\partial_{y})_{\text{{{e}}}}
is transported to

y(\partial_{y})_{g\,\text{{{e}}}}
.

Let
\varepsilon_{j}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
with
1
at position
j
be the

j
-th unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
. If
j\geq p+1
, then for

\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, with use of (
2.16
),

\displaystyle g\,\mathrm{n}(\eta\varepsilon_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})\,\mathrm{a}(y)\,\mathrm{n}(\eta\varepsilon_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})\,\mathrm{n}(y^{2}\eta\varepsilon_{j})\,\mathrm{a}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+y^{2}\eta,x_{j+1},\cdots,x_{p+q})\,\mathrm{a}(y)\,,

and
\sqrt{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})_{\text{{{e}}}}\leftrightarrow\partial_{x_{j}}
is transported
to
y^{2}(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}
.

If
1\leq j\leq p
, then we have to take into account the bilinear map

b
in (
2.17
):

g\,\mathrm{n}(\eta\varepsilon_{j})=\mathrm{n}\bigl(x_{1}\cdots,x_{j}+y\eta,\cdots x_{p},x^{(2)}+y\xi\,b(x^{(1)},\varepsilon_{j})\bigr)\mathrm{a}(y)\,.

So
\sqrt{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})_{\text{{{e}}}}\leftrightarrow\partial_{x_{j}}
is transported
to

y\,(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}+\sum_{i=p+1}^{q}y\,b\bigl(x^{(1)},\varepsilon_{j})_{i}\,(\partial_{x_{i}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\,,

with linear forms
c_{j,i}:x^{(1)}\mapsto b\bigl(x^{(1)},\varepsilon_{j})_{i}
on

\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
1\leq j\leq p
,
p+1\leq i\leq p+q
.

Thus, we have obtained an orthogonal basis of
\mathrm{T}_{\!g\text{{{e}}}}S
at each
point
g\,\text{{{e}}}
of
S
:

(2.27)

\displaystyle m_{j}

\displaystyle=y\,(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}+\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}y\,c_{j,i}(x^{(1)})\,(\partial_{x_{i}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq p\,,

\displaystyle m_{j}

\displaystyle=y^{2}\,(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}

\displaystyle p+1\leq j\leq p+q\,,

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{d}}=m_{p+q+1}

\displaystyle=y\,(\partial_{y})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\,,

with the linear function
c_{j,i}
defined above. This basis depends on
the choice of
g\in gK
, but the corresponding scalar product is
independent of this choice, since
K
acts orthogonally on

\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
.

Inverting the relations (
2.27
) we arrive at

(2.28)

\displaystyle(\partial_{y})_{g\text{{{e}}}}

\displaystyle=y^{-1}m_{d}\,,

\displaystyle(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}

\displaystyle=y^{-2}m_{j}

\displaystyle\text{ for }p+1\leq j\leq p+q\,,

\displaystyle(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}

\displaystyle=y^{-1}m_{j}-\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}y^{-2}c_{j,i}(x^{(1)})\,m_{i}

\displaystyle\text{ for }1\leq j\leq p\,.

So the scalar product
\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(\cdot,\cdot)
on
\mathrm{T}_{\!g\text{{{e}}}}S

satisfies

(2.29)

\displaystyle\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}\bigl((\partial_{y})_{g\text{{{e}}}},(\partial_{y})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=y^{-2}\,,

\displaystyle\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}\bigl((\partial_{y})_{g\text{{{e}}}},(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }1\leq j\leq p+q\,,

\displaystyle\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}\bigl((\partial_{x_{i}})_{g\text{{{e}}}},(\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=y^{-4}\,\delta_{i,j}

\displaystyle\text{ for }p+1\leq i,j\leq p+q\,.

For
1\leq j\leq p
and
p+1\leq l\leq p+q

(2.30)

\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}\bigl((\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}},(\partial_{x_{l}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\bigr)=-y^{-4}c_{j,l}(x^{(1)})\,.

If both
j
and
l
are in
[1,p]
, then

(2.31)

\displaystyle\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}\bigl((\partial_{x_{j}})_{g\text{{{e}}}},(\partial_{x_{l}})_{g\text{{{e}}}}\bigr)=y^{-2}\,\delta_{j,l}+y^{-4}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}c_{j,i}(x^{(1)})c_{l,i}(x^{(1)})\,.

These formulas hold at all points
g\,\text{{{e}}}
in
S
. We write the matrix
describing the scalar product in
(p,q,1)
block
form:

(2.32)

\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}=\begin{pmatrix}y^{-2}I_{p}+y^{-4}C(x^{(1)})\,C(x^{(1)})^{t}&-y^{-4}C(x^{(1)})&0\\
-y^{-4}C(x^{(1)})^{t}&y^{-4}I_{q}&0\\
0&0&y^{-2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

with
I_{n}
the unit matrix of size
n\times n
, and

C(x^{(1)})
the
p\times q
matrix with

c_{j,l}(x^{(1)})
at the position
(j,l-p)
.

If we use another coordinate system on an open set in
S
, then we get
other matrices describing the Riemannian structure. We will still
denote those matrices by
g^{(R)}
, although they need not have
exactly the structure in (
2.32
). When dealing with discrete
subgroups of
G
it will be useful to take coordinates

x^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots x_{p+q}^{\prime})
on
N
that depend linearly on

\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)}\bigr)
, and to keep the coordinate
y
. Then
the Riemannian structure is given by a
(p+q)\times 1
block matrix of
the form

(2.33)

\begin{pmatrix}Q(x^{\prime},y)&0\\
0&y^{-2}\end{pmatrix}

with a positive definite matrix

Q(x^{\prime},y)=Q_{1}(x^{\prime})y^{-2}+Q_{2}(x^{\prime})y^{-4}
where

Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
depend on
x^{\prime}
by quadratic polynomials.

Distance function.
The Riemannian structure describes the length
of a path
t\mapsto p(t)
,
t_{1}\leq t\leq t_{2}
, as

(2.34)

\int_{t=t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\sqrt{\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(p^{\prime}(t))}\,dt\,,

where
\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(p^{\prime}(t))
is a short-hand notation for

(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})_{p(t)}(p^{\prime}(t),p^{\prime}(t))
, i.e., the inner product on

\mathrm{T}_{\!p(t)}S
that is given by the Riemannian metric at
p(t)
,
evaluated on the tangent vector
p^{\prime}(t)
. The distance

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{1},x_{2}\bigr)

is the minimum of these lengths for all paths with
p(t_{1})=x_{1}
and

p(t_{2})=x_{2}
. One can check that for
y_{1},y_{2}>0

(2.35)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y_{1})\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{a}(y_{2})\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=\bigl|\log(y_{2}/y_{1})\bigr|\,.

This relation stays valid for the description of the Riemannian
structure with the matrix in (
2.33
).

Invariant
{\mathrm{d}}
-form.
The matrix elements
g_{i,j}
are
functions on
S
, and the
{\mathrm{d}}
-form

(2.36)

{\zeta}_{S}=(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{1/2}\,dx_{1}\wedge dx_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{d}

satisfies
{\zeta}_{S}\circ h={\zeta}_{S}
for all
h\in G
. Integration of
functions on
S
against
{\zeta}_{S}
gives a
G
-invariant measure on
S
.
In normalized horospherical coordinates

\det(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{1/2}=y^{-2\rho-1}
, with
g^{(R)}
as in
(
2.32
).

2.5.
Laplace operator

In general, the
Laplace–Beltrami operator is described as in
[
20
, Chap X, §2.1,
equation (4)]
:

(2.37)

\Delta_{+}=(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1/2}\,\sum_{k}\partial_{x_{k}}\sum_{i}(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1}_{i,k}\sqrt{\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,,

with respect to any choice of coordinates
x_{1},\ldots x_{\mathrm{d}}
. The matrix

g^{(R)}
describes the inner products of the basis vectors

\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{\mathrm{d}}}
. The expression in
(
2.32
) is for normalized horospherical coordinates.

By
loc. cit.
equations (2) and
(3), we can obtain
\Delta_{+}
as a
product of first order differential operators:

(2.38)

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}f

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}\,\grad\,f\,,

\displaystyle\grad

\displaystyle f=\sum_{i,j}(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1}_{i,j}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{j}}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\sum_{i}a_{i}\,\partial_{x_{i}}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1/2}\sum_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\bigl((\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{1/2}\,a_{i}\bigr)\,.

We prefer to use

(2.39)

\Delta=-\Delta_{+}\,.

Since the Riemannian metric is invariant under the action of
G
on
S
,
the Laplace operator commutes with the action of
G
on
S
:

(2.40)

\Delta L(g)f=L(g)\Delta f\,.

2.5.1.
Laplace operator in horospherical
coordinates

To apply the definition in
(
2.37
) we need the inverse of the matrix
g^{(R)}
. In normalized
horospherical coordinates we can check that

(2.41)

(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}y^{2}I_{p}&y^{2}C(x^{(1)})&0\\
y^{2}C(x^{(1)})^{t}&y^{4}I_{q}+y^{2}C(x^{(1)})^{t}C(x^{(1)})&0\\
0&0&y^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

with the matrix
C(x^{(1)})
in (
2.32
). Left multiplication by

\begin{pmatrix}I_{p}&C(x^{(1)})&0\\
0&I_{q}&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

brings
g^{(R)}
into a form that shows that

(2.42)

\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}=y^{-4\rho-2}\,.

The form of this matrix implies that the differentiation with respect to
the last variable
y
does not interact with the other coordinates. We
conclude that

(2.43)

\Delta=-y^{2}\,\partial_{y}^{2}+(2\rho-1)y\,\partial y-y^{2}L_{1}-y^{4}L_{2}\,,

with second order differential operators in the variables

x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q}
. Actually, we have

L_{2}=-\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}
. The operator
L_{1}
is
more complicated.

2.5.2.
Ellipticity of the Laplace operator

The matrix elements of
g^{(R)}
are analytic functions on
S
provided
we work with coordinates that are analytic. Furthermore, the matrix

g^{(R)}
and its inverse are positive definite. The principal symbol
of
\Delta_{+}
is

(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1/2}\,\sum_{i,k}(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{-1}_{i,k}\sqrt{\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}}\,X_{i}X_{k}\,.

It is obtained by taking the highest order terms and replacing partial
derivatives by variables. The resulting polynomial is the principal
symbol of the differential operator. In this way we see that the
principal symbol of
\Delta_{+}
is positive definite, which implies that

\Delta_{+}
, and hence
\Delta
as well, are elliptic differential operators.

All eigenfunctions of
\Delta
are automatically smooth by elliptic
regularity, as discussed for instance by Folland
[
14
, Theorem
(6.33)]
or Lang
[
28
, Appendix 4]
. By elliptic
regularity, any function or distribution that is an eigenvector of

\Delta
is a
C^{\infty}
-function. Analytic elliptic regularity implies
that if the coefficients of the elliptic operator are analytic, then
the eigenvectors are analytic functions. Lang refers to Bers and
Schechter
[
1
]
for analytic elliptic regularity.

2.6.
The Casimir element

The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
generates the universal enveloping algebra

U({\mathfrak{g}})
. We can view the elements of
U({\mathfrak{g}})

as non-commutative polynomials in elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The
representations
R
and
L
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
C^{\infty}(G)
by right and
left differentiation extend as representations of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
where the
differential operators
R(u)
,
u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
, commute with
L(g)
,

g\in G
, and the differential operators
L(u)
commute with
R(g)
. If
we apply
R(u)
for
u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
to elements of
C^{\infty}(G/K)
,
i.e., of
C^{\infty}(S)
, we end up with functions that are in general
not right
K
-invariant.

The Casimir element

{\mathsf{Cas}}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
is in the center of the universal
enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
. It can be described starting from any
basis
\bigl\{{\mathbf{X}}_{j}\bigr\}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and its dual basis

\bigl\{{\mathbf{X}}_{j}^{\prime}\bigr\}
with respect to the normalized Killing form as

(2.44)

{\mathsf{Cas}}=\sum_{j}{\mathbf{X}}_{j}{\mathbf{X}}_{j}^{\prime}\,.

In the representation
R
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
C^{\infty}(G)
by right
differentiation, we call
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
the
Casimir operator
. It is
the unique second order differential operator that commutes with all
operators
R({\mathbf{X}})
for
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{k}}
, and has vanishing constant term.
Hence
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
preserves the space of right
K
-invariant functions,
which we can identify with
C^{\infty}(S)
. Since right differentiation
commutes with left translation, we have a differential operator

R({\mathsf{Cas}})
in
C^{\infty}(S)
commuting with
L(g)
.

Proposition 2.2
.

The differential operators
R({\mathsf{Cas}})

and
\Delta_{+}
in
C^{\infty}(S)
coincide.

This is Exercise C5 in
[
20
]
.

With the bases of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) we find the
Casimir element

(2.45)

{\mathsf{Cas}}={\mathbf{H}}_{0}^{2}-\sum_{j}{\mathbf{U}}_{j}^{2}-\sum_{i}{\mathbf{X}}_{i}(\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i})-\sum_{i}(\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i}){\mathbf{X}}_{i}\,,

with
1\leq j\leq\dim{\mathfrak{m}}
, and
1\leq i\leq p+q
. For application
on right
K
-invariant functions we can use that
R({\mathbf{U}}_{j})
acts as
zero. Using that
{\mathbf{X}}_{i}+\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{k}}
acts as zero as well,
we arrive at the action

(2.46)

R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{p+q}2R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})^{2}-2\rho\,R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})\,.

3.
Automorphic forms

After describing in §
3.1
a class of discrete
subgroups of
G
, we define automorphic forms in §
3.2
.
The last subsection gives some proofs.

3.1.
Discrete subgroup

We fix a
discrete subgroup
\Gamma\subset G
satisfying the following
conditions:

\mathrm{(i)}

\Gamma
is cofinite, but not cocompact.

Cofinite means that the quotient
\Gamma\backslash S
has finite measure
for the measure induced by
d\mu
. Since the group is not cocompact, it
has cusps, discussed below.

\mathrm{(ii)}

\Gamma
is torsion-free. This means that the sole
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
finite order is the unit element
e
of
\Gamma
.

By requiring
\Gamma
to be torsion-free, we avoid some technical
difficulties.

Cusps.
Each point
g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\in\partial S
is fixed by the group

gNg^{-1}
. The element
g\in G
is determined by the point

g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
up to right multiplication by
NAM
. A cusp

{\mathsf{c}}=g_{\mathsf{c}}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
of
\Gamma
is a point of

\partial S
for which
N_{\mathsf{c}}:=g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
intersects
\Gamma
in
a lattice.
(A discrete subgroup
\Lambda\subset N
is called a
(uniform)

lattice
if the quotient
\Lambda\backslash N
is
compact.)

All maximal unipotent subgroups in
G
are conjugate to each other. So
there exist elements
g_{\mathsf{c}}\in G
such that

N_{\mathsf{c}}=g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
. The requirement that the
intersection
\Gamma\cap N_{\mathsf{c}}
is a lattice in
N_{\mathsf{c}}
is that the
quotient
\left(\Gamma\cap N_{\mathsf{c}}\right)\backslash N_{\mathsf{c}}
is compact. The
set
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
of all cusps of
\Gamma
is invariant under the
action of
\Gamma
on
\partial S
. It consists of finitely many

\Gamma
-orbits.

By
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
we denote the subgroup
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
of

\Gamma
stabilizing
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The condition that
\Gamma
is torsion-free
implies that
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}=\Gamma\cap N_{\mathsf{c}}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

We can (and do) normalize the choice of the elements
g_{\mathsf{c}}
such that
the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}}=g_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}g_{\mathsf{c}}
in
N
depends only on
the
\Gamma
-orbit of
{\mathsf{c}}
in
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.

Proofs in §
3.4
.

3.2.
Automorphic forms and cusp forms

We now define spaces of various types of
\Gamma
-invariant functions on

S
: invariant eigenfunctions, automorphic forms, and cusp forms.

Definition 3.3
.

We define an
invariant eigenfunction
as a function

f\in C^{\infty}(S)
that satisfies

\mathrm{(a)}

f(\gamma x)=f(x)
for each
x\in S
and each
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

\mathrm{(b)}

\Delta f=\lambda f
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

Since the Laplace operator is elliptic with analytic coefficients, all
its eigenfunctions are analytic. See §
2.5.2
.

We will parametrize the eigenvalue as
\lambda=\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
, for a reason
that we will discuss after Proposition
5.1
. We call

\nu
the
spectral parameter
, and
denote by
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)^{\Gamma}={\mathcal{E}}_{-\nu}(S)^{\Gamma}
the space of
invariant eigenfunctions with spectral parameter
\nu
.

In general, the spaces
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)^{\Gamma}
have infinite dimension. We
define interesting subspaces by imposing growth conditions at the
cusps.

Definition 3.4
.

We define
polynomial growth

and
quick decay
at a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}

by

(3.1)

\displaystyle f\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{a}(t)x\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{O}}(t^{b}\bigr)\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow\infty\text{ uniformly for $x$ in compact
sets}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\text{ for some }b\in\mathbb{R}&\text{\emph{polynomial
growth},}\\
\text{ for each }b\in\mathbb{R}&\text{\emph{quick decay}.}\end{cases}

For polynomial growth large values of
b
are relevant, and for quick
decay, negative values of
b
with
|b|
large. See
§
3.3
for a further discussion of growth conditions.

Definition 3.5
.

An invariant eigenfunction
f\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)^{\Gamma}
is an

automorphic form
if it has polynomial
growth at all cusps. It suffices to impose this growth condition for a
representative of each
\Gamma
-orbit of cusps.

By
{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma;\nu)
we denote the space of all
automorphic forms for
\Gamma
with spectral parameter
\nu
.

Identifying an automorphic form
f
on
S
with the function

g\mapsto f(g\,\text{{{e}}})
on
G
we have an automorphic form of trivial

K
-type, as defined by Harish-Chandra. The spaces
{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma;\nu)
have
finite dimension. See
[
18
, p 7, and Theorem 1, p 8]
.

If
f\in{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma;\nu)
, then the integral

(3.2)

f_{\mathsf{c}}(x)=\int_{n\in(\Gamma\cap N_{\mathsf{c}})\backslash N_{\mathsf{c}}}f(n\,x)\,dn

is well-defined for each cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
, since

(\Gamma\cap N_{\mathsf{c}})\backslash N_{\mathsf{c}}
is compact.

Definition 3.6
.

The space of
cusp
forms

{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
is the
linear subspace of
f\in{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma;\nu)
for which
f_{\mathsf{c}}=0
for all
cusps
{\mathsf{c}}
.

It suffices to require that
f_{\mathsf{c}}=0
for
{\mathsf{c}}
in a set of
representatives of the
\Gamma
-orbits of cusps.

Proposition 3.7

(Harish-Chandra)
.

Cusp forms have quick decay at each cusp.

Proof.

Let
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. Harish-Chandra
[
18
, Chap I, § 3,
4]
shows that if a
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
-invariant eigenfunction of
\Delta

with polynomial growth at the cusps has vanishing Fourier term of order
zero (i.e.,
f_{\mathsf{c}}=0
), then it has quick decay.
∎

We can characterize cusp forms by the condition of quick decay at the
cusps. If the unipotent subgroup
N
of
G
is abelian, then cusp
forms have exponential decay. In more general situations we have to be
content with quick decay.

Proposition 3.8
.

The set of
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
such that

{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\neq\{0\}
is a discrete set in

\bigl\{\nu\in\mathbb{C}\;:\;\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0\bigr\}
.

The discreteness of the spectrum follows from the compactness of
appropriate integral operators. See, for instance,
[
18
, Theorems 2
(p 9), 3 (p 15), and the remarks on p 16]
. For the inequality

\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0
it is essential that we work with cusp forms on

S
, corresponding to right
K
-invariant functions on
G
. See the
completion of the proof in §
3.4
.

The proposition implies that
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
might be non-zero for
infinitely many values
\nu\in i\mathbb{R}
, and for at most finitely many
real values in
[-\rho,\rho]
. For cusp forms of non-trivial
K
-type,
the eigenvalues
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
of the Casimir operator on cusp forms
can be negative.

3.3.
Growth conditions on the group

The growth conditions at a cusp can be formulated for all functions on
the group, and can be applied to functions on
S
by considering them
as functions on the group that are right
K
-invariant. On the group,
polynomial growth and quick decay at the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
can be formulated
as

(3.3)

\displaystyle f\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{a}(t)g\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{O}}(t^{b}\bigr)\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow\infty\text{ uniformly for $g$ in compact
sets in $G$}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\text{ for some }b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}&\text{\emph{polynomial
growth},}\\
\text{ for each }b\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}&\text{\emph{quick decay}.}\end{cases}

By replacing the single element
g_{\mathsf{c}}
in (
3.3
) by an element

k
running over
K
we obtain stronger conditions: polynomial growth
on
G
, and quick decay on
G
, defined by

(3.4)

\displaystyle f(k\mathrm{a}(t)g)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{O}}(t^{b})\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow\infty

\displaystyle\text{for each $k\in K$, uniformly for $g$ in compact sets in
$G$}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\text{for some }b\in\mathbb{R}&\text{\emph{polynomial
growth}}\,,\\
\text{for each }b\in\mathbb{R}&\text{\emph{quick decay}}\,.\end{cases}

Applied to functions on
S
, we call these conditions

polynomial growth

(respectively
quick decay
)

at the boundary
.

Proposition 3.9
.

The conditions of polynomial growth and
quick decay on
G
are preserved under left translation by elements
of
G
.

Proof.

Left translation by
h^{-1}
turns the quantity to be considered
into

\bigl(L(h^{-1})f\bigr)(k\mathrm{a}(t)g)=f(hk\mathrm{a}(t)g)\,.

By the Iwasawa decomposition we can write

hk=k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1})n_{1}
with
k_{1}\in K
,
t_{1}>0
, and

n_{1}=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
(in horospherical coordinates). The quantity
to estimate is

f\bigl(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1})n_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)g\bigr)=f\bigl(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1}t)\mathrm{n}(t^{-1}x^{(1)},t^{-2}x^{(2)})g\bigr)\,.

As
t\uparrow\infty
the element
\mathrm{n}(t^{-1}x^{(1)},t^{-2}x^{(2)})g

stays in a compact subset of
G
. The influence of
t_{1}
is hidden in
the implicit constant in
{\mathrm{O}}(t)
.∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Let the
K
-finite
f\in C^{\infty}(G)_{K}
be
an eigenfunction of the differential operator
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
determined by
the Casimir element in §
2.6
. If
f
satisfies one of the
growth conditions mentioned above, then so does
R(u)f
for all

u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
.

Indication of the proof.

The proposition is a direct
consequence of the convolution representation of Harish-Chandra in
Theorem 1 on p 18 of
[
17
]
. It gives

f(g)=\int_{G}f(gh^{-1})\,\alpha(h)\,dh=\int_{G}f(h)\,\alpha(h^{-1}g)\,dh\,,

for a suitable compactly supported function
\alpha\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
. In
the second form of the convolution, the differentiation concerns only
the factor
\alpha(h^{-1}g)
. This allows us to show that the growth
property of
f
is valid for
R({\mathbf{X}})f
for any
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. These
derivatives are eigenfunctions of
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
with the same eigenvalue.
So we can repeat the argument to get preservation of the growth
property under all elements of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Suppose that
f\in C^{\infty}(G)_{K}
is an eigenfunction of
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
and
has quick decay at the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
. Then all derivatives of
f
of any
order in the horospherical coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q},y
have
quick decay at
{\mathsf{c}}
.

Proof.

The derivatives

\bigl(R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})f\bigr)\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)
are
given by a linear combination of the
\partial_{x_{i}}
with polynomial
coefficients in the
x_{j}
and
y
.
∎

Definition
3.6
characterizes the space of cusp forms in

{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma;\nu)
by the vanishing of the integrals
f_{\mathsf{c}}
in (
3.2
)
for all
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. The following lemma gives another
characterization of cusps forms. We recall that non-zero cusp forms
occur only for
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0
, which implies that we have

|\re\nu|\leq\rho
.

Lemma 3.12
.

We can characterize cusp forms within
the space of bounded invariant eigenfunctions with eigenvalue

\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
by the condition

(3.5)

f(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{a}(y)\,x)={\mathrm{O}}\bigl(y^{b}\bigr)\qquad\text{as }y\uparrow\infty\text{ for all cusps }{\mathsf{c}}

for some
b<\rho-|\re\nu|
.

Proof.

Since
f
is bounded, it is an automorphic form. The
functions
f_{\mathsf{c}}
inherit the boundedness from the function
f
.
Moreover,
f_{\mathsf{c}}(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})
does not depend on
n\in N
.
(See the integral in (
3.2
) and use that
f
is left-invariant
under
\Gamma\cap g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
.)

For any
f\in{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma;\nu)
the functions
f_{\mathsf{c}}
are linear combinations
of
g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\mapsto t^{\rho\pm\nu}
, or of
t^{\rho}

and
t^{\rho}\log t
if
\nu=0
. Condition (
3.5
) implies that all

f_{\mathsf{c}}
have to vanish, and
f
is a cusp form.
∎

3.4.
Proofs

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

We have to show that if

\Gamma
is torsion-free, then

\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}=\bigl\{\gamma\in\Gamma\;:\;\gamma\,{\mathsf{c}}={\mathsf{c}}\}
is equal to

\Gamma\cap g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{{\mathsf{c}}}^{-1}
.

Since
NMA
is the normalizer of
N
in
G
, any element of
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}

is of the form
g_{\mathsf{c}}nmag_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
, with
n\in N
,
a\in A
and

m\in M
. Since

\mathrm{a}(t)^{-1}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(t)=\mathrm{n}(t^{-1}x^{(1)},t^{-2}x^{(2)})\,,

the discreteness of
N_{\mathsf{c}}
implies that
t=1
, and
\gamma=nm
. Since

M
is compact, the group
\Gamma\cap g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
has finite index
in
\Gamma\cap g_{\mathsf{c}}NMg_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
. So there exists
k\geq 1
such that

g_{\mathsf{c}}(nm)^{k}g_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}\in g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
, and then
m^{k}\in N

since
M
normalizes
N
. However,
M\cap N=\{e\}
, and
k=1
since

\Gamma
is torsion-free. So
\gamma\in\Gamma\cap g_{\mathsf{c}}Ng_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

For each
\Gamma
-orbit in

{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
we choose a representative
{\mathsf{d}}
and choose some
g_{\mathsf{d}}\in G

such that
N_{\mathsf{d}}=g_{\mathsf{d}}Ng_{\mathsf{d}}^{-1}
. If
{\mathsf{c}}=\gamma{\mathsf{d}}
with

\gamma\in\Gamma
, then

N_{\mathsf{c}}=\gamma g_{\mathsf{d}}Ng_{\mathsf{d}}^{-1}\gamma^{-1}=\gamma N_{\mathsf{d}}\gamma^{-1}
, and
also
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}=\gamma\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{d}}}\gamma^{-1}
. So we can take

g_{\mathsf{c}}=\gamma g_{\mathsf{d}}
. Then

(3.6)

\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}}=(\gamma g_{\mathsf{d}})^{-1}\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}\gamma g_{\mathsf{d}}=g_{\mathsf{d}}^{-1}\gamma^{-1}\gamma\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{d}}}\gamma^{-1}\gamma g_{\mathsf{d}}=g_{\mathsf{d}}^{-1}\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{d}}}g_{\mathsf{d}}=\Lambda_{\mathsf{d}}\,.

The choice of
\gamma
has the freedom
\gamma\mapsto\gamma\delta
with

\delta\in\Gamma_{\mathsf{d}}
, and has no effect on the relation (
3.6
).
∎

Completion of the proof of Proposition

3.8
.

After the statement of the proposition, we already
mentioned that
[
18
, Theorem 2, p 9]
implies the discreteness of
the set of
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
such that
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\neq\{0\}
.

For functions
h_{1},h_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash S)
we have

(3.7)

\int_{\Gamma\backslash S}(\Delta h_{1})\,\bar{h}_{2}\,{\zeta}_{S}=\int_{\Gamma\backslash S}\sum_{i,j}\bigl(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}^{-1}\bigr)_{j,i}\,(\partial_{x_{j}}h_{1})\,(\overline{\partial_{x_{i}}h_{2}})\,{\zeta}_{S}\,.

To obtain this, we work with a fundamental domain
{\mathfrak{F}}
of
\Gamma
in
S
,
for instance obtained by the Dirichlet method that we will also use in
§
4.4.3
. Obtaining (
3.7
) by partial integration
over
{\mathfrak{F}}
, we use that the boundary term is zero, since the
contribution of
\Gamma
-equivalent boundary components cancel each other.

We apply this with
h_{1}=h_{2}=\chi f
for a non-zero cusp form

f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
, with a cut-off function
\chi
that equals
1
on
most of
{\mathfrak{F}}
, and behaves near each cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
in the closure of
{\mathfrak{F}}

in
S\cup(\partial S)
like

\chi\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=\varphi_{\mathsf{c}}(t)
, where
\varphi_{\mathsf{c}}(t)

goes down from
1
to
0
in some interval
