[T,T+1]
. The quick decay
of cusp forms implies that
\chi f
approaches
f
and
\Delta(\chi f)

approaches
\Delta f
in
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash S)
as
T\uparrow\infty
. So

(3.8)

\int_{\mathfrak{F}}\bigl(\Delta(\chi f)\bigr)\,\overline{\chi f}\,{\zeta}_{S}

approaches
(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})\,\int_{S}|f|^{2}\,{\zeta}_{s}
. On the other hand, the
quantity in (
3.8
) is equal to

\int_{{\mathfrak{F}}}\sum_{j,i}\bigl(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}^{-1}\bigr)_{i,j}(\partial_{x_{j}}(\chi f))\,(\overline{\partial_{x_{i}}(\chi f)}){\zeta}_{S}\,,

and is non-negative since
\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}^{-1}
is positive definite.
∎

With more work we can show that
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}=0
can happen only for
constant functions, which are not cusp forms.

4.
Cohomology

In this paper we relate spaces of cusp forms to parabolic cohomology
spaces
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
for
\Gamma
-modules
V
that are

\mathbb{C}
-linear spaces. That implies that
H^{j}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
is a vector
space over
\mathbb{C}
for all
j
. We prefer to speak of
‘cohomology spaces’ instead of the more
usual name ‘cohomology groups’.

We use methods that worked well in the case of
{\mathrm{d}}=\dim S=2
, and we
follow as far as possible the approach in
[
4
, Sections 6 and
11]
and
[
5
, Chap. 9]
.

There are many ways to describe parabolic cohomology spaces and the more
usual cohomology spaces
H^{j}(\Gamma;V)
. The description that we use here
has a geometrical flavor. It is based on tessellations of the symmetric
space
S
.

The first three subsections give a discussion of the concepts and
results that we will need in this paper, skipping some proofs. The
subsequent subsections give more details and provide some of the proofs
skipped earlier.

4.1.
Horoballs and horospheres

We start with some geometrical definitions.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
Y>0
. The

horosphere

H_{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(Y)
at
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
with

height

Y
, and the corresponding

horoball

B_{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(Y)
are the following
subsets of the symmetric space
S
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle H_{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(Y)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{n\mathrm{a}(Y)\,\text{{{e}}}\;:\;n\in N\bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle B_{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(Y)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\;:\;n\in N,\;y\geq Y\bigr\}\,.

The corresponding subsets at the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
of
\Gamma

are

(4.2)

H_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)=g_{\mathsf{c}}\,H_{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(Y)\,,\qquad B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)=g_{\mathsf{c}}\,B_{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(Y)\,.

In this definition it is important to use a normalization in
Lemma
3.2
of the elements
g_{\mathsf{c}}\in G
.

Definition 4.2
.

The
extended
symmetric space
of
\Gamma
is

(4.3)

S^{\!\ast}=S^{\!\ast}_{\!\Gamma}\;:=\;S\cup{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}\subset S\cup\partial S\,.

For each cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
the extended horoballs

B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y):=B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)\cup\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
with
Y>0
form a neighborhood basis of
{\mathsf{c}}

in
S^{\!\ast}
.

Figure 4.1.
Some horoballs for
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\backslash{\mathfrak{H}}
, with
Y=1.2
,
and
{\mathfrak{H}}
the complex upper half-plane.

Each horoball intersects
\partial S
only in one cusp. Each pair of
points of
\partial S\smallsetminus{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
(i.e., a pair of irrational
numbers)
can be joined by a continuous path in
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
.

In this context, we say that the height
Y
is

suitably large
if all horoballs
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
with
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
are
pairwise disjoint. The constructions in this section are based on the
choice of a suitably large number
Y
. We assume that

Y\geq 4
, and we keep it fixed from here on.

Definition 4.3
.

For the fixed suitably large number
Y
we define

S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\subset S
as the closure in
S
of

S\smallsetminus\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}}B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
.

The set
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
intersects each horoball
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
in the horosphere

H_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
. We obtain the decompositions

(4.4)

\displaystyle S=S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\cup\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}}B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)\,,

\displaystyle S^{\!\ast}=S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\cup\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}}B_{\mathsf{c}}^{\ast}(Y)\,.

The quotient
\Gamma\backslash S
is a manifold, since we suppose
\Gamma
to
be torsion-free. The quotient
\Gamma\backslash S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
is a manifold with
boundary. The quotient
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
is a compactification of

\Gamma\backslash S
, obtained by adding one point for each
\Gamma
-orbit of
cusps.

The projection

{\mathrm{pr}}\colon S^{\!\ast}\rightarrow\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
maps
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
to a
compact part of
\Gamma\backslash S
. The image of
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
is a
closed neighborhood of the image of
{\mathsf{c}}
in
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
. The
sets in the decomposition

(4.5)

\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}={\mathrm{pr}}\bigl(S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\bigr)\cup\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}}{\mathrm{pr}}\bigl(B_{\mathsf{c}}^{\ast}(Y)\bigr)

overlap only in their common boundary components.

4.2.
Tessellations

We will use a

\Gamma
-invariant tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}
of
S^{\!\ast}
. The idea of a
tessellation is explained in
[
4
, §6.2, §11]
. Here we give an
informal discussion of the concept of a tessellation of
S^{\!\ast}
.

We start with two tessellations in Figure
4.2
of the upper
half-plane
{\mathfrak{H}}
(case
{\mathrm{d}}=2
), both for the principal congruence
subgroup
\Gamma(2)
generated by
\left[{1\@@atop 0}{2\@@atop 1}\right]
and
\left[{1\@@atop 2}{0\@@atop 1}\right]
. This is
the largest subgroup of
\Gamma(1)=\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
that has no torsion.

Figure 4.2.
Two
\Gamma(2)
-invariant tessellations of

{\mathfrak{H}}
.

The upper tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
consists of all
\Gamma(2)
-translates of
a fundamental domain for
\Gamma(2)
in
{\mathfrak{H}}
. The lower tessellation is
the refinement
{\mathcal{T}}_{l}
, where each translate is divided into six
translates of the standard fundamental domain for
\Gamma(1)
in
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

A tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}
of

S^{\!\ast}
provides us with collections

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
of
i
-cells,
for
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
. The following conditions should be satisfied:

\mathrm{(a)}

An
i
-cell,
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, is a closed subset of

S^{\!\ast}
that is homeomorphic to the closed unit disk in
\mathbb{R}^{i}
. A

0
-cell is a singleton in
S^{\!\ast}
.

In the upper tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
in Figure
4.2
the set

\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{u}}_{0}
consists of the cusps
\infty
,
0
and
1
and all
their
\Gamma(2)
-translates, and all
\Gamma(2)
-translates of the point

{\zeta}_{6}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}\sqrt{3}
. The set
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{u}}_{1}
consists of
the edges
e_{{\zeta}_{6}-1,\infty}
,
e_{0,{\zeta}_{6}}
,
e_{1,{\zeta}_{6}+1}
and all
their
\Gamma(2)
-translates. The set
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{u}}_{2}
consists of all

\Gamma(2)
-translates of the fundamental domain
F
.

\mathrm{(b)}

For
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
the boundary
\partial X
of an

i
-cell
X
is the union

\bigcup_{Y\in B(X)}Y

where
B(X)\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i-1}
is a finite collection of

(i-1)
-cells.

In
{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
the
2
-cell
F
containing the cusps
\infty
and
0
has

B(F)=\bigl\{e_{{\zeta}_{6}-1,\infty},t\,e_{{\zeta}_{6}-1,\infty},e_{0,{\zeta}_{6}-1},t^{\prime}e_{0,{\zeta}_{6}-1},e_{{\zeta}_{6}+1,1},t^{\prime}t^{-1}\,e_{{\zeta}_{6}+1,1}\bigr\}\,,

where
t=\left[{1\@@atop 0}{2\@@atop 1}\right]
,
t^{\prime}=\left[{1\@@atop 2}{0\@@atop 1}\right]
. Furthermore,

B(e_{{\zeta}_{6}-1,\infty})=\{{\zeta}_{6}-1,\infty\}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

S^{\!\ast}=\bigcup_{X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}}X
.

\mathrm{(d)}

For
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, the pairwise intersection of

i
-cells is equal to the intersection of their boundaries.

\mathrm{(e)}

For
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1
, each
i
-cell is a boundary
component of at least one
(i+1)
-cell.

\mathrm{(f)}

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
and each
i
-cell
X
, the
set
\gamma\,X=\bigl\{\gamma\,x\;:\;x\in X\bigr\}
is an
i
-cell as
well. Furthermore
Y\in B(\gamma X)
if and only if
\gamma^{-1}Y\in B(X)
.
This action of
\Gamma
on
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, has finitely
many orbits.

In the description of
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{u}}_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq 2
in (a) we have
given generating elements for the
\Gamma(2)
-orbits in
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{u}}_{i}
.
Condition (

f

) requires that such finite choices can be made in
each tessellation. We can divide up
F
into finitely many subsets
(intersecting only in their boundaries), obtaining a refinement of the
tessellation. A division of
F
into infinitely many subcells is not
allowed.

Orientation.
We have to take into account the orientation of

i
-cells. Each
i
-cell
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{i}
can be given two orientations.
(See
4.4.1
for a further discussion.) We choose one of these
for each
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, and denote by
-X
the same

i
-cell with the opposite orientation. For
i=0
and
i={\mathrm{d}}
there are
obvious choices of the orientation, for the other dimensions the choice
seems arbitrary.

For our purpose it is important that the chosen orientation is invariant
under the action of
\Gamma
. If there were
\gamma\in\Gamma\smallsetminus\{e\}

such that
\gamma X=X
, then it might happen that
\gamma X
and
X
have
different orientations. Such an element
\gamma
would have finite order.
Since
\Gamma
is torsion-free, we see that
\gamma=e
.

We proceed with a
\Gamma
-invariant tessellations
{\mathcal{T}}
for which we
have chosen a
\Gamma
-invariant orientation. For
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, the
elements
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{\mathcal{T}}
form an algebraic basis of the vector space

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{\mathcal{T}}]
. The basis elements
X
have positive orientation,
and
-X
is the same
i
-cell as
X
, but with negative orientation.
These spaces are
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-modules for the action

\gamma\colon X\mapsto\gamma X
.

Separation between
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
and cuspidal horoballs.
The
tessellations that are handy for our purpose have the special property
that the boundary of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
in
S
is covered by
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells. An
example is sketched in Figure
4.3
.

Figure 4.3.
A
\Gamma(2)
-invariant tessellation of
{\mathfrak{H}}
in which

\partial S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
is covered by
1
-cells.
(For clarity different values of
Y
are used for the
horospheres.)

The procedure followed in this example works generally: Take a
fundamental domain
F
that has only one cusp in each
\Gamma
-orbit of
cusps and divide it up into
F\cap S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
and
F\cap B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
for all
cusps that occur in the closure of
F
in
S^{\!\ast}
. This leads to a
tessellation that is a refinement of the tessellation obtained from all

\Gamma
-translates of
F
.

For our purpose it does not matter if we move the intersections

F\cap B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
by an element of
N_{\mathsf{c}}
. See Figure
4.4
.
Of course, the boundary structure of the lower-dimensional cells will
become more complicated.

Figure 4.4.
The union of the four
2
-cells form a fundamental domain for

\Gamma(2)
in
{\mathfrak{H}}
. The
\Gamma(2)
-translates of these cells generate a
tessellation for which
\partial S_{\!\Gamma(2)}(Y)
is covered by

1
-cells.

By a
standard tessellation

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}
we indicate a tessellation of this
type: The set
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}}_{\mathrm{d}}
consists of the (free)
\Gamma
-orbits
of a cell
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
that is a fundamental domain for

\Gamma
in
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
, and the (free)
\Gamma
-orbits of

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}
for a set of representatives of

\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. Each cell
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}
is a fundamental domain for

\Gamma_{\mathsf{c}}
in
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
that has the product structure

F_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}g_{\mathsf{c}}A^{+}_{Y}
with a fundamental domain
F_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
for

\Gamma_{\mathsf{c}}
in
N_{\mathsf{c}}
.

For most of our discussions a standard tessellation will suffice.
Sometimes we need a refinement of a standard tessellation.

4.3.
Parabolic cohomology

We describe parabolic cohomology spaces based on a tessellation, and
discuss their geometric relevance.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
denote a standard tessellation, or a refinement of a
standard tessellation. We define the
\Gamma
-modules

F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}]
of
i
-chains for

0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, and take
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}=\{0\}
for
i>{\mathrm{d}}
.

For each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
,
i\geq 1
, we define
\partial_{i}X
as the
sum
\sum_{Y\in B(X)}\varepsilon_{Y}Y
with
\varepsilon_{Y}\in\{1,-1\}
such that the
orientation of
\varepsilon_{Y}Y
is the orientation that the boundary component

Y
inherits from the orientation of
X
(as will be discussed in
§
4.4.1
). The map
X\mapsto\partial_{i}X
induces the
boundary operator
\partial_{i}
, which is a

\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-equivariant map

\partial_{i}:F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\rightarrow F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i-1}
. In this way we arrive at the resolution

F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}

(4.6)

0\rightarrow F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathrm{d}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots F^{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{C}\,.

The augmentation
\varepsilon

is determined by
\varepsilon X=1
for all
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
. Giving
\mathbb{C}
the
structure of the trivial
\Gamma
-module, we get a
resolution of
\mathbb{C}
by
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-modules.
(See for instance
[
2
, p 10]
.)
The resolution is exact: one can check that at each step

\partial_{i}\partial_{i+1}=0
, and, moreover, the kernel of
\partial_{i}
is equal to the
image of
\partial_{i+1}
, for
i<{\mathrm{d}}
. (Take
\varepsilon
as replacement for

\partial_{0}
.) The resolution
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
is not a free
resolution: each cusp
{\mathsf{c}}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
is fixed by the infinite
subgroup
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}\subset\Gamma
.

The spaces of cochains

C^{i}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V\bigr)
,
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, for a
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module
V
consist of
the
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear maps
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\rightarrow V
. An
i
-cochain

c\in C^{i}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V\bigr)
is completely determined by
the
\Gamma
-equivariant map
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\rightarrow V
given by

X\mapsto c(X)
.

The boundary maps
\partial_{i+1}
induce
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear
coboundary maps
d^{i}:C^{i}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)\rightarrow C^{i+1}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)

induced by
(d^{i}c)(X)=c\bigl(\partial_{i+1}X)
for
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
.

The spaces

Z^{i}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)
of
cocycles are defined as
\ker(d^{i})
, and the subspaces

B^{i}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)\subset Z^{i}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)
of coboundaries
are defined as
\im(d^{i-1})
. Since there is no
C^{-1}
we have

B^{0}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)=\{0\}
.

The cohomology spaces

H^{i}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)
are the
quotients
Z^{i}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V\bigr)/B^{i}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V\bigr)
. For standard
tessellations
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}
and its refinements, the cohomology spaces
are naturally isomorphic, and we can define the parabolic cohomology spaces
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
as

H^{i}(F^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}}_{\bullet};V)
. In Subsection
4.5
we
will give a more detailed discussion.

Standard cohomology spaces.
For each
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module
C
the
space of
invariants
is

V^{\Gamma}=\bigl\{v\in V\;:\;\gamma v=v\text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma\bigr\}
, and the space

V_{\Gamma}
of
coinvariants
, is the space
V

modulo the subspace generated by the elements
\gamma\,v-v
with

\gamma\in\Gamma
and
v\in V
.

By defining
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}=\bigl\{X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\;:\;X\subset S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\bigr\}
we obtain a
tessellation of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
. This leads to a resolution

F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet}
in an analogous
way. This is a free resolution, and the cohomology spaces

H^{i}(F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet};V)
are isomorphic to the usual cohomology
spaces
H^{i}(\Gamma;V)
for all suitably large
Y
. The restrictions of
cochains
c\in C^{i}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet},V)
to
F_{i}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}
induce linear
maps
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)\rightarrow H^{i}(\Gamma;V)
.

Proposition 4.4
.

The spaces
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
and
H^{i}(\Gamma;V)

are zero for
i>{\mathrm{d}}
, and

(4.7)

\displaystyle H^{0}\bigl(\Gamma;V)

\displaystyle\cong H^{0}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V\bigr)\cong V^{\Gamma}\,,

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;V)

\displaystyle\cong V_{\Gamma}\,,\qquad H^{\mathrm{d}}\bigl(\Gamma;V\bigr)=\{0\}\,.

We will give a proof in §
4.5.3
.

Relation to sheaf cohomology.
The geometric relevance of parabolic
cohomology spaces
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
is their interpretation as sheaf
cohomology spaces on the quotient
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
. The global
functor
{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast})
has derived functors

{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto H^{i}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{F}})
. These cohomology
groups can be computed as C̆ech cohomology. See, e.g.
[
19
, Chap III,
§2, §4]
.

If
V
is a
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module, then
V\times S^{\!\ast}
can be considered as
a constant sheaf on
S^{\!\ast}
. The group
\Gamma
acts on this sheaf by

\gamma\,(v,x)=\bigl(\gamma v,\gamma\,x\bigr)
. The
quotient

(4.8)

{\mathcal{Q}}[V]=\Gamma\bigm\backslash(V\times S^{\!\ast})

is a locally constant sheaf on
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
. The parabolic
cohomology spaces can be related by cohomology spaces for sheaves on
the quotient
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
, like in
[
4
, Proposition
11.8]
.

Proposition 4.5
.

In this situation, for all
j\geq 0
,

(4.9)

H^{j}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;V\bigr)\;\cong\;H^{j}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{Q}}[V]\bigr)\,.

In §
4.6
we will give a sketch of a proof.

Remark 4.6
.

This proposition shows that the parabolic cohomology spaces are
connected to a well-known cohomology theory. It implies that there are
long exact sequences of parabolic cohomology spaces associated to short
exact sequences of
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-modules.

This finishes a discussion of parabolic cohomology and tessellations
that seems essential for the following sections of this paper. In the
further subsections of this section we give proofs of Propositions

4.4
and
4.5
, and more details on standard
tessellations.

4.4.
Further discussion of
\Gamma
-invariant
tessellations

The following definition complements
the informal discussion of tessellations in §
4.2
.

Definition 4.7
.

A
tessellation

{\mathcal{T}}
of
S^{\!\ast}

consists of
{\mathrm{d}}+1
collections
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
,

0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, of subsets of
S^{\!\ast}
with the following
properties.

\mathrm{(i)}

Cells.
The elements
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
,

0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, called
i
-cells
, are
closed subsets of
S^{\!\ast}
that have the following properties:

\mathrm{(a)}

Topological structure.
There is a
homeomorphism from
X
to the closed unit ball

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{i}\;:\;\|x\|\leq 1\bigr\}
.

By the boundary
\partial X
of
X
,
respectively the interior

\mathring{X}
of
X
, we understand the image
of the unit
i
-sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{i}
, respectively the image of the open
unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{i}
under this homeomorphism.

\mathrm{(b)}

Analytic structure.
Each
i
-cell
X
can be
described as

(4.10)

X=\bigl\{x\in S^{\!\ast}\;:\;f_{j}(x)=0\text{ for }1\leq j\leq a_{X}\,,\;h_{j}(x)\geq 0\text{ for }1\leq j\leq b_{X}\bigr\}\,,

with a finite number of continuous functions
f_{j}
, with

1\leq j\leq a_{X}
, and
h_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq b_{X}
, on a neighborhood of

X
in
S^{\!\ast}
, such that the restriction of each
f_{j}
to
S
is
analytic and the
h_{j}
are analytic on
\partial X\cap S
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Boundary decomposition.
Let

1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
. For each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
there is a finite subset

B(X)\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i-1}
such that

(4.11)

\partial X=\bigcup_{Y\in B(X)}Y\,.

\mathrm{(iii)}

Coverings.
Let
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The pairwise intersections of
i
-cells are
contained in the intersection of their boundaries.

\mathrm{(b)}

We have

(4.12)

\bigcup_{X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}}X=\begin{cases}W&\text{if }i={\mathrm{d}}\,,\\
\bigcup_{Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}}\partial Y&\text{ if }1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1\,.\end{cases}

\mathrm{(iv)}

All cells occur in a boundary.
If

0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1
, then

(4.13)

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}=\bigcup_{Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}}B(Y)\,.

Remarks.

\mathrm{(1)}

The
0
-cells are points in
W
.

\mathrm{(2)}

The interior and boundary of an
i
-cell
X
with

1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1
are not the interior and boundary of
X
as a
subset of the surrounding topological space
W
.

\mathrm{(3)}

Condition (

iv

) ensures that for
j>i
each
i
-cell
is contained in the boundary of some
j
-cell.

Definition 4.8
.

A tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}
of
S^{\!\ast}
is
\Gamma
-invariant if the following conditions are satisfied:

\mathrm{(a)}

Let
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
. If
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
, then

\gamma X=\bigl\{\gamma x\;:\;x\in X
,
\gamma\in\Gamma\bigr\}
is in

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
as well.

\mathrm{(b)}

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{j}
with

1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}

(4.14)

B(\gamma\,X)=\gamma\,B(X)=\bigl\{\gamma Y\;:\;Y\in B(X)\bigr\}\,.

4.4.1.
Orientation

There are various ways to formulate what we mean by orientation of the
cells of a tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}
. Here we use the analytic structure of
cells.

For points
p\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
we just view
p
as positively oriented,
and
-p
as negatively oriented. In the top dimension
{\mathrm{d}}
we consider

X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}
as positively oriented.

For
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1
, each choice of a
homeomorphism
\varphi:X\rightarrow U\subset\mathbb{R}^{i}
implies the choice
of coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i})
on
X
. We use differential forms

\omega=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i})\,dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{i}
with a
real-valued analytic function
f
.

Let
\varphi_{1}
be another homeomorphism as in Definition
4.7

(

i

-

i

a

). It can be viewed as the choice of other
coordinates
y_{1},\ldots,y_{i}
. The coordinate transformation from

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i})
to
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{i})
is described by the map

\psi=\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi^{-1}
. We have

\omega\circ\psi=(f\circ\psi)\;\det J(\psi)\,dy_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dy_{i}\,,

where
\det J(\psi)
is the determinant of the
Jacobian matrix
\Bigl(\frac{\partial\psi_{j}}{\partial x_{n}}\Bigr)

of the vector-valued function
\psi
. Whatever we have chosen as the
orientation of
X
, the transformation preserves the orientation if

\det J(\psi)>0
, and reverses it if
\det J(\psi)<0
. This is clear
for dimensions
0
and
{\mathrm{d}}
, but not for the intermediate dimensions.

Suppose now that for each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
we have chosen coordinates

\bigl(x_{X,1},\ldots,x_{X,i}\bigr)
as corresponding to the
positive orientation. For
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1
, we
have the transformation
s(\gamma,i)
fitting in the following scheme.

(4.15)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{U_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s(\gamma,i)}
\textstyle{\bigl(x_{X,1},\ldots,x_{X,i}\bigr)\text{ coord.}}
\textstyle{\gamma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\gamma X}}
\textstyle{U_{\gamma X}}
\textstyle{\bigl(x_{\gamma X,1},\ldots,x_{\gamma X,i}\bigr)\text{ coord.}}

The action of
\gamma
sending
X
to
\gamma X
determines a transformation

s(\gamma,i)
of
i
-forms. We have

(4.16)

\bigl(dx_{X,1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{X,i}\bigr)\circ s(\gamma,i)=\det J\bigl(s(\gamma,i)\bigr)\,\bigl(dx_{\gamma X,1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{\gamma X,i}\bigr)\,.

So the action of
\gamma\in\Gamma
preserves the chosen orientation if and
only if
\det J\bigl(s(\gamma,i)\bigr)>0
. For
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
there
are no non-trivial
\gamma\in\Gamma
stabilizing
X
(by the fact that

\Gamma
is torsion-free). So after we choose the orientation of
X
, this
induces a unique orientation of all
\gamma X
in the
\Gamma
-orbit of
X
.

4.4.2.
Refinements

We call a
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation

{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
a refinement of the
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}
if
for
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
is the union of a finite
set of
X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}_{i}
where different
X_{l}^{\prime}

overlap only in their common boundary. This induces
\mathbb{C}
-linear maps

F_{i}^{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow F^{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}_{i}
that we require to be

\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear and to be compatible with the boundary maps

\partial_{i}
in
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
and
F^{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}_{\bullet}
.

Lemma 4.9

(Refining a tessellation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}

be a
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation of
W\subset S
, and let

X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
. If

X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\ldots\cup X_{n}
with
i
-cells
X_{l}
such that the
pairwise intersections
X_{l}\cap X_{l^{\prime}}
have a structure as indicated
in part (

i

) of Definition
4.7
, then there is a
refined
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
of
W
such that

X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
is replaced by
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
in
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}_{i}
.
In this refinement,
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}}_{j}=\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{j}
for
i<j\leq{\mathrm{d}}
. The
sets
B(X)
with
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{j}
are unchanged for

i+1<j\leq{\mathrm{d}}
.

Proof.

The process is straightforward. For each
\gamma\in\Gamma

replace
\gamma X
by the new cells
\gamma X_{1},\ldots,\gamma X_{n}
. Adapt the
boundary decomposition of all
Y\in B(X)
. Next go down dimension by
dimension, adapting
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{j}
and the boundary decomposition in
dimension
j+1
.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Condition on refinements.

We use only refinements that respect the product form

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}=g_{\mathsf{c}}{\mathfrak{F}}(\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}})A^{+}_{T}\,\text{{{e}}}\cong{\mathfrak{F}}(\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}})\times[T,\infty)

in the subdivisions of the generators
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}
,
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.
We use the notation
A^{+}_{T}=\bigl\{\mathrm{a}(t)\;:\;t\geq T\bigr\}
.
We split
up
{\mathfrak{F}}(\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}})
into a finite number of cells defined by inequalities

a_{i}\leq x_{i}\leq b_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}-1
, in coordinates that
are linear forms in normalized horospherical coordinates, and split up

[T,\infty)
into a finite number of subintervals. This system of
coordinates need not be horospherical.

4.4.3.
Standard tessellations

On
p
4.2
we mentioned standard tessellations. The idea is
illustrated in Figures
4.2
–
4.4
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
Y
be suitably large. There exist
standard
\Gamma
-invariant tessellations
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}
of
S^{\!\ast}

with the following properties.

\mathrm{(i)}

For all
i
,
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, there is a subset

\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}
of
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
such that

\mathrm{(a)}

For each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
:

X\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad X\subset S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\,.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}
,
then there is a unique cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
such that
X\subset B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

The collection of sets
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}
,
determines a
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

Let
n
denote the number of cuspidal
\Gamma
-orbits.
The set
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}
is generated by
n+1
elements, namely

•

A
{\mathrm{d}}
-cell
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
, which is a fundamental
domain for
\Gamma
in
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
.

•

A
{\mathrm{d}}
-cell
{\mathfrak{F}}_{c}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{d}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
for
each representative
{\mathsf{c}}
in a system of representatives of

\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. The set
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}
is a fundamental domain for

\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
in
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
.

Proof.

We start with a fundamental domain of
\Gamma
in
S
. These domains can be
constructed in various ways. A nice example in the context of

G=\mathrm{PSU}(2,1)
is given by Falbel and Parker,
[
13
, Theorem 5.8 and
5.9]
. For the cofinite groups
\Gamma
under consideration the Ford
domains in
[
36
, Theorem 3.18]
also satisfy the requirements.
Here we use the Dirichlet method of construction of fundamental
domains; see the discussion of tessellations of type
Dir
in

[
4
, §6.2]
.

Tessellation of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
.
We choose
p_{0}
in the interior of

S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
for suitably large
Y
. Its
\Gamma
-orbit
\Gamma p_{0}
is an infinite
discrete subset of
S
. We use the distance function
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}
to define
the following fundamental domain:

(4.17)

D(p_{0})=\bigl\{x\in S\;:\;{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,p_{0})\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,p)\text{ for all }p\in\Gamma p_{0}\smallsetminus\{p_{0}\}\bigr\}\,.

This is a fundamental domain for
\Gamma
on
S
. Since
\Gamma
is discrete,
in the definition we need to take into account only a finite set

\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}\subset\Gamma p_{0}\smallsetminus\{p_{0}\}
.

The functions
h_{l}(x)={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,p_{l})-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,p_{0})
,
1\leq l\leq n
,
are analytic on a neighborhood of the boundary of
D(p_{0})
, and

(4.18)

D(p_{0})=\bigl\{x\in S\;:\;h_{l}(x)\geq 0\text{ for
}l=1,\ldots,n\bigr\}\,.

We intersect
D(p_{0})
with
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
, where
Y
is such that all horoballs

B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
,
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
, are pairwise disjoint. The number of cusps
in the closure of
D(p_{0})
in
S\cup\partial S
is finite. If we
further enlarge
Y
sufficiently, then

{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}:=D(p_{0})\cap S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
has a description like
(
4.18
).

The
\Gamma
-translates of
D(p_{0})\cap S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
cover
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
, and lead to a
tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}(D,Y)
of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
.

Tessellation of
N
.
Next we construct a tessellation of
the
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-dimensional group that is invariant under a lattice

\Lambda\subset N
. If
{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}=\{0\}
, then the group
N
is
isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{p}=\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
(as a group),
\Lambda
is isomorphic
to
\mathbb{Z}^{p}
, and
\Lambda\backslash N
is a torus, for which it is easy to
construct a fundamental domain, leading to a
\Lambda
-invariant
tessellation of
N
, for which the
i
-cells are determined by

(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p})\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}
and by a finite set

J\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
, namely

(4.19)

\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\;:\;n_{i}\leq x_{i}\leq n_{i}+1\text{ for }i\in J\,,\text{ and }x_{i}=n_{i}\text{ for }i\not\in J\bigr\}\,.

If
N
is not abelian, we can choose coordinates

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q})
on
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
such that the intersection

Z_{N}\cap\Lambda
of the lattice with the center
Z_{N}
of
N
is generated
by specific elements
\lambda_{j}
,
p+1\leq j\leq p+q
, such that the group
operation satisfies

(4.20)

\lambda_{j}\,\mathrm{n}[x]=\mathrm{n}\bigl[x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+1,x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{p+q}\bigr]\,.

We remind ourselves by the use of
square
brackets that these coordinates are not necessarily horospherical
coordinates. We can complete the choice of generators of
\Lambda
by
choosing
\lambda_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq p
, for which

(4.21)

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}\,\mathrm{n}[x]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{n}\bigl[x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+1,x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{p},

\displaystyle\hbox{}x_{p+1}+a_{p+1}(x^{(1)}),\ldots,x_{p+q}+a_{p+q}(x^{(1)})\bigr]\,,

with linear forms
a_{j}
on
\mathbb{R}^{p}
for
p+1\leq j\leq q
.

We can choose a fundamental domain
X(\Lambda)
for
\Lambda\backslash N
of
the form

\bigl\{x\;:\;0\leq x_{j}\leq 1\text{ for }1\leq j\leq p+q\bigr\}
.
We can bring any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
into
X(\Lambda)
by first applying

\lambda_{j}^{n}
with
1\leq j\leq p
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, to arrive at

0\leq x_{j}\leq 1
for
1\leq j\leq p
. Next we apply
\lambda_{j}^{n}
with

p+1\leq j\leq p+q
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, to adjust the coordinates with index
at least
p+1
. This fundamental domain has
(p+q-1)
-dimensional
boundary cells
B_{\varepsilon,j}
with
1\leq j\leq p+q
and
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,
determined by
x_{j}=\varepsilon
and
x_{j^{\prime}}\in[0,1]
for
j^{\prime}\neq j
.

For
j\geq p+1
we have the nice pairing of

\lambda_{j}\,B_{0,j}=B_{1,j}
. This does not work well if
j\leq p
. Then
the presence of the linear forms
a_{j^{\prime}}
will in general require

\mu\lambda_{j}
with
\mu\in\Lambda\cap Z_{N}
to carry out the
transformation. The element
\mu
depends on the values of
x
in

\mathrm{n}[x]\in B_{0,j}
. The values that the
a_{j^{\prime}}
will take on elements
of
B_{0,j}
are bounded, so finitely many elements of
\mu
in the
center of
\Lambda
can occur. See Figure
4.5
.

Figure 4.5.
The black cube is a fundamental domain
B
for

\Lambda\backslash N
with some lattice
\Lambda
in the context of

G=\PU(2,1)
, for which
{\mathrm{d}}=4
,
p=2
and
q=1
. On the left, the red
cube is the image of
B
under
\lambda_{3}=\lambda_{p+1}
. On the right the
image
\lambda_{1}B
is deformed.

The fundamental domain
X(\Lambda)
intersects a finite number of translates

\mu_{i}\lambda_{j}X(\Lambda)
with
\mu_{i}\in\Lambda\cap Z_{N}
,

1\leq i\leq n_{0,j}
. This intersection is the union

\bigcup_{i}B_{1,j}\cap\mu_{i}\lambda_{j}B_{0,j}
. The elements of this union
have pairwise intersections contained in boundary components. We
replace the boundary component
B_{0,j}
of
X(\Lambda)
by the subsets

B_{0,j}\cap\lambda_{j}^{-1}\mu_{i}^{-1}B_{1,j}
. (We recall that

\Lambda\subset N
, and that
Z_{N}
is center of
N
; so

\lambda_{j},\mu_{j}\in N
.) We write the
\left(p+q-1\right)
-cells
B_{0,j}

with
1\leq j\leq p
as a union
\bigcup_{i}B_{0,j}^{(i)}
. The
subcells of dimension
\left(p+q-1\right)
in this union intersect only
in their boundaries. If we increase the coordinate
x_{j}
by
1
we get
a subdivision of
B_{1,j}
. We can take the subdivisions in such a way
that
B_{1,j}
is the union of cells
\mu_{i}\lambda_{j}B_{0,j}
with

\mu_{i}\in\Lambda\cap Z_{N}
.

This process increases the number of
(p+q-1)
-cells. The resulting
system of cells gives a
\Lambda
-invariant tessellation

\mathcal{U}(\Lambda)
of
N
that satisfies
conditions analogous to those in Definitions
4.7

and
4.8
. The set
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}(\Lambda)}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
consists of one

\Lambda
-orbit. As a generator we may take the hypercube

{\mathfrak{F}}(\Lambda)=[0,1]^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
in the coordinates
indicated above.

Tessellation of a horoball.
We fix a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
and
describe the horoball
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
as

g_{\mathsf{c}}\bigl(N\times A_{Y}^{+}\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)
, with

A_{T}^{+}=\bigl\{\mathrm{a}(t)\in A\;:\;t\geq T\bigr\}
.

The group
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
stabilizing the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
acts on
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
. This
action leaves
g_{\mathsf{c}}
and
y
in

g_{\mathsf{c}}(n,y)\in g_{\mathsf{c}}\bigl(N\times[Y,\infty)\bigr)
invariant, and
changes only the element
n\in N
. Under conjugation by
g_{\mathsf{c}}
this
corresponds to the action of the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}}
on
N
by left
translation, for which we constructed a fundamental domain in the
previous subsection. This corresponds to a fundamental domain

g_{\mathsf{c}}\bigl({\mathfrak{F}}(\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}})\times[Y,\infty)\bigr)
of
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
. We
close it in
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
by adding the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
. This results in a
fundamental domain
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}
for

\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}\backslash B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
. It is a cone with top
{\mathsf{c}}
on the
hypercube
g_{\mathsf{c}}\bigl({\mathfrak{F}}(\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}})\times\{\mathrm{a}(Y)\}\bigr)
. The
resulting tessellation of
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
is a
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
-invariant
tessellation.

If
\gamma
runs over
\Gamma\backslash\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
then
\gamma\,B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
runs
over all horoballs
B_{{\mathsf{c}}^{\prime}}(Y)
with
{\mathsf{c}}^{\prime}
running over the

\Gamma
-orbit
\Gamma\,{\mathsf{c}}
. Then the
\Gamma
-translates
\gamma\,{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}

provide us with a
\Gamma
-invariant tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{a}
of the union

U_{a}=\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}^{\prime}\in a}B_{{\mathsf{c}}^{\prime}}(Y)
for the
\Gamma
-equivalence class

a\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
of
{\mathsf{c}}
.

Putting tessellations together.
We have obtained
n+1

tessellations: the tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}(D,Y)
of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
introduced at the
end of §
4.4.3
, and, for each of the
n
elements

a\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
, the tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{a}
of a union
U_{a}

of horoballs.

We have still to handle the intersections of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
with the
U_{a}
. This
intersection is a union of
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-dimensional horospheres. The
common refinement of these
n+1
tessellations forms a tessellation

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}
that satisfies the properties in Theorem
4.11
.

As generating
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells we can take
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}=D(p_{0})\cap S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
, and

{\mathfrak{F}}_{a}
as the generating element in
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
for the representative
of
a
that we used.
∎

4.5.
Tessellations and parabolic cohomology

We
will justify the name parabolic cohomology by showing that the
following three ways to construct cohomology spaces lead to canonically
isomorphic results:

\mathrm{(i)}

The cohomology spaces based on the resolution

F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
for a standard tessellation or refinements of it.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Sheaf cohomology spaces for the quotient

\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

Use of a resolution
F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}
based on the set
of cusps
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
and the
\Gamma
-action on it, to be defined in
§
4.5.2
.

The relation between (

i

) and (

ii

) is given by
Proposition
4.5
, which we will prove in §
4.6
.
Proposition
4.13
will relate (

iii

) to (

i

).
We define the parabolic cohomology
spaces
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
as the cohomology
spaces obtained from (

i

)–(

iii

).

The proof of Proposition
4.4
is in §
4.5.3
.

4.5.1.
Acyclic resolutions

We consider refinements
{\mathcal{T}}
of
a standard tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}
of the space
S^{\!\ast}
. It leads to
the resolution
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
and to cohomology spaces

H^{i}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\cdot)
with values in
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-modules.
We have also the tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{Y}
of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
,
connected to the resolution

F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet}=\bigl(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}]\bigr)_{i\geq 0}
.

We mention some terminology and facts from
[
2
, Chap I]
. One
speaks of an
acyclic resolution

if the complex is exact at each place. In each complex we have

\partial_{i-1}\partial_{i}=0
, and hence
\im(\partial_{i})

is contained in
\ker(\partial_{i-1})
.
The complex is acyclic if
\im(\partial_{i})=\ker(\partial_{i-1})
for
all
i\geq 2
, and in the case of a resolution of the trivial

\Gamma
-module
\mathbb{C}
the condition
\varepsilon\,\partial_{1}=0
for
i=1

(with the augmentation
\varepsilon
instead of
\partial_{0}
).

Let
A_{\bullet}
and
B_{\bullet}
denote complexes. A
chain map

f_{\bullet}:A_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
gives

\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear maps
f_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}
such that

\partial_{i}f_{i}=f_{i}\partial_{i}
for all
i
. (We allow ourselves to denote the boundary operator by

\partial_{i}
in all complexes.)

Two chain maps
f_{\bullet}:A_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
and

g_{\bullet}:A_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
are

homotopic
if the difference

f_{\bullet}-g_{\bullet}
can be written as

(4.22)

\partial_{i+1}h_{i}+h_{i-1}\partial_{i}=f_{i}-g_{i}

for some family
h_{\bullet}:A_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
of
homomorphisms
h_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B_{i+1}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Such a family

h_{\bullet}
is called a
homotopy
. Even if we
have a
\Gamma
-action on the complexes, the homotopies
h_{i}
need only be

\mathbb{C}
-linear.

Contractible spaces.
A space
W
is
contractible if it is homotopic to a space with
one point. In that case tessellations of
W
lead to acyclic
resolutions.

The horoballs
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
are contractible by use of the map

(4.23)

[0,1]\times B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)\rightarrow B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}\;:\;\begin{cases}(t,{\mathsf{c}})\mapsto{\mathsf{c}}\,,\\
\bigl(t,g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)\mapsto\begin{cases}g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}\bigl(y/(1-t)\bigr)&\text{ if }t<1\,,\\
{\mathsf{c}}&\text{ if }t=1\,.\end{cases}\end{cases}

The space
S
is contractible as well:

(4.24)

\bigl(t,k\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\mapsto k\mathrm{a}\bigl(y(1-t)\bigr)\,\text{{{e}}}\,.

Proposition 4.12
.

The resolutions
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
and

F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet}
are exact.

Proof.

Let
Y^{\prime}<Y
be suitably large: the horoballs
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y^{\prime})

for
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
are pairwise disjoint.

We showed above that each extended horoball
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
can be
contracted to
\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
. In a similar way we can contract
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y^{\prime})

to
B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y^{\prime})\smallsetminus B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
. Doing this for all cusps and leaving
the points of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y^{\prime})
on their place, we get a continuous
deformation of
S
into
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
. This implies that
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
is contractible.
That implies that
F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet}
is acyclic.

The contractibility of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
implies that there is a homotopy

h^{Y}_{\bullet}
between the identity chain map and the zero chain map
from
F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet}
in itself. It satisfies for all
i

(4.25)

\partial_{i+1}h_{i}^{Y}\xi+h_{i-1}^{Y}\partial_{i}\xi=\xi\qquad\text{for all }\xi\in F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}\,.

To show that the complex
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
is acyclic we take

C\in F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
and assume that
\partial C=0
. We want to show that

C\in\partial_{i+1}F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
.
We can write

(4.26)

C=a_{0}+\sum_{\mathsf{c}}a_{\mathsf{c}}

with
a_{0}\in F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}
, and
a_{\mathsf{c}}\in F^{{\mathcal{T}}({\mathsf{c}})}_{i}
for all

{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
, for the tessellation

{\mathcal{T}}({\mathsf{c}})
of
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
induced by

F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
. Since elements of
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
are finite linear
combinations of elements of
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
, all but finitely many
a_{\mathsf{c}}

are zero. Since

(4.27)

0=\partial_{i}a_{0}+\sum_{\mathsf{c}}\partial_{i}a_{\mathsf{c}},

each chain
\partial_{i}a_{\mathsf{c}}
is a linear combination of

X\in F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
that are contained in the horosphere
H_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)

(Definition
4.1
), and hence

\partial_{i}a_{\mathsf{c}}\in F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}\cap F^{{\mathcal{T}}({\mathsf{c}})}_{i}
.
For each cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
we put

(4.28)

\displaystyle q_{\mathsf{c}}

\displaystyle=h^{Y}_{i-1}\partial_{i}a_{\mathsf{c}}

\displaystyle\in F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}

\displaystyle=a_{\mathsf{c}}-\partial_{i+1}h^{Y}_{i}a_{\mathsf{c}}\,.

Hence

\partial_{i}(q_{\mathsf{c}}-a_{\mathsf{c}})=-\partial_{i}\partial_{i+1}h_{i}^{Y}a_{\mathsf{c}}=0
,
and

(4.29)

q_{\mathsf{c}}-a_{\mathsf{c}}\in\partial_{i+1}F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\,.

Hence

C=a_{0}+\sum_{\mathsf{c}}q_{\mathsf{c}}+\sum_{c}(a_{\mathsf{c}}-q_{\mathsf{c}})\equiv a_{0}+\sum_{\mathsf{c}}q_{\mathsf{c}}\bmod\partial_{i+1}F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\,.

The sum
C_{1}=a_{0}+\sum_{\mathsf{c}}q_{\mathsf{c}}
is an element of
F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}
,
and satisfies
\partial_{i}C_{1}=\partial_{i}C=0
. Since the space
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)

is contractible, we conclude that

C_{1}\in\partial_{i+1}F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{i}\subset\partial_{i+1}F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
.
∎

4.5.2.
A resolution based on cusps

Define

F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}
by

(4.30)

F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{i}=\mathbb{C}\bigl[{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}^{i+1}\bigr]\,,

with boundary operators
\partial_{i}:F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{i}\rightarrow F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i-1}

determined by

(4.31)

\partial_{i}({\mathsf{c}}_{0},\cdots,{\mathsf{c}}_{i})=\sum_{j=0}^{i}(-1)^{j}\,({\mathsf{c}}_{0},\ldots,\hat{\mathsf{c}}_{j},\ldots,{\mathsf{c}}_{i})\,.

(
\hat{\mathsf{c}}_{j}
means that the
j
-th coordinate is omitted. )
This gives a resolution

(4.32)

\cdots\rightarrow F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{C}\rightarrow 0

of the trivial
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-module

\mathbb{C}
. If we replace
{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
by a set with a free
\Gamma
-action, for
instance
\Gamma
itself, we have a free resolution, and its cohomology
spaces are the standard group cohomology spaces.

The action of
\Gamma
on
{\mathsf{c}}
is not free, and the resolution

F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}
is not free. It is acyclic, which one can check with
the homotopy
h_{i}:F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i}\rightarrow F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i+1}
determined by

(4.33)

h_{i}({\mathsf{c}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathsf{c}}_{i})=({\mathsf{b}},{\mathsf{c}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathsf{c}}_{i})\,,

for some fixed cusp
{\mathsf{b}}
.

Proposition 4.13
.

(Parabolic cohomology and tessellations)

If
{\mathcal{T}}
is a standard tessellation or a refinement
of it then

H^{i}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V\bigr)\cong H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;V\bigr)
for
all
i\geq 0
and all
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-modules
V
.

A consequence is that
H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)=\{0\}
for
i>{\mathrm{d}}
.

Proof.

We will choose
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear chain maps

a_{\bullet}:F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}\rightarrow F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
and

b_{\bullet}:F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}\rightarrow F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}
, and

\mathbb{C}
-linear homotopies
k_{\bullet}
and
h_{\bullet}
showing that

b_{\bullet}\circ a_{\bullet}
is homotopic to the identity in

F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\bullet}
and that
a_{\bullet}\circ b_{\bullet}
is homotopic to
the identity in
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
. Explicitly, these four systems of
maps have to satisfy the following relations.

(4.34)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}a_{i}\xi

\displaystyle=a_{i-1}\partial_{i}\xi

\displaystyle\text{ for }\xi\in F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i},\;i\geq 0\,,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}b_{i}\eta

\displaystyle=b_{i-1}\partial_{i}\eta

\displaystyle\text{ for }\eta\in F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i},\;i\geq 0\,,

(4.36)

\displaystyle b_{i}a_{i}\xi-\xi

\displaystyle=\partial_{i+1}k_{i}\xi+k_{i-1}\partial_{i}\xi

\displaystyle\text{ for }\xi\in F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i},\;i\geq 0\,,

(4.37)

\displaystyle a_{i}b_{i}\eta-\eta

\displaystyle=\partial_{i+1}h_{i}\eta+h_{i-1}\partial_{i}\eta

\displaystyle\text{ for }\eta\in F^{\mathcal{T}}_{i},\;i\geq 0\,.

We start with
\Gamma
-equivariant maps
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
. We can make the
easy choice
a_{0}{\mathsf{c}}={\mathsf{c}}
for all
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. With the
interpretation of
\partial_{0}
as the augmentation, this satisfies
(
4.34
), if we take
a_{-1}
as the identity map in the trivial

\Gamma
-module
\mathbb{C}
.

We choose representatives
{\mathsf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{c}}_{n}
of the
\Gamma
-orbits of
cusps, and representatives
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
of the
\Gamma
-orbits in

\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}
. We take
b_{0}\,{\mathsf{c}}_{r}={\mathsf{c}}_{r}
for
1\leq r\leq n

and
b_{0}\,p_{s}={\mathsf{c}}_{1}
for
1\leq s\leq m
. This determines

b_{0}:F^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\rightarrow F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!0}
as a
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear map, which
satisfies (
4.35
) for
i=0
, if we take
b_{-1}
as the identity
map in
\mathbb{C}
.

Start of the induction.
For
i=0
we get from (
4.36
) for all

{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
:

0={\mathsf{c}}-{\mathsf{c}}=\partial_{1}k_{0}{\mathsf{c}}+k_{-1}\partial_{0}{\mathsf{c}}=\partial_{1}k_{0}{\mathsf{c}}+k_{-1}\,1\,.

We use that
\partial_{0}
is the augmentation, and sends all cusps
{\mathsf{c}}
to
1\in\mathbb{C}
for all cusps
{\mathsf{c}}
.
We can satisfy this by taking
k_{0}=k_{-1}=0
.

Not all maps in (
4.36
) and (
4.37
) are necessarily

\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear, and we have to let
\xi
and
\eta
run through all

0
-cells. From (
4.37
) we get for
i=0

\displaystyle 0=a_{0}b_{0}(\gamma{\mathsf{c}}_{r})-\gamma{\mathsf{c}}_{r}

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}h_{0}(\gamma c_{r})+h_{-1}1\,,

\displaystyle\gamma{\mathsf{c}}_{1}-\gamma p_{s}=a_{0}b_{0}(\gamma p_{s})-\gamma p_{s}

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}h_{0}(\gamma p_{s})+h_{-1}\,1\,.

Here we can take
h_{-1}=0
and
h_{0}({\mathsf{c}})=0
for all
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. We
take
-h_{0}(\gamma p_{s})
as a path in
\mathbb{Z}\bigl[\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{1}]
along edges
in
X^{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
from
\gamma p_{s}
to
\gamma{\mathsf{c}}_{1}
for

\gamma p_{s}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
.

Induction steps.
Now we assume that we have made choices so that
(
4.34
)–(
4.37
) are satisfied for all values of
i
satisfying

0\leq i\leq j
.

Relation (
4.34
) requires that

\partial_{i+1}a_{i+1}\xi=a_{i}\partial_{i+1}\xi
.
This would imply that

\partial_{i}a_{i}\partial_{i+1}\xi=\partial_{i}\partial_{i+1}a_{i+1}\xi=0\,a_{i+1}\xi=0\,.

By the acyclicity of the complex, there exist
\vartheta\in F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i+1}

such that

\partial_{i+1}a_{i+1}\xi=a_{i}\partial_{i+1}\xi=\partial_{i+1}\vartheta\,.

So we can take
a_{i+1}\xi
equal to this solution
\vartheta
. To get

\Gamma
-equivariance, we do this for each
\xi
in a system of
representatives of
\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}^{i+2}
, and extend this to a
definition of
a_{i+1}
. We note that this procedure is not
constructive, and needs the axiom of choice.

For
b_{i+1}
we follow the same approach. For
i+1>{\mathrm{d}}
the map

b_{i+1}
is automatically zero. For
i+1\leq{\mathrm{d}}
, the sets

\Gamma\backslash\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
are finite. (This illustrates an
advantage of the use of tessellations.)

We turn to the relation

\partial_{i+2}k_{i+1}\xi=b_{i+1}a_{i+1}\xi-\xi-k_{i}\partial_{i+1}\xi

for all
\xi\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}^{i+2}
. We check that the right-hand side is in the
kernel of
\partial_{i+1}
. Hence we can pick a solution
k_{i+1}\xi
,
and extend it
\mathbb{C}
-linearly to
F^{\mathrm{pb}}_{\!\!i+1}
. For
h_{i+1}
we proceed
similarly.
∎

4.5.3.
Proof of Proposition
4.4

We use a standard tessellation
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}
, and compute the parabolic
cohomology as
H^{i}\bigl(F^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{st}}}_{\bullet};V)
.

For
i=0
in (
4.7
) we note that any two points

p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{0}
are connected by a path in

\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{1}]
along edges of the tessellation. If

c\in Z^{0}\bigl(F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet};V\bigr)
then
c(p_{1})=c(p_{2})
if

p_{1}
and
p_{2}\in X^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{0}
are connected by an element of

\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{1}
. So
c
is a constant function on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{0}
.
Since
\Gamma
acts freely on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{0}
the constant value is

\Gamma
-invariant. In dimension
0
the space of coboundaries is zero. So

H^{0}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V\bigr)\cong V^{\Gamma}
.

For
F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet}
we can give a similar argument. The inclusion

\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
induces a map sending a constant
function on
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
to its restriction to

\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{0}
.

Now let
i={\mathrm{d}}
. We use the generating
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells indicated in part
(

iii

) of Proposition
4.11
.

Let
c\in Z^{\mathrm{d}}\bigl(F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet};V)
. It is determined by its
value on the
{\mathrm{d}}
-cell
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
, which is the sole generator of

\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
. We can add a coboundary
b
by giving an arbitrary
value on one boundary component
y_{0}
of the first type, extending it

\Gamma
-equivariantly to its
\Gamma
-orbit, and by taking
b(z)=0
for
z
in
all other
\Gamma
-orbits of
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells.
In this way we can add to

c({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})
an arbitrary value in
V
. This implies that

H^{\mathrm{d}}\bigl(F^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\bullet};V\bigr)=\{0\}
.

The cocycles in
Z^{\mathrm{d}}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};V)
are determined by their
values on
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
and by the cells
{\mathfrak{F}}_{a}
,
a\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.

For each
a\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
the cell
{\mathfrak{F}}_{a}
has boundary
components in common with
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
. Let
Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
be one
of these components. We define a coboundary
db
, giving
b(Y)
an
arbitrary value
\beta\in V
, extending this by

b(\gamma Y)=\tau(\gamma)b(Y)
to the orbit of
Y
, and taking
b
equal
to zero on all other
\Gamma
-orbits in
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
. (Here we use

\tau
to indicate the representation of
\Gamma
in
V
.) Since
Y

occurs in
\partial{\mathfrak{F}}_{a}
and in
\partial{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
with opposite signs,
we can move in this way the value of
c({\mathfrak{F}}_{a})
to
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
. In this way
we can replace
c
by a cocycle
c^{\prime}
in its cohomology class that
satisfies
c^{\prime}({\mathfrak{F}}_{a})=0
for all
a\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. So the
cohomology class of
c^{\prime}
is determined by
c^{\prime}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})
.

We still have the freedom of adding a coboundary
db
such that
b
has
an arbitrary value
v
on
Y\in B({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})
that is not contained in a
horosphere. There is a boundary identification by some
\gamma\in\Gamma

which adds
\tau(\gamma)v-v
to
c^{\prime}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})
. Since
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}
is a
fundamental domain for
\Gamma
in
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
, this changes
c({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})
by a
trivial coinvariant. We may also change
c^{\prime}
by adding a coboundary
based on
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells in the intersection of
{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{F}}_{a}
for
some class
a
of cusps. However, if we want to keep
c^{\prime}({\mathfrak{F}}_{a})
equal
to zero, this does not change the value
c({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})
.

Thus, we conclude that the cohomology class is determined by

c^{\prime}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})\in V\bmod\sum_{\gamma}\bigl(\tau(\gamma)-1\bigr)V=V_{\Gamma}
.
∎

4.6.
Proof of Proposition
4.5

For
each
\Gamma
-module
V
we consider
V\times S^{\!\ast}
as a constant sheaf
over
S^{\!\ast}
. The quotient
{\mathcal{Q}}[V]
determines a locally constant sheaf
on
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
. We have to show that
H^{j}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;V)
is
isomorphic to the sheaf cohomology space

H^{j}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{Q}}[V]\bigr)
.

We follow the approach of the proof of Proposition 11.8 in
[
4
]
,
with the references given there. The fact that here we do not use mixed
parabolic cohomology gives a simplification. For each
0
-cell
p
, we
form the open set
\Omega_{p}
that is the union of
\{p\}
and of the
interiors (as defined in Definition
4.7

(

i

-

i

a

))
\mathring{X}
for all

X\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq{\mathrm{d}}}\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
that contain
p
. We use
a refinement
{\mathcal{T}}
of the standard tessellation
{{\mathcal{T}}_{\!{\mathrm{st}}}}
with the
property that for all
x\in\Omega_{p}\cap S
and all

\gamma\in\Gamma\smallsetminus\{e\}
the point
\gamma x
is not in
\Omega_{p}
. If

p={\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
then
\Omega_{\mathsf{c}}
is contained in the union of the open
horoball
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)\smallsetminus H_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
, which does not contain
other cusps.

Let
\pi:S^{\!\ast}\rightarrow\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
be the natural
projection. The set

\mathfrak{A}=\bigl\{\pi\Omega_{p}\;:\;p\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\bigr\}
is a
finite covering of
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
. Proceeding as in
[
4
]

this gives an isomorphism with C̆ech cohomology spaces:

H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;V\bigr)\cong\check{H}^{i}\bigl(\mathfrak{A};{\mathcal{Q}}[V]\bigr)\,.

See, eg,
[
19
, Chap III, §4]
for C̆ech cohomology. Leray’s
theorem
[
19
, Exerc. 4.11]
gives

H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\mathfrak{A};{\mathcal{Q}}[V])=\check{H}^{i}(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{Q}}[V])

if
H^{k}(U;{\mathcal{Q}}[V]|_{U})=\{0\}
for all
k\geq 1
for all intersections
U

of elements of
\mathfrak{A}
.

If
U
is in
\pi S
, the sheaf
{\mathcal{Q}}[V]
is the constant sheaf
V

on
U
, and
H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{Q}}[V])=0
for all
k\geq 1
.

Finally, suppose that
U
contains the image
\pi{\mathsf{c}}
of a cusp. Then

{\mathcal{Q}}[V]
restricted to
U\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
is the constant sheaf
V
,
and we have the short exact sequence

(4.38)

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}[V]|_{U}\rightarrow V\rightarrow j_{\ast}(V/V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}})\rightarrow 0\,,

where
j:\{{\mathsf{c}}\}\rightarrow U
is the natural injection, and
j_{\ast}

is the direct image functor determined by
j
, defined on
[
19
, p
65]
. To see this observe that the stalks at

u\in U\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
form the sequence

0\rightarrow V\rightarrow V\rightarrow 0\rightarrow 0\,,

and the stalk at
p
gives

0\rightarrow V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}}\rightarrow V\rightarrow V/V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}}\rightarrow 0\,.

The sheaf
j_{\ast}(V/V^{\Gamma{\mathsf{c}}})
is a skyscraper sheaf with support

\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
. To (
4.38
) corresponds a long exact sequence that starts
with

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U;({\mathcal{Q}}[V])|_{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U;V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H^{0}(U;j_{\ast}(V/V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V/V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

So the next part starts with

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(U;({\mathcal{Q}}[V])|_{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(U;V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{0}

The constant sheaves have zero cohomology spaces in dimension
1
and
larger. We see that
H^{1}(U;({\mathcal{Q}}[V])|_{U})=0
.

For
i\geq 2

\textstyle{H^{i-1}\bigl(U;j_{\ast}(V/V^{\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}})\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H^{i}(U;({\mathcal{Q}}[V])|_{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}(U;V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

In dimensions
i\geq 1
the cohomology spaces of skyscraper sheaves are
zero. We conclude that
H^{i}(U;({\mathcal{Q}}[V])|_{U})=0
for all
i\geq 1
. This
gives the last ingredient to get the desired isomorphism.∎

5.
Spherical principal series

We will use the spherical principal series representations of
G
. In
particular, we need the realization in functions on
\partial S

discussed in §
5.2
.

5.1.
The spherical principal series of
G

The

principal series

H^{\xi,\nu\alpha}_{0}

depends on a spectral parameter
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and a representation

\xi
of
M

(the centralizer of
A
in
K
)
in a finite-dimensional vector space
V
. The space
H^{\xi,\nu\alpha}_{0}

consists of the continuous functions
f:G\rightarrow V
that satisfy

(5.1)

f\bigl(nm\mathrm{a}(t)g)=t^{\rho+\nu}\,\xi(m)\,f(g)\qquad(n\in N,\;t>0,\;m\in M)\,,

and becomes a representation of
G
with the action
R
by right
translation. In
t^{\rho+\nu}=\mathrm{a}(t)^{(\rho+\nu)\alpha}
we tacitly
identify the number
\rho+\nu\in\mathbb{C}
with

(\rho+\nu)\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\alpha
, the space of linear forms on
{\mathfrak{a}}
.

In this paper we use the spherical principal series representations

H^{1,\nu\alpha}_{0}
, in which
\xi
is the trivial representation of
M

in
V=\mathbb{C}
. We denote

H^{\nu}_{0}=H^{1,\nu\alpha}_{0}
. One may
impose more strict regularity conditions on the functions

f\in H^{\nu}_{0}
. We will use
H^{\nu}_{\infty}\subset C^{\infty}(G)
,

H^{\nu}_{\omega}\subset C^{\omega}(G)

(the space of (real-)analytic functions).

The space
H^{\nu}_{K}

consists of
K
-finite functions. For each
f\in H^{\nu}_{K}
the elements

R(k)f
with
k\in K
lie in a finite-dimensional subspace. The Lie
algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
acts in
H^{\nu}_{K}
by right differentiation. The
restriction of this action to
{\mathfrak{k}}
corresponds to an action of
K

by right translation.

The Casimir element
{\mathsf{Cas}}
in the center of the universal enveloping
algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
was discussed in §
2.6
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The operator
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
acts in
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
as multiplication by the
factor
\nu^{2}-\rho^{2}
.

This parametrization, by the spectral parameter
\nu
, explains the use
of
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
as eigenvalue of the Laplace operator
\Delta
,
corresponding to
-R({\mathsf{Cas}})
, in Definition
3.5
of automorphic
forms.

See §
5.3
for a proof of this proposition.

Proposition 5.2

(Kostant,
[
27
, Theorem 2]
)
.

H^{\nu}_{K}
is an irreducible
({\mathfrak{g}},K)
-module unless

(5.2)

\begin{cases}\nu+\rho\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu\not\in(-\rho,\rho)&\text{
if }q=0\,,\\
\nu+\rho\in 2\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu\not\in\bigl(-\tfrac{p}{2}-1,\tfrac{p}{2}+1\bigr)&\text{ if }q\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

We recall that
p
and
q
are the dimensions of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{2\alpha}
, and that
\rho=\frac{1}{2}p+q
; see §
2.2
.
In the region
i\mathbb{R}\cup[-\rho,\rho]
in which spectral parameters

\nu
of cusp forms can occur, the endpoints
\nu=\pm\rho

correspond to reducibility of
H^{\nu}_{K}
if
q=0
or
1
. If
q\geq 2
,
then reducibility occurs for
\pm\nu=\rho-2\ell
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}

and
0\leq 2\ell\leq q-1
.

Table
5.1
may be helpful to compare the notation in
Kostant’s statement with our notation.

Kostant
[
27
, §2]

here

\Lambda_{+}^{1}

\{\alpha\}

\alpha

\lambda-\rho=\langle\lambda-\rho,w_{\alpha}\rangle

m_{\alpha}
