Corresponding notations.

5.2.
The realization on
\partial S

Functions on
G
that satisfy (
5.1
) are determined by their values
on
K
, by the Iwasawa decomposition. In the spherical principal series
they satisfy
f(mk)=f(k)
for
m\in M
, hence they are functions on

M\backslash K
. So
k\mapsto f(k^{-1})
is a function on

K/M=\partial S
, and the transformation
\Psi_{\nu}f(k)=f(k^{-1})

gives a linear bijection from the space
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
to

C^{\infty}(\partial S)
. Moreover,

\Psi_{\nu}H^{\nu}_{\omega}=C^{\omega}(\partial S)
.

Functions on
\partial S
.
When dealing with functions on

\partial S
we often use that
\partial S\cong K/M\cong G/MAN
.
We use
b
as a variable on
\partial S
, and write

f(b)=f(k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})=f(g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})
if
k
represents
b
in
K/M
or if

g
represents
b
in
G/MAN
. Then

f(g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})=f\bigl(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(g)\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})
. By
db
we denote the invariant
measure on
\partial S
such that

\int_{b\in\partial S}f(b)\,db=\int_{k\in K}f(k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})\,dk
. We
normalize the Haar measure
dk
so that
K
has volume
1
.

Action of
G
.
We put for
g\in G
and

k\in K

(5.3)

J(g,k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\ast}\,,

in the notation of Proposition
2.1
. To see that this is
well-defined we note that
m\in M
normalizes
N
and centralizes
A
,
and hence

gkm=\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\,\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\bigr)\,\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)m\in Km\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\bigr)N=K\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\bigr)N\,.

Proposition 5.3
.

J(g,b)
is analytic on
G\times(K/M)
,
and satisfies

(5.4)

J(gh,b)=J(g,h\,b)J(h,b)\qquad(g,h\in G,\;b\in\partial S)\,.

Proof.

Let
b=k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
with some
k\in K
. Then for

g\in G
we have

gk=\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\bigr)\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)\in KAN\,,

and
\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)=J(g,k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})
. This shows, together with
Proposition
2.1
, that
J(g,k)
is analytic in
(g,k)
.

The action of
G
on
K/M
is given by
g\,(k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})=\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk)
. We
apply the opposite form of the Iwasawa decomposition first to
hk
, and
then to
g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)
:

\displaystyle ghk

\displaystyle\in g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\mathrm{a}(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk))N=\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\;\mathrm{a}(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk))N

\displaystyle\subset\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\mathrm{a}(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk))N\subset K\;\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\;\mathrm{a}(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk))N

\displaystyle=K\,\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\,\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\,N\,,

where we use that
A
normalizes
N
, and that
t\mapsto\mathrm{a}(t)
is a group
homomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}_{>0}\rightarrow A
. This implies that

\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\,\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)
is the component in
A
in
the opposite Iwasawa decomposition of
ghk
, and hence that

\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(ghk)=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(g\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\bigr)\,\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(hk)\,.

This gives (
5.4
).
∎

Property (
5.4
) shows that
J
is a factor of automorphy, and,
since
J
has positive values, powers
J^{z}
with
z\in\mathbb{C}
are factors
of automorphy as well. We put for functions
f
on

\partial S

(5.5)

\tau_{\nu}(g)f:b\mapsto J(g^{-1},b)^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,f(g^{-1}\,b)\qquad(g\in G,\;b\in\partial S)\,.

Then
\tau_{\nu}
is a representation of
G
in the functions on

\partial S
.

We have thus obtained the realization of the spherical principal series
in functions on
\partial S
:

Proposition 5.4
.

The operators
\tau_{\nu}(g)
in
(
5.5
) define a representation
\tau_{\nu}
of
G
in each of the
spaces of (real-)analytic functions, and smooth functions on

\partial S
. The resulting representations
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)

and
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)

are isomorphic to
H^{\nu}_{\omega}
and
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
. The isomorphism is
given by the bijective linear operator
\Psi_{\nu}

that associates to a function
f
on
K/M
the function
\Psi_{\nu}f
on

G
given by

(5.6)

(\Psi_{\nu}f)(n\mathrm{a}(t)k)=t^{\rho+\nu}\,f(k^{-1})\,.

A
G
-equivariant sheaf.
If
f
is an analytic function on
an open subset
I\subset\partial S
, then the formula in
(
5.5
) gives the analytic function
\tau_{\nu}(g)f
on
gI
.

Definition 5.5
.

We define
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
as a sheaf on
\partial S

by defining for open
I\subset\partial S

the linear space
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)
of analytic functions on
I
. We make it
into a
G
-equivariant sheaf by the operators

\tau_{\nu}(g):\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(g\,I)
for
g\in G
.

5.3.
Proof of Proposition
5.1

We determine the eigenvalue of
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
in
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
. The Casimir
element
{\mathsf{Cas}}
is in the center of the universal enveloping algebra

U({\mathfrak{g}})
. As a differential operator on
G
it commutes with left and
right translations. The representation
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
is irreducible
for a dense set of values of
\nu
in
\mathbb{C}
. So for general values of

\nu
the action of
R({\mathsf{Cas}})
in
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
is given by an
eigenvalue
\lambda_{\nu}
. Actually, this extends to all
\nu
, since the
spaces
H^{\nu}_{\infty}
form a holomorphic family depending on
\nu
. To
compute the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\nu}
we consider
R({\mathsf{Cas}})f
for a
function
f
as in (
5.1
) with
f(e)\neq 0
. Then

R({\mathsf{Cas}}f)(e)=\lambda_{\nu}f(e)
.

Since
\exp({\mathbf{X}})\in N
for all
{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathfrak{n}}
we have

(5.7)

R({\mathbf{X}}_{i})f(e)=\partial_{x}f\bigl(\exp(x{\mathbf{X}}_{i})\bigr)\bigm|_{x=0}=0\,.

In a similar way, we have
R({\mathbf{U}}_{j})f(e)=0
. Using this and
(

v

) in §
2.2.1
we obtain at the unit element

e\in G

\displaystyle\bigl(R({\mathsf{Cas}})f\bigr)(e)

\displaystyle=R\Bigl({\mathbf{H}}_{0}^{2}-\sum_{i}{\mathbf{U}}_{i}^{2}-\sum_{i}2(\theta{\mathbf{X}}_{i}){\mathbf{X}}_{i}+(p+2q){\mathbf{H}}_{0}\Bigr)f(e)

\displaystyle=R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})^{2}f(e)-2\rho\,R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})f(e)\,.

We use that

f\bigl(\exp(x{\mathbf{H}}_{0})\bigr)=f\bigl(\mathrm{a}(e^{x})\bigr)=e^{x(\rho+\nu)}\,f(e)
, and hence

\displaystyle R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})f(e)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}f\bigl(\exp(x{\mathbf{H}}_{0})\bigr)\bigm|_{x=0}=\partial_{x}e^{x(\rho+\nu)}f(e)\bigm|_{x=0}

\displaystyle=(\nu+\rho)\,f(e)

to get

\displaystyle R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})^{2}f(e)

\displaystyle-2\rho\,R({\mathbf{H}}_{0})f(e)=(\nu+\rho)^{2}\,f(e)-2\rho(\nu+\rho)\,f(e)

\displaystyle=(\nu^{2}-\rho^{2})\,f(e)\,,

and

\lambda_{\nu}=\nu^{2}-\rho^{2}\,.

6.
From cusp forms to cocycles

The preparations in the previous sections are sufficient to construct
from a given cusp form a cocycle with values in the spherical principal
series. See Theorem
6.6
.

We now generalize the Green form in
[
4
, §1.3]
: We define a
kernel function on
\left(\partial S\right)\times G
, a generalization
of the Green form, and the definition of a
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-form on
S
with
values in a spherical principal series representation. Integrating this
differential form over
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells in a tessellation leads to

({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cocycles that describe the map from cusp forms to cohomology
classes.

6.1.
Kernel function

One of the tools in

[
4
]
to go from automorphic forms to cocycles is the kernel
function
R(t;z)^{s}
in
[
4
, (1.6)]
. It relates functions of

t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\!\mathbb{R}}
(the boundary of the symmetric space) to functions of

z\in{\mathfrak{H}}
(the symmetric space).

We start with the function
\Phi_{\nu}\in H^{\nu}_{\omega}
given, in the notation
in (
2.8
), by

(6.1)

\Phi_{\nu}(n\mathrm{a}(t)k)=\mathrm{a}(t)^{(\rho+\nu)\alpha}=t^{\rho+\nu}\qquad(n\in N,\;k\in K,\;t>0)\,.

It behaves according to a character of
NAM
on the left, and is
invariant under
K
on the right.

In §
5.2
we realized the principal series on the boundary

\partial S
. The function
\Phi_{\nu}
corresponds to the constant
function
k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\mapsto\Phi_{\nu}(k^{-1})=1
in this realization.
Proposition
5.1
and equation (
2.39
) imply that

\Delta\Phi_{\nu}=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})\Phi_{\nu}
.

The kernel function
r_{\nu}
is defined by

(6.2)

r_{\nu}(k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}};g\,\text{{{e}}})=\Phi_{\nu}(k^{-1}g)\qquad\text{ for }k\in K,\;g\in G\,.

To see that this is well-defined, we check that for
m\in M
and

k_{1}\in K

\displaystyle m^{-1}k^{-1}gk_{1}

\displaystyle=m^{-1}\mathbf{n}(k^{-1}g)\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}(k^{-1}g)\bigr)\mathbf{k}(g^{-1}k)k_{1}\in m^{-1}Nm\,\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}(k^{-1}g)\bigr)\,m^{-1}\,K

\displaystyle=N\,\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}(k^{-1}g)\bigr)\,K\,.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
.

\mathrm{(i)}

The function
r_{\nu}(\cdot;\cdot)
is a well-defined
analytic function on
(\partial S)\times S
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Let
b\in\partial S
and
g\in G
.

\mathrm{(a)}

r_{\nu}(\cdot;g\,\text{{{e}}})=\tau_{\nu}(g)\,1

as an equality of functions in
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.

\mathrm{(b)}

The function
r_{\nu}(b;\cdot)
on
S
is an
eigenfunction of
\Delta
with eigenvalue
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

The function
r_{\nu}(b;\cdot)
has polynomial
growth at the boundary.

\mathrm{(iii)}

For each
g\in G

(6.3)

\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(g)r_{\nu}(\cdot;x)\bigr)(b)=\bigl(L(g^{-1})r_{\nu}(b;\cdot)\bigr)(x)\,,

which is equivalent to

(6.4)

\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(g)\times L(g)\bigr)r_{\nu}(\cdot;\cdot)=r_{\nu}(\cdot;\cdot)\,.

Proof.

Since
\Phi_{\nu}
is analytic, part (

i

) follows from
(
6.2
).

The invariance of
\Delta
under left translation implies
(

ii

-

ii

b

). The function
\Phi_{\nu}
has polynomial
growth at the boundary. This is preserved under left translation. This
gives (

ii

-

ii

c

).

The kernel
r_{\nu}
on
(\partial S)\times S
given in (
6.2
)
satisfies
\bigl(L(k)\times L(k)\bigr)r_{\nu}=r_{\nu}
for
k\in K
. Let

b=k_{b}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
,
k_{b}\in K
and
x=h_{x}\,\text{{{e}}}
,
h_{x}\in G
. For

g\in G

\displaystyle\Bigl(\bigl(1\times L(g^{-1})\bigr)

\displaystyle r_{\nu}\Bigr)(b;x)=\Phi_{\nu}\bigl(k_{b}^{-1}gh_{x}\bigr)=\Phi_{\nu}\bigl(\mathbf{n}(k_{b}^{-1}g)\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}(k_{b}^{-1}g)\bigr)\mathbf{k}(k_{b}^{-1}g)h_{x}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t}\bigl(k_{b}^{-1}g\bigr)^{\rho+\nu}\,\Phi_{\nu}\bigl(\mathbf{k}(k_{b}^{-1}g)h_{x}\bigr)=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(g^{-1}k_{b})^{-\rho-\nu}\,\Phi_{\nu}\bigl(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(g^{-1}k_{b})h_{x}\bigr)

\displaystyle=J(g^{-1},b)^{-\rho-\nu}\,r_{\nu}\bigl(g^{-1}\,b;x\bigr)=\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(g)\times 1)r_{\nu}(b;x)\,.

This gives (

iii

).

For (

ii

-

ii

a

) we note that

r_{\nu}(b;\text{{{e}}})=\Phi_{\nu}(k_{b}^{-1})=1
. Hence

r_{\nu}(b;g\,\text{{{e}}})=\bigl(L(g^{-1})r_{\nu}(b;\cdot)\bigr)(\text{{{e}}})=\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(g)r_{\nu}(\cdot^{\prime}\text{{{e}}})\bigr)(b)=\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(g)1\bigr)(b)\,.\qed

6.2.
Generalization of Green’s form

We now generalize the
Green’s form in
[
4
, §1.3]
.

We define for
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(S)
the antisymmetric bilinear form
in
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
with values in
C^{\infty}(S)

(6.5)

{\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})=f_{1}\,\Delta f_{2}-f_{2}\,\Delta f_{1}\,.

This bilinear form is the starting point in the derivation of the
Maass–Selberg relations. This works for more general Lie groups; see
for instance
[
18
, Chap IV, §2]
.

The
G
-invariant measure
d\mu
on
S
is obtained by integration of
the invariant volume form

{\zeta}_{S}=\sqrt{\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x)}\,dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{\mathrm{d}}

in any choice of coordinates on
S
. The matrix
g^{(R)}
describes the
Riemannian metric; see the discussion on §
2.4
. In terms of
horospherical coordinates,
g^{(R)}
has the description
in (
2.32
). Then
{\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})\,{\zeta}_{S}
is a
{\mathrm{d}}
-form on
S
that
satisfies

{\mathrm{MS}}\bigl(L(h^{-1})f_{1},L(h^{-1})f_{2}\bigr){\zeta}_{S}={\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2}){\zeta}_{S}\circ h

for all
h\in G
.

The
{\mathrm{d}}
-form
{\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})\,{\zeta}_{S}
is closed (since
{\mathrm{d}}=\dim S
),
and hence there are
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-forms
\eta
such that

d\eta={\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2}){\zeta}_{S}
. We can choose
\eta
in the following way.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
U\subset S
be open. For
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(U)
there is a

({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-form
\eta(f_{1},f_{2})
on
U
such that

\mathrm{(a)}

d\eta(f_{1},f_{2})={\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})\,{\zeta}_{S}
,

\mathrm{(b)}

\eta(f_{1},f_{2})
is skew symmetric bilinear in

(f_{1},f_{2})
,

\mathrm{(c)}

\eta\bigl(L(h^{-1})f_{1},L(h^{-1})f_{2}\bigr)=\eta(f_{1},f_{2})\circ h
for all
h\in G
.

\mathrm{(d)}

If
\Delta f_{1}=\lambda f_{1}
and
\Delta f_{2}=\lambda f_{2}
for
the same eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, then
\eta(f_{1},f_{2})
is a closed

({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-form.

Proof.

We recall the Riemannian structure on
S
, which for a
given choice of coordinates
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathrm{d}})
is described by a

{\mathrm{d}}\times{\mathrm{d}}
-matrix
\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x)
, as discussed
in §
2.4
.

We check first, with (
2.38
), that for
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
and
for any
C^{\infty}
vector field
X
on
S
, we have

(6.6)

\mathrm{div}(fX)=f\,\mathrm{div}X+Xf\,,

and, further, that for
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(S)

(6.7)

(\grad f_{1})(f_{2})=\sum_{i,j}(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x)^{-1})_{i,j}(\partial_{x_{i}}f_{1})\,(\partial_{x_{j}}f_{2})

satisfies
(\grad f_{1})(f_{2})=(\grad f_{2})(f_{1})
. With

-\Delta F=\mathrm{div}\,\grad F
we get

(6.8)

{\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})=\mathrm{div}\Bigl(f_{2}\,\grad f_{1}-f_{1}\,\grad f_{2}\Bigr)\,.

We note that
f_{1}\,\grad f_{2}-f_{2}\,\grad f_{1}
is a vector field on
S
.
We can write it in the form
\sum_{j}a_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}
with

a_{j}\in C^{\infty}(S)
. The divergence looks like

(6.9)

\mathrm{div}\sum_{j}a_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}=(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{-1/2}\sum_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}\bigl((\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{1/2}a_{j}\bigr)\,.

With the differential forms of degree
{\mathrm{d}}-1
:

(6.10)

{\zeta}[j]=dx_{1}\wedge dx_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{dx_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{\mathrm{d}}

(
dx_{j}
omitted), we form for any vector

b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\mathrm{d}})\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\mathrm{d}}

(6.11)

\eta_{b}=\sum_{j}(-1)^{j+1}\,(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{1/2}b_{j}\,{\zeta}[j]\,.

The exterior derivative is

(6.12)

\displaystyle d\eta_{b}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}(-1)^{j+1}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\bigl((\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$})^{1/2}b_{j}\bigr)\,dx_{j}\wedge{\zeta}[j]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}\bigl((\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{1/2}b_{j}\bigr)\,dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{\mathrm{d}}

\displaystyle=(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{-1/2}\sum_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}\bigl((\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{1/2}b_{j}\bigr)\,{\zeta}_{S}\,.

Taking
b
with components

b_{j}=\sum_{i}(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x)^{-1})_{i,j}\Bigl(f_{2}\partial_{x_{i}}f_{1}-f_{1}\partial_{x_{i}}f_{2}\bigr)
we get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})\,{\zeta}_{S}

\displaystyle=(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{-1/2}\,\sum_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}\bigl((\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{1/2}b_{j}\bigr)\,{\zeta}_{S}

\displaystyle=d\Bigl(\sum_{j}(-1)^{j+1}(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{1/2}\,b_{j}{\zeta}[j]\Bigr)\,.

So we put

(6.13)

\eta(f_{1},f_{2})=\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{1+j}(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x)^{-1})_{i,j}\Bigl(f_{2}\,\partial_{x_{i}}f_{1}-f_{1}\,\partial_{x_{i}}f_{2}\Bigr)(\det\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{-1/2}{\zeta}[j]\,.

This satisfies assertions (

a

) and (

b

), and is
independent of the choice of the coordinates.

Let
h\in G
. Since
h
preserves the Riemannian metric, taking the
gradient commutes with left translation of functions on
S
:

\grad\bigl(L(h^{-1})f\bigr)=(\grad f)\circ h
. Furthermore

{\zeta}_{S}\circ h={\zeta}_{S}
. Hence

\eta(f_{1},f_{2})\circ h=\eta\bigl(L(h^{-1})f_{1},L(h^{-1})f_{2}\bigr)
.
This gives part (

c

). If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are eigenfunctions
of
\Delta
with the same eigenvalue, then
{\mathrm{MS}}(f_{1},f_{2})=0
. This gives
part (

d

).
∎

6.3.
Differential forms with values in the principal series

We combine the kernel function
r_{\nu}
in (
6.2
) and the
differential form
\eta
in (
6.13
) in

(6.14)

\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,\cdot)=\eta\bigl(f(\cdot),r_{\nu}(b;\cdot)\bigr)\,,

for
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
,
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
,
b\in\partial S
.
The dot in
f(\cdot)
and
r_{\nu}(b;\cdot)
refers to the variable in

S
used in (
6.13
) to get a
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-form on
S
. This
differential form depends on an additional variable
b
in
\partial S
.
The coefficient function of each
{\zeta}[j]
in this differential form is a

C^{\infty}
-function on
(\partial S)\times S
, which is analytic in the
variable
\xi
.

Now we assume that
f
satisfies
\Delta f=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})\,f
.
Proposition
6.2
(

d

) shows that
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is
a closed differential form. For
h\in G
,
b\in\partial S
and

x\in S
:

\displaystyle\bigl(\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f

\displaystyle;b,\cdot)\circ h^{-1}\bigr)(x)=\eta\bigl(L(h)f(\cdot),L(h)\,r_{\nu}(b;\cdot\bigr)(x)\bigr)

Prop.
6.2
(

c

)

\displaystyle=\eta\bigl(L(h)f(x),\bigl((\tau_{\nu}(h^{-1})r_{\nu}(\cdot;x)\bigr)(b)\bigr)

Prop
6.1
(

iii

)

\displaystyle=J(h,b)^{-\rho-\nu}\eta\bigl(L(h)f(x),r_{\nu}(h\,b;x)\bigr)

(
5.5
)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(h^{-1})\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(L(h)f;\cdot,x)\bigr)(b)\,.

This gives the following result:

Proposition 6.3
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
satisfies

\Delta f=(\rho^{2}-\penalty\nu^{2})f
, then the

\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
-valued differential form
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,x)
is
closed, and satisfies for
h\in G

(6.15)

\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,\cdot)\circ h^{-1}\;(x)=\bigl(\tau_{\nu}(h^{-1})\,\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}\bigl(L(h)f;\cdot,x\bigr)\bigr)(b)\,.

6.4.
The linear map from
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
to a
cohomology space

We will integrate the differential form
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,x)
in
(
6.14
) along
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cycles of the standard tessellation

{\mathcal{T}}
to obtain cocycles. The interpretation of the map to cohomology
leads us to define spaces of semi-analytic vectors in the spherical
principal series.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
,
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
,
b\in\partial S
,

C\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}
.

\mathrm{(i)}

The integral

I(f,C;b)=\int_{x\in C}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,x)

is absolutely convergent. For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
it satisfies

(6.16)

\int_{x\in\gamma^{-1}C}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;\cdot,x)=\tau_{\nu}(\gamma^{-1})\int_{x\in C}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;\cdot,x)\,.

\mathrm{(ii)}

The map
I(f,C;\cdot)\colon\partial S\to\mathbb{C}
is
in

\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)\cap\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S\smallsetminus(C\cap{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}))
.
In particular, if
C
does not contain any cusp,

I(f,C;\cdot)\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

The linear extension of
I(f,\cdot;b)
to the boundary

\partial X=\sum_{B\in B(X)}B
vanishes; i.e.,

\sum_{B\in B(X)}I(f,B;b)=\sum_{B\in B(X)}\int_{x\in B}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,x)=0\,.

Regarding Proposition
6.4
(

ii

), we remark
that
C\cap{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
contains at most one cusp. Thus,
I(f,C;\cdot)
is
analytic on
\partial S
except for at most one point.

Proof.

We use the description of tessellations in
Section
4
.

The integral
I(f,C;\xi)
has an analytic integrand, since the cusp
form
f
and the kernel function
r_{\nu}
are analytic. So integration
over a compact
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cell
C
is no problem, and the result is an
analytic function of the variable
\xi\in\partial S
.

We recall that
A^{+}_{T}=\bigl\{\mathrm{a}(t)\;:\;t\geq T\}
, and that in
Definition
4.3
we fixed the number
Y\geq 4
. If
C
contains
a cusp, then
C\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
has the form

C=g_{\mathsf{c}}DA^{+}_{T}\,\text{{{e}}}
, where
D
is a compact contractible subset
of
N
and
T\geq Y
. Here we use that
{\mathcal{T}}
satisfies the condition
in Remark
4.10
. In view of (
6.4
), and part
(

c

) in Proposition
6.2
we can transform the
cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
to
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
, and also work with the cusp form

x\mapsto f(g_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}\,x)
, which has quick decay at the cusp
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
.

We use a system of coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{{\mathrm{d}}-1})
on
N
given by
linear forms in the standard coordinates
(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
on
N
,
and moreover such that
x_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}=0
on
C
. With (
6.14
) and
(
6.13
)

(6.17)

\displaystyle\int_{x\in D}

\displaystyle\int_{y=T}^{\infty}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;b,x)=\int_{x\in D}\int_{y=T}^{\infty}(-1)^{\mathrm{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl((\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{-1})_{i,{\mathrm{d}}-1}

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\Bigl(r_{\nu}(b;x)\,\partial_{x_{i}}f(x)-f(x)\,\partial_{x_{i}}r_{\nu}(b;x)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\det(\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x))^{-1/2}\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,\ldots dx_{{\mathrm{d}}-2}\,dy\,.

Here
\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}(x)
describes the Riemannian structure in

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{{\mathrm{d}}-1},y)
as discussed in (
2.33
), with
y

in the role of
x_{\mathrm{d}}
. The cusp form
f
has quick decay at
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
.
Corollary
3.11
implies that the derivatives

\partial_{x_{i}}f
and
\partial_{y}f
have quick decay at
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
as
well.

Furthermore,
r_{\nu}(b;x)=\Phi_{\nu}(k_{b}^{-1}g_{x})
with the simple
function
\Phi_{\nu}
in (
6.1
),
k_{b}\in K
such that

k_{b}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=b
, and
g_{x}\in G
such that
g_{x}\,\text{{{e}}}=x
. The
polynomial growth of
\Phi_{\nu}
at the boundary implies that the
integral converges.

Let first
b\neq{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
. Then
k_{b}\in K
such that
k_{b}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=b

satisfies
k_{b}\not\in M
, and hence
k_{b}^{-1}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\in N\,\text{{{o}}}
. As

\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)
varies through
C
and
b
is in a compact
set of
\partial S\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
, then
r_{\nu}(b;x)
has at most
polynomial growth, and the corresponding estimate is uniform in
b
.
The function
r_{\nu}(\cdot;x)
is analytic, and hence the integral

I(f;C;b)
in (

i

) converges absolutely and is analytic in

b\in\partial S\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
.

If
b
varies through a neighborhood of
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
in
\partial S
, then
still
r_{\nu}(b;x)
is analytic in
(b,x)
jointly, and the same holds
for all derivatives with respect to coordinates of
\xi
, all with
polynomial growth at the boundary. This shows that the integral is
continuous in
b
on a neighborhood of
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
, and also that all its
derivatives of any order with respect to
b
exist and are continuous
on a neighborhood of
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
. So
I(f,C;b)
is in

C^{\infty}(\partial S)
. We do not have information on the growth of the
derivatives, and hence cannot show analyticity at
b={\boldsymbol{\infty}}
.

Assertion (

iii

) is no problem if
X
does not contain
cusps, since
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is a closed differential form, by
Proposition
6.3
. If
C
contains the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
, then we
cut off
X
at the horosphere
H_{\mathsf{c}}(Z)
for larger values of
Z
. That
adds
H_{\mathsf{c}}(Z)\cap X
as a boundary component. We use the estimates
discussed above to see that the contribution of this component tends to
zero as
Z
tends to
\infty
.

Relation (
6.16
) follows from the transformation behavior in
(
6.15
).
∎

Semi-analytic vectors in the spherical principal series.

Proposition
6.4
shows that to a given cusp form

f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
we can associate a cocycle
\psi^{f}
in the space

Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)\bigr)

by

(6.18)

\psi^{f}(X)=I(f,X;\cdot)\qquad\text{ for each }X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\,.

This cocycle has values in a submodule of
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
with
plenty of additional regularity:
\psi^{f}(X)\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(U)
for

U=(\partial S)\smallsetminus E
, where
E
is a set with at most one point.

For each finite set
E
of cusps of
\Gamma
we have the vector space

\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus E)
.
The action of
\Gamma
induces

(6.19)

\tau_{\nu}(\gamma):\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus E\bigr)\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus\gamma E\bigr)\,.

We need to work with transformations in
\Gamma
to send cusps to cusps. We
put

(6.20)

\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}=\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{E}}}\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus E\bigr)\,,

where
E
runs over the finite sets of cusps of
\Gamma
. The use of direct
limits ensures that we identify an analytic element

f\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left(\partial S\right)
with the restriction of
f
to a
set
\left(\partial S\right)\smallsetminus E
for each finite set
E
of
cusps. We call
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
the
\Gamma
-module of

semi-analytic vectors
in

\mathcal{V}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.
Here we use the submodule

(6.21)

\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}=\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}\cap\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)

of
smooth semi-analytic
vectors
. In this way,
\psi^{f}
determines a class in the space

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)
.

Recapitulation of principal series modules.
Let us list the
principal series modules introduced up till now:

•

The
G
-module
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
of
analytic
vectors
,

•

The
G
-module
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
of
smooth
vectors
,

•

The
\Gamma
-module
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
of
semi-analytic
vectors
,

•

The
\Gamma
-module
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}
of
smooth
semi-analytic vectors
.

(6.22)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.24977pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.24977pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.24979pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.56903pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.56903pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 122.88829pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-34.36893pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 49.36784pt\raise-34.36893pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

The module
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
is the space of global sections of
the sheaf
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
, and similarly for the smooth vectors. The
modules
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}
are extension of the
module
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
. We have not defined these modules as
sections of a sheaf.

Definition 6.5
.

If
f\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
, then we denote by
\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}(f)
(the set of

boundary singularities
of
f
) the
minimal set
E
of cusps such that

f\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus E\bigr)
.
So
\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}(f)=\emptyset
if and only if

f\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl(\partial S\bigr)
.

We say that a cocycle

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)
satisfies the

boundary condition

{\mathrm{bdc}}
if
for
C\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}

(6.23)

\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}\bigl(\psi(C)\bigr)\subset\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{ if
}C\subset S\,,\\
\{{\mathsf{c}}\}&\text{ if }C\cap{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}=\{{\mathsf{c}}\}\,.\end{cases}

By
Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
we denote the
set of cocycles satisfying condition
{\mathrm{bdc}}
, and by

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
the space of those cohomology
classes that have a representative in

Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
.

So
\psi^{f}\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
for
each cusp form
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
.

The above considerations lead to the first theorem relating cusp forms
to cohomology classes:

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. There is a linear map

(6.24)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}:{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{{\omega^{0},\infty}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}\,,

associating to
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
the cohomology class of
\psi^{f}
(in
(
6.18
)).

Remarks.

\mathrm{(1)}

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is the zero map for most
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. It might be
non-zero only if
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0
. (See Proposition

3.8
.)

\mathrm{(2)}

In Sections
8
–
12
we aim at

•

a characterization of the image
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
in
cohomological terms,

•

the determination of a set of
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
for which
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is
injective.

7.
The extension
\hat{S}
of
S

In the previous sections we dealt with
S
and its boundary

\partial S
, each with its own real-analytic structure. In the next
section we will need to go beyond the boundary
\partial S
in an
analytic way. The aim of this section is to view
S
and
\partial S

as embedded in a larger analytic manifold
\hat{S}
. We view this as a
generalization of the embedding
{\mathfrak{H}}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\!\mathbb{R}}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\!\mathbb{C}}
in
the case
{\mathrm{d}}=2
, for which
S={\mathfrak{H}}
and
\partial S=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\!\mathbb{R}}
.

With this embedding in place, functions on
S
may extend analytically
to a neighborhood of
S
in
\hat{S}
. Lemmas
7.4

and
7.5
give such extensions for some distance-related
functions.

7.1.
Construction of
\hat{S}

The space
S
is
isomorphic to
N\times A
as an analytic manifold. The corresponding
coordinates are the normalized horospherical coordinates

(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},y)
in
\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\times(0,\infty)
.

We define the space
S^{-}
as
\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\times(-\infty,0)
. It
is isomorphic to
S
as a manifold under the involution

(7.1)

j:(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},y)\leftrightarrow(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},-y)\,.

The action of
G
on
x\in S^{-}
is given by
g\,x=jgj\,x
. We put

\text{{{e}}}^{-}=j\,\text{{{e}}}
.

To make the set
\hat{S}=S\cup(\partial S)\cup S^{-}
into an analytic
manifold we cover it by the sets

(7.2)

\displaystyle\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}

\displaystyle=S\cup(N\,\text{{{o}}})\cup S^{-}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{w}S\cup(\mathrm{w}N\text{{{o}}})\cup\mathrm{w}S^{-}=S\cup\mathrm{w}N\mathrm{w}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\cup S^{-}\,,

forming an atlas.

We provide
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
with the standard analytic
structure of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
such that

(7.3)

\displaystyle\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},y)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}\text{
corresponds to }

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}&\text{ if }y>0\,,\\
\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\,\text{{{o}}}&\text{ if }y=0\,,\\
\bigl(j\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\mathrm{a}(y)j\bigr)\,\text{{{e}}}^{-}&\text{ if }y<0\,.\end{cases}

We call
\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},y)

extended horospherical coordinates
.

The transformation
\mathrm{w}
is an involution in
S
,
\partial S
and
S^{-}

that interchanges
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
and
o
. The set

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\infty\}\cong\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
has the analytic structure
determined by the extended horospherical coordinates

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{{\mathrm{d}}-1},y)
. The functions

x_{1}\circ\mathrm{w},\ldots,x_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\circ\mathrm{w},y\circ\mathrm{w}
are defined on

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\text{{{o}}}
. They determine an analytic structure on

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}
. Restriction of these analytic structures to
the intersection
\left(\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\infty\}\right)\cap\left(\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}\right)=\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\infty,\text{{{o}}}\}
gives two analytic structures on

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}},\text{{{o}}}\}
.

Lemma 7.1

(Relation between the analytic structures)
.

\mathrm{(i)}

On
\left(\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}\right)\cap\left(\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}\right)
both analytic structures are
isomorphic.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Both analytic structures induce the standard analytic
structure on
S
and on
S^{-}
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

The restrictions to
\partial S\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}

of the analytic structures of
\partial S=K/M
and of

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
coincide.

\mathrm{(iv)}

The restrictions to
\partial S\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}

of the analytic structures of
\partial S=K/M
and of

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}
coincide.

Proof.

The analytic structure on
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
is given in
(
7.3
) by the analytic structure on
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}\times\mathbb{R}
.
On
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}
we have this structure, transformed
by
\mathrm{w}=\mathrm{w}^{-1}
. We have to consider the effect of conjugation by

\mathrm{w}
on the structure on
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}},\text{{{o}}}\}
. We write

n=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
and
a=\mathrm{a}(y)
, and use

(7.4)

\mathrm{w}na\mathrm{w}\,\text{{{e}}}=\mathrm{w}n\mathrm{w}a^{-1}\,\text{{{e}}}=\mathbf{n}\bigl(\mathrm{w}n\mathrm{w}\bigr)\,\Bigl(\mathrm{a}\bigl(\mathbf{t}(\mathrm{w}n\mathrm{w})\bigr)\,a^{-1}\Bigr)\,\text{{{e}}}\,.

The element
\mathrm{w}n\mathrm{w}
depends analytically on
(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
,
and
a^{-1}=\mathrm{a}(y^{-1})
depends analytically on
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\neq 0}
.
This shows that the relation between both analytic structures on

S\cup\left(\partial S\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}},\text{{{o}}}\}\right)
is analytic. Use
the isomorphism
j
to get the same for

S^{-}\cup\left(\partial S\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}},\text{{{o}}}\}\right)
.
This gives (

i

).

Assertion (

ii

) is clear from the use of the extended
horospherical coordinates.

The analytic structure of
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\infty\}
determines the
analytic coordinates
(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
on
N\,\text{{{o}}}
. An analytic
map to
K/M
sends
n=\mathrm{n}(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})
to
\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(n\mathrm{w})
. For the
inverse relation we take
k\in K\smallsetminus M
. Then
k
is in the big
cell
N\mathrm{w}MAN
of the Bruhat decomposition. So
k\,\infty
is sent
to an element of
N\mathrm{w}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=N\,\text{{{o}}}
. In this way we obtain
(

iii

), and analogously (

iv

) as well.
∎

The analytic manifold
\hat{S}
is the one-point-compactification of

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}\cong\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. We call it the

extension
of
S
by reflection. On

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{e}}},\text{{{e}}}^{-}\}
we have
extended polar coordinates

(b,\tau)\in K/M\times(-1,1)
corresponding to

(7.5)

\begin{cases}k_{b}\mathrm{a}(1/\tau)\,\text{{{e}}}&\qquad\text{for }\ \tau\in(0,1)\,,\\
k_{b}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}&\qquad\text{for }\ \tau=0\,,\\
jk_{b}\mathrm{a}(-1/\tau)j\,\text{{{e}}}^{-}&\qquad\text{for }\ \tau\in(-1,0)\,,\end{cases}

with
k_{b}\in K
such that
k_{b}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=b
.

Lemma 7.2
.

The extended polar coordinates are compatible
with the analytic structure of
\hat{S}
.

Proof.

For
\tau=0
the extended polar coordinates give the
standard analytic structure on
\partial S
, which is also the
structure induced by the analytic structure of
\hat{S}
according to
(

iii

) and (

iv

) in Lemma
7.1
.

For
\tau>0
we have the analytic map

(b,\tau)\mapsto(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},y)
determined by

(7.6)

\mathrm{n}\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)}\bigr)=\mathbf{n}\bigl(k_{b}\mathrm{a}(1/\tau)\bigr)\,,\qquad y=\mathbf{t}\bigl(k_{b}\mathrm{a}(1/\tau)\bigr)\,.

The inverse relation is determined by

\text{{{e}}}\neq\mathrm{n}\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)}\bigr)\,\mathrm{a}(y)k=k_{1}\mathrm{a}(1/\tau)

with
\tau\in(0,1)
and
k_{1}\in K
determined up to

k_{1}\mapsto k_{1}m
, with
m\in M
, by the Cartan decomposition.
Application of
j:S\leftrightarrow S^{-}
gives the correspondence on

S^{-}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{e}}}^{-}\}
.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

The space
\hat{S}=S\cup(\partial S)\cup S^{-}
with the
analytic structure indicated above is a compact analytic manifold. The
group
G
acts on it by analytic transformations.

The compact manifold
\hat{S}
can be realized as a sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}

provided with an analytic action of the group
G
.

Proof.

After Lemmas
7.1
and
7.2
only the group
action is left to be checked.

The action of
G
on
S=G/K
is analytic with respect to the standard
analytic structure of the homogeneous space; this structure determines
the analytic structure of
\hat{S}
. The action of
G
on
S^{-}
is
derived from the analytic action on
S
, since the transformation
j

is analytic.

On
\partial S=K/M\cong G/MAN
the action is analytic for the
structure as homogeneous space. This standard structure determines the
analytic structure of
\hat{S}
. We still have to check that these
actions on the three subsets are the restriction of one analytic action
on
\hat{S}
. The action of
NA
on
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
is
clearly analytic, in particular on
(\partial S)\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
.

Let
V
be an open neighborhood of
n_{0}\,\text{{{o}}}
,
n_{0}\in N
, in

\hat{S}
. For
k
in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
e
in
K
,
the action on points of
V
is determined by

kna\,\text{{{e}}}=\mathbf{n}(kna)\mathrm{a}(\mathbf{t}(kna))\,\text{{{e}}}
, which is analytic on

V
. Together, this shows that locally on
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}

the action of
g
is analytic for
g
in a sufficiently small
neighborhood of
e
in
G
.

The action of
N^{-}A=\mathrm{w}NA\mathrm{w}^{-1}
is analytic on

\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{\text{{{o}}}\}
. The action of
k
near
e
in
K
is
analytic near points
n_{0}^{-}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
with
n_{0}^{-}\in N^{-}
analogously to
the previous case.

All together, this shows that the action of
g\in G
is analytic at any
point
z\in\hat{S}
for
g
in a small neighborhood, which depends on
the point
z
. Since
G
is connected, this implies analyticity of the
action of
G
on
\hat{S}
.
∎

7.2.
Distance function

The length of a differentiable curve

p:[t_{1},t_{2}]\rightarrow S
is given by the integral in (
2.34
).
The Riemannian distance
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,x)
is the minimal value of the length
of all paths from
z
to
x
. This minimal length is attained for a
geodesic segment, which can be described as the curve of the form

p(s)=g\mathrm{a}(e^{s})\,\text{{{e}}}
,
0\leq s\leq s_{1}
, with
z=g\,\text{{{e}}}
and

x=g\mathrm{a}(e^{s_{1}})
. This leads to the formula
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,x)=s_{1}
, or
equivalently

(7.7)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(g\,\text{{{e}}},\,g\,\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=\max(y,y^{-1})\qquad(g\in G,\;y>0)\,,

with
y=e^{s_{1}}
. See also (
2.35
). The distance tends to

\infty
as
y
tends to
0
or to
\infty
. The distance function

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}
makes
S
into a metric space, and

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(g\,z,\,g\,x)={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,x)
for all
x,z\in S
. Moreover,

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}
is an analytic function on

\bigl\{(z,x)\in S\times S\;:\;x\neq z\bigr\}
.

The following lemmas extend the function
z\mapsto-\log{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,x)

into
\hat{S}
, and use the involution
j
in (
7.1
).

Lemma 7.4
.

There is a real-valued analytic function

{\delta_{S}}
on

\bigl\{(x,z)\;:\;x\in S,\;z\in\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{x,jx\}\bigr\}
such
that if
z
is in the smaller set
S\smallsetminus\{x\}
, then

(7.8)

{\delta_{S}}(x,z)=e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,z)}\,.

For fixed
x\in S
the function
z\mapsto{\delta_{S}}(x,z)
has a simple zero
along
\partial S
and is non-zero elsewhere.

Proof.

For given
(x,z)
in the prescribed domain take first

p\in NA
such that
p\,x=\text{{{e}}}
. The element
p
depends
analytically on
x
. The action of
K
leaves
p\,x=\text{{{e}}}
fixed, and
can be used to bring
kp\,z
on the line with normalized
horospherical coordinates
(0,0,y)
with
-1<y<1
. The element

p\in NA
depends analytically on
x
, and the element
k
depends
analytically on
p\,x
and
z
. So the value of
y
in
(-1,1)

depends analytically on
(x,z)
; we take
{\delta_{S}}(x,z)=y
. We recall
that in the definition of
\hat{S}
the coordinate function
y
, which
is positive on
S
, is zero along
N\,\text{{{o}}}\subset\partial S

and is negative on
S^{-}
. The value
{\delta_{S}}(x,z)
is positive for

z\in S\smallsetminus\{x\}
, where
{\delta_{S}}(x,z)=e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,z)}
, and
negative for
z\in S^{-}
. The zero along
\partial S
is a simple zero,
since letting
z=k\mathrm{a}(1/\tau)
move through
\partial S
gives
y

as a function of
\tau
with positive derivative at
\tau=0
.

If
z\in S
, then
y\in(0,1)
, and

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,z)={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(kp\,x,\,kp\,z)={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},(0,0,y))=-\log y
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in S
. The function

(x_{1},x_{2},z)\,\mapsto\,e^{{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{1},z)-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{2},z)}

defined for
z\in S\smallsetminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
, has values in the interval

\bigl[e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{1},x_{2})},e^{{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{1},x_{2})}\bigr]
, and has a
positive analytic extension to the region

\bigl\{(x_{1},x_{2},z)\;:\;x_{1},x_{2}\in S,\;z\in\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{x_{1},x_{2},jx_{1},jx_{2}\}\bigr\}\,.

Proof.

The triangle inequality implies the bounds.

The quotient
{\delta_{S}}(x_{2},z)/{\delta_{S}}(x_{1},z)
of analytic functions is
analytic, except on the zero set of the denominator. The functions

z\mapsto{\delta_{S}}(x_{1},z)
and
z\mapsto{\delta_{S}}(x_{2},z)
both have a
simple zero along
\partial S
. These zeros cancel each other in the
quotient.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in S
be fixed. For each
k\in K

we write
x_{j}=kn_{j}\mathrm{a}(t_{j})
with
t_{j}>0
and
n_{j}\in N
. Then

(7.9)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{1},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{2},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=\log t_{2}-\log t_{1}+{\mathrm{O}}_{n_{1},n_{2}}(t^{-1})\quad\text{ as }t\uparrow\infty.

(The implicit constants in the estimate depend on
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
.)
Moreover, with
z(t)=k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
and

z_{j}(t)=kn_{j}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
for
j=1,2
, we have

(7.10)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x_{1},z(t)\bigr)-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{2},z(t)\bigr)-\log(t_{2}/t_{1})\begin{cases}\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{1}(t),z(t)\bigr)\,,\\
\geq-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{2}(t),z(t)\bigr)\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

The three points
z_{1}(t)
,
z_{2}(t)
and
z(t)
as defined
in the lemma tend to
k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
as
t\uparrow\infty
. The invariance of
the distance function allows us to consider only
k=e
. Then we have
the situation sketched in Figure
7.1
.

Figure 7.1.
Sketch illustrating the proof of Lemma
7.6
.

The vertical axis is the
y
-axis in horospherical coordinates. The
horizontal axis represents
N\,\text{{{o}}}
.

For
j=1,2
we have

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{j},z_{j}(t)\bigr)\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{j},z(t)\bigr)\,.

To see this we note that
x_{j}
and
z_{j}(t)
are on the same geodesic
half-line to
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
. With (
2.35
) we have

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{j},z_{j}(t)\bigr)=\log\bigl(t/t_{j}\bigr)\,.

The geodesic segment from
x_{j}
to
z(t)
has the form

\tau\mapsto\mathrm{n}(x(\tau))\mathrm{a}(\tau)\,\text{{{e}}}
with some function

\tau\mapsto x(\tau)
. Its length is given by

\int_{\tau=t_{j}}^{t}\sqrt{Q(x(\tau),\tau)\,(x^{\prime}(\tau),1)+\tau^{-2}}\,d\tau\geq\log\bigl(t/t_{j}\bigr)\,,

since
Q(x,\tau)
is positive definite; see (
2.33
).

Together with the triangle inequality we obtain:

(7.11)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{j},z_{j}(t)\bigr)\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{j},z(t)\bigr)\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{j},z_{j}(t)\bigr)+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{j}(t),z(t)\bigr)\,.

We have for
t\geq\max(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{1},z(t)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{1}(t_{1}),z_{1}(t)\bigr)+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{1}(t),z(t)\bigr)=\log(t/t_{1})+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{1}(t),z(t)\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{2},z(t)\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z_{2}(t_{2}),z_{2}(t)\bigr)-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z(t),z_{2}(t)\bigr)\geq\log(t/t_{2})\,.

Then

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{1},z(t)\bigr)-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{2},z(t)\bigr)\leq\log(t_{2}/t_{1})+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z_{1}(t),z(t)\bigr)

gives the first inequality in (
7.10
). Application of the same
approach to the difference

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{2},x(z)\bigr)-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(x_{1},z(t)\bigr)
gives the other
inequality in (
7.10
).

Formula (
2.32
) expressing the Riemannian structure in normalized
horospherical coordinates implies that

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z_{j}(t),z(t))={\mathrm{O}}(t^{-1})
for large values of
t
, with an
implicit constant depending on
n_{j}
.
∎

8.
Boundary germs

Theorem
6.6
has shown that there are maps from spaces of cusp
forms to cohomology groups of dimension
{\mathrm{d}}-1
with values in modules
of analytic functions on
\partial S
with finitely many singularities.
To get hold of the images of the spaces of cusp forms it has turned out
to be useful to work with modules of analytic boundary germs.

The definition and study of these modules will take a number of
sections, leading to a reformulation of Theorem
6.6
in
Section
10
.

8.1.
Sheaves of boundary germs

For open

U\subset S
we put

(8.1)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(U)=\bigl\{f\in C^{\infty}(U)\;:\;\Delta f=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})\,f\bigr\}\,.

This defines
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}
as a sheaf on
S
.

Let
I
be an open subset of
\partial S
.
The space of
boundary germs

{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(I)
on
I
is the direct limit

(8.2)

{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(I)=\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{U}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}\bigl(U\cap S)\,,

where
U
runs through the open neighborhoods of
I
in
\hat{S}
. So

{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}
is a sheaf on
\partial S
.

The action
L
of
g\in G
, given by
L(g)f(x)=f(g^{-1}x)
, sends
functions
f
on
I
to functions
L(g)f
on
g\,I
, and if
U
is a
neighborhood of
I
in
\hat{S}
, then functions
f
on
U\cap S
are
sent to functions
L(g)f
on the neighborhood
g\,U
of
g\,I

in
\hat{S}
. This induces a bijective linear map

g^{\ast}:{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(I)\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(g\,I)
. This defines
{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}
as a

G
-equivariant sheaf. In particular,
L
is a representation of
G

in
{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(\partial S)
.

All elements of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
represent a unique element of

{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(\partial S)
. The embedding

{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(\partial S)
is compatible with the action
of
G
.

Analytic boundary germs.
By an analytic boundary germ we mean a boundary germ that “extends
across
\partial S
analytically” in the following way:

Definition 8.1
.

Let
I
be an open subset of
\partial S
. The
space

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)

of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs on
I
consists of the boundary
germs in
{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(I)
that are represented on
U\cap S
, for some
neighborhood
U
of
I
in
\hat{S}
, by a function
u
of the form

(8.3)

u(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=t^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,A_{u}(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\qquad(k\in K,\;t>1\text{ large})\,,

where
A_{u}
is an analytic function on
U
.

We note that if
U
is connected, the function
A_{u}
on
U
is uniquely
determined by
u
. Not every analytic function on some neighborhood
U

of
\partial S
in
\hat{S}
can occur as a function
A_{u}
. The fact
that
\left(\Delta-\rho^{2}+\nu^{2}\right)u=0
on
U\cap S
imposes a
partial differential equation for
A_{u}
on
U\cap S
with analytic
coefficients that extend to
U
if
U
is connected. The extended
differential operator is not elliptic on
U\cap\partial S
. The
condition that
A_{u}
is analytic is an additional condition, which most
elements of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\!\nu}(I)
do not satisfy.

Suppose that the function
u\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=t^{-(\nu+\rho)}\,A_{u}(k\mathrm{a}(t)\text{{{e}}})
represents an analytic boundary germ. Using that

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}=k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
, we obtain the
value

A_{u}(k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}t^{\nu+\rho}u(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})

at
k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\in\partial S
.
If
\re\nu>-\rho
the representatives
u
of
\nu
-analytic boundary
germs are small near the boundary
\partial S
.

Definition
8.1
is based on polar coordinates. It is useful to
have a formulation in horospherical coordinates as well.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
I
be an open subset of
N\,\text{{{o}}}\subset\partial S
.
Let
U
be a neighborhood of
I
in
\hat{S}\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
. Then

u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(U\cap S)
represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary germ in

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)
if and only if there is an analytic function

B_{u}
on
U
such that

(8.4)

u\bigl(n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=y^{\rho+\nu}\,B_{u}\bigl(n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\qquad n\in N,\;n\,\text{{{o}}}\in I,y\in(0,\varepsilon)

for some
\varepsilon>0
.

There is a neighborhood
U_{1}\subset U
of
I
in
\hat{S}
such that the
functions
A_{u}
and
B_{u}
are related by

(8.5)

B_{u}(z)=D_{S,\nu}(z)\,A_{u}(z)\,,

where the function
D_{S,\nu}
is non-zero and analytic. On
U_{1}\cap S
it is
given by

(8.6)

D_{S,\nu}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\bigr)=e^{(\rho+\nu)\bigl(-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\text{{{e}}})+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\mathbf{n}(k\mathrm{a}(t))\,\text{{{e}}},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\bigr)}\,.

We note that
t\uparrow\infty
in (
8.3
), whereas
y\downarrow 0

in (
8.4
).

Proof.

Let
n\in N
and
k\in K
vary subject to the condition
that
n\,\text{{{o}}}=k\,\infty
.

As
y\downarrow 0
, the point
n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
approaches
n\,\text{{{o}}}
,
and as
t\uparrow{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
the point
k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
approaches the same
point
n\,\text{{{o}}}
.

Let us compute
u
at
k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
in two ways. By (
8.3
)

\displaystyle u\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=t^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,A_{u}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=e^{{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})}\,.

We use (
8.4
) and Proposition
2.1
to write

k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}=n_{1}\mathrm{a}(y_{1})\,\text{{{e}}}
, with
n_{1}=\mathbf{n}(k\mathrm{a}(t))
and

y_{1}=\mathbf{t}(k\mathrm{a}(t))
, to obtain

\displaystyle u\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=u\bigl(n_{1}\mathrm{a}(y_{1})\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=y_{1}^{\rho+\nu}\,B_{u}\bigl(n_{1}\mathrm{a}(y_{1})\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=y_{1}^{\rho+\nu}\,B_{u}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{a}(y_{1})\,\text{{{e}}})}=e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(k^{-1}n_{1}\,\text{{{e}}},\,k^{-1}n_{1}\mathrm{a}(y_{1})\,\text{{{e}}})}=e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(k^{-1}n_{1}\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})}\,.

We consider the quotient of the factors:

\displaystyle t^{-(\rho+\nu)}\bigm/y_{1}^{\rho+\nu}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(e^{-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(k^{-1}n_{1}\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})}\Bigr)^{\rho+\nu}

(8.7)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{{\delta_{S}}(\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})}{{\delta_{S}}(k^{-1}n_{1}\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})}\Bigr)^{\rho+\nu}\,.

Lemma
7.5
shows that this function is analytic in

k^{-1}n_{1}\,\text{{{e}}}
and in
\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
, where
n_{1}
depends on
t

and
k
, and
k^{-1}n_{1}\,\text{{{e}}}
stays in
S
. Hence the factor extends
analytically as a function on a neighborhood of
U\cap\partial S
in

\hat{S}
. We denote this extension by
D_{S,\nu}
. The function
B_{u}
extends
analytically as well. Conversely, if we know that
B_{u}
extends to
U
,
then
A_{u}
extends analytically.
∎

Example.
The function

(8.8)

u\bigl(n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=\Phi_{\nu}(n\mathrm{a}(y)k)={y}^{\rho+\nu}

in (
6.1
) (with
y\downarrow 0
) represents a
\nu
-analytic
boundary germ on
N\,\text{{{o}}}
, but not on
\partial S
.
Near points of
N\,\text{{{o}}}
we have
B_{u}=1
, which clearly extends
analytically to
\hat{S}
. Near
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
we get for
k\in M
and
t>1

A_{u}(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=t^{\rho+\nu}u(\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=t^{2(\rho+\nu)}\,.

Action of
G
.
Let
u
represent an element of

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)
for
I\subset\partial S
.
For each
g\in G
the function
u_{1}=L(g^{-1})u:x\mapsto u(g\,x)

represents an element of
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(g^{-1}I)
.

Proposition 8.3
.

(Action of
G
)
The action
L
on the
representatives
u
of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs corresponds to

\tilde{\tau}_{\nu}
on the corresponding analytic functions
A_{u}
with

(8.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}_{\nu}(g^{-1})A_{u}(z)

\displaystyle=\tilde{J}(g,z)^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,A_{u}(g\,z)\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{\nu}(g,z)

\displaystyle={\delta_{S}}(g^{-1}\,\text{{{e}}},\,z)\bigm/{\delta_{S}}(\text{{{e}}},\,z)\,.

The value
\tilde{J}(g,b)
for
b\in\partial S
is equal to the value

J(g,b)
of the factor of automorphy in the realization
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
of
the principal series in §
5.2
.

Proof.

For
g\in G
, let
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(U\cap S)
represent an
analytic boundary germ, with
A_{u}
analytic on
U
, and denote

u_{1}=L(g^{-1})u
. If
u_{1}
would represent a
\nu
-analytic boundary
germ as well, then the corresponding function
A_{u_{1}}
would be given
for sufficiently large
t
by

(8.10)

\displaystyle A_{u_{1}}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=t^{(\rho+\nu)}u\bigl(gk\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=t^{\rho+\nu}u\bigl(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1})\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=t^{\rho+\nu}t_{1}^{-\rho-\nu}A_{u}\bigl(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1})\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=(t_{1}/t)^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,A_{u}\bigl(gk\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\,,

with
k_{1}\in K
and
t_{1}>1
such that

gk\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}=k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1})\,\text{{{e}}}
(Cartan decomposition). We
have

\log t_{1}={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t_{1}))={\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(g^{-1}\,\text{{{e}}},k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\,.

Lemmas
7.4
and
7.5
show that

t_{1}/t=e^{{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(g^{-1}\,\text{{{e}}},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\text{{{e}}})-{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\text{{{e}}},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})}={\delta_{S}}(\text{{{e}}},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\bigm/{\delta_{S}}(g^{-1}\,\text{{{e}}},\,k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})

extends analytically across
\partial S
. Since
A_{u}
is analytic on

U
, we conclude that
A_{L(g^{-1})u}
is analytic on a neighborhood
of
g^{-1}U\cap\partial S
.

We put
\tilde{\tau}_{\nu}(g^{-1})A_{u}=A_{L(g^{-1})u}
. That leads to the
formula in (
8.9
) for the factor of automorphy
\tilde{J}
.

The description as a quotient of analytic functions that are zero on

\partial S
is not handy to compute
\tilde{J}(g,b)
for

b\in\partial S
.
Instead, we use the analyticity of all quantities involved, to obtain
for
b\in\partial S\cap U

(8.11)

\tilde{J}(g,b)^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}A_{u}\bigl(p_{2}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}A_{u_{1}}\bigl(p_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

with
p_{1},p_{2}\in G
such that
p_{1}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=b
and
p_{2}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=g\,b
. We
can take
p_{1}=k_{1}\in K
. Then
p_{2}=gk_{1}
. We use

A_{u_{1}}(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=t^{\rho+\nu}\,u_{1}(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=t^{\rho+\nu}\,u(gk_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\,,

We write
gk_{1}=k_{2}n_{2}\mathrm{a}(t_{2})
with
k_{2}\in K
,
n_{2}\in N
and

t_{2}>0
, as a variant of Proposition
2.1
. Then

\displaystyle u(gk_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=u\bigl(k_{2}n_{2}\mathrm{a}(t_{2}t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=(t_{2}t)^{-\rho-\nu}\,A_{u}\bigl(k_{2}n_{2}\mathrm{a}(t_{2}t),\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=(t_{2}t)^{-\rho-\nu}A_{u}\bigl(gk_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\,.

So

A_{u_{1}}\bigl(p_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=(t_{2})^{-\rho-\nu}\,A_{u}(gk_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\,.

Taking the limit in (
8.11
) we find

\tilde{J}(g,b)^{-\rho-\nu}\,A_{u}(g\,b)=t_{2}^{-\rho-\nu}A_{u}(g\,b)\,.

Hence
\tilde{J}(g,b)=t_{2}
. We have

k_{2}n_{2}\mathrm{a}(t_{2})=k_{2}\mathrm{a}(t_{2})\,\bigl(\mathrm{a}(t_{2})^{-1}n_{2}\mathrm{a}(t_{2})\bigr)
,
hence
\tilde{J}(g,b)=\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}(gk_{1})
, which is just the value of
J(g,b)

in (
5.3
).
∎

8.2.
The restriction morphism

The sheaf of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
turns out to be
isomorphic to the sheaf
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
of analytic functions
on
\partial S
, for general values of the spectral parameter. This
implies that the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in Theorem
6.6
from cusp
forms to
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}
-valued cohomology classes can be
formulated in terms of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs. We will use this
formulation to get in §
10
an inverse of
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
.

Definition 8.4
.

For open subsets
I\subset\partial S
the
restriction map

\rho_{\nu}:\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(I)

sends the boundary germ represented by
u
to the analytic function

A_{u}
restricted to
\partial S
.

See Figure
8.1
.

Figure 8.1.
Domains of
u
,
A_{u}
and
\varphi
where
\varphi
is the image under

\rho_{\nu}
of the
\nu
-analytic boundary germ represented by
u
. The
thick line corresponds to an open part of
\partial S
.

The representative
u
is defined on a region in
S
near the boundary

\partial S
. The function

A_{u}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=t^{\rho+\nu}u\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})

extends analytically into
\hat{S}
across part of
\partial S
. The
restriction of
A_{u}
to
\partial S
is an analytic function
\varphi
on
an open subset of
\partial S
.

Theorem 8.5
.

The restriction map
\rho_{\nu}
determines a
morphism
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
of
G
-equivariant sheaves,
with the action of
G
on
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
given by
L
on representatives,
and the action
\tau_{\nu}
of
G
on
\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
in (
5.5
).

If
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, then
\rho_{\nu}
is an
isomorphism of
G
-equivariant sheaves.

This result generalizes
[
3
, Theorem 5.6]
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\rho_{\nu}
gives a morphism of

G
-equivariant sheaves. It will take the rest of this section to
establish the isomorphism
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\cong\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
.

Morphism of
G
-equivariant sheaves.
For
g\in G
, the
operator
L(g)
on
u
corresponds to
\tilde{\tau}_{\nu}(g)
on
A_{u}
,
by Proposition
8.3
. This proposition also implies that

\tilde{\tau}_{\nu}(g)
acts as
\tau_{\nu}(g)
on the restriction to

\partial S
.

Reduction of the statement to an isomorphism of stalks at one
point.
The local structure of sheaves implies that morphisms of sheaves
are isomorphisms if the induced morphisms of the stalks at all points
of
\partial S
are isomorphisms. The action of
G
induces
isomorphisms of stalks at different points of
\partial S
.
Hence it suffices to prove that
\rho_{\nu}
induces an isomorphism

\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)_{x}\rightarrow\bigl(\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)_{x}
for one
point
x\in\partial S
. We choose
x=\text{{{o}}}
, for which we will use
Proposition
8.2
.

Subsubsection
2.5.1
gives a description of the Laplace
operator in normalized horospherical coordinates. A computation shows
that
\Delta u=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})u
leads for
u=y^{\rho+\nu}B_{u}
to the
relation

(8.12)

\Bigl(y\,\partial_{y}^{2}+(2\nu+1)\partial_{y}+y\,L_{1}+y^{3}\,L_{2}\Bigr)B_{u}=0\,.

This relation extends to the domain of
B_{u}
by analyticity. We recall
that the operators
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are second order differential
operators in the
x_{j}
without constant term, with coefficients that
have at most total degree
2
in the
x_{j}
. Actually, consultation of
(
2.39
), (
2.37
), and (
2.41
) shows that

L_{2}=\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}
.

Injectivity.
Suppose that
U
is an open neighborhood of
o
in

\hat{S}
, and that
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(U)
represents a
\nu
-analytic
boundary germ. Then
B_{u}
has a power series expansion that is
absolutely convergent on
[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]^{\mathrm{d}}
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. We
write this expansion as

(8.13)

B_{u}(x,y)=\sum_{m\geq 0}a_{m}(x)y^{m}\,,

where
x
denotes
(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{{\mathrm{d}}-1})
.
Insertion into the differential equation

\Delta B_{u}-(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})B_{u}=0
yields

(8.14)

m(m+2\nu)a_{m}+L_{1}a_{m-2}+L_{2}a_{m-4}=0\,,

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. It is practical to take
a_{m}=0
for
m<0

to make the relation valid for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Since we assume that
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, we can use this
relation as a recursion relation. The relation does not impose
conditions on
a_{0}
, and shows that
a_{1}=0
. Recursively this implies
that
a_{m}=0
for all odd
m
, and that (
8.13
) takes the form

(8.15)

B_{u}(x,y)=\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{2k}(x)\,y^{2k}\,.

Since
a_{0}(x)=B_{u}(x,0)
, the function
a_{0}
is equal to the
restriction
\varphi=\rho_{\nu}u
. We can use the relations to express each

a_{m}
in terms of
\varphi
and its derivatives. In this way we have shown
the injectivity of the map

(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{\text{{{o}}}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{\text{{{o}}}}
, which implies the
injectivity of
\rho_{\nu}
as a morphism of sheaves.

Surjectivity.
We start with
a_{0}(x)=\varphi(x)
, analytic on the
region
B_{\varepsilon}
indicated above. The recursion relation

(8.16)

a_{2k}=\frac{-1}{4k(k+\nu)}\bigl(L_{1}a_{2(k-1)}+L_{2}a_{2(k-2)}\bigr)

describes all
a_{2k}
in terms of
\varphi
and its derivatives with
respect to
x_{1},\ldots,x_{p+q}
. Then we have a formal series

(8.17)

F=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{2k}(x)\,y^{2k},

which satisfies the differential equation for elements
B_{u}
. The
question is whether the series
F
converges absolutely on some
neighborhood of
o
in
\hat{S}
. If that is the case, then
F
can
be taken as an analytic function
B_{u}
such that
u=y^{\rho+\nu}B_{u}

represents a boundary germ with restriction
\varphi
.

This reduces our task to showing that if
\varphi
is analytic on
B_{\varepsilon}
,
then the series in (
8.17
) converges absolutely on some
neighborhood of
\text{{{o}}}\in\hat{S}
.

We use multi-indices in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{p+q}
, with

\partial^{\alpha}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\partial_{x_{p+q}}^{\alpha_{p+q}}
,

x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{p+q}^{\alpha_{p+q}}
. Further

|\alpha|:=\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}\alpha_{j}
,

\alpha!:=\alpha_{1}!\;\alpha_{2}!\;\cdots\alpha_{p+q}!
and

\max(\alpha)=\max(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p+q}\bigr)
.

The description in (
2.37
) and (
2.41
) shows that

L_{2}=\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+q}\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}
. The operator
L_{2}
is a
linear combination of operators

\partial_{x_{i}}\circ p_{i,k}(x)\circ\partial_{x_{k}}
with

i,k\in\{1,\cdots,p+q\}
. The polynomials have degree at most
2
in

x
, with coefficients determined by the linear forms
c_{j,i}
in
§
2.4
. We rewrite

\partial_{x_{i}}\circ p_{i,k}(x)\circ\partial_{x_{k}}=p_{i,k}(x)\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{\partial p_{i,k}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\partial_{k}
.
In total, the operator
L_{1}
is a linear combination of finitely many
operators of the form
x^{\tau}\partial^{\sigma}
, with multi-indices
\sigma
and

\tau
satisfying
|\sigma|\leq 2
,
|\tau|\leq 2
.

The recursion relation (
8.16
) can be used to write all
coefficients
a_{2k}
as linear combinations of basis functions

x^{\beta}\partial^{\alpha}\varphi
, with
\alpha
and
\beta
running through the
multi-indices. It turned out to be helpful to give another form of the
formal series
F
, of which we want to show analyticity on a
neighborhood of
\nu
in
\hat{S}
. We define the linear operator
\mathcal{L}

by prescribing it on the basis functions.

(8.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bigl({y}^{2h}\,x^{\beta}\,\partial^{\alpha}\varphi\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{-y^{2h+2}}{4(h+1)(h+1+\nu)}L_{1}\bigl(x^{\beta}\,\partial^{\alpha}\varphi\bigr)

\displaystyle\hbox{}+\frac{-y^{2h+4}}{4(h+2)(h+2+\nu)}L_{2}\bigl(x^{\beta}\,\partial^{\alpha}\varphi\bigr)\,.

Lemma 8.6
.

The formal sum
F
in (
8.17
) is equal to
the formal sum

(8.19)

H=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi\,,

and for each
m\geq 0
the function
a_{2m}
is equal to the (finite)
sum of all terms in
H
of degree
2m
in
y
.

Proof.

The second statement implies that
F
is equal to

\sum_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi
. We show it by recursion. The statement
holds for
m=0
, since each application of
\mathcal{L}
increases the degree
in
y
.

Let
S(n)
denote the sum of the terms in
H
of degree
2n
in
y
. By
(
8.16
)

(8.20)

a_{n}=\frac{-1}{4n(n+\nu)}\Bigl(\sum_{f\in S(n-1)}L_{1}f+\sum_{g\in S(n-2)}L_{2}g\Bigr)\,.

We use that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are differential operators in
x
, and that
they do not change the dependence on
y
. If
f\in S(n-1)
then

\mathcal{L}f=\frac{-y^{2n}}{4n(n+\nu)}L_{1}\bigl(f/y^{2n-2}\bigr)+\frac{-y^{2n+2}}{4(n+1)(n+1+\nu)}L_{2}\bigl(f/y^{2n-2}\bigr)\,.

The first of these two terms has degree
2n
in
y
, and the second one
has degree
2n+2
. In an analogous way, we obtain that for

g\in S(n-2)
the function
\mathcal{L}g
is the sum of two terms, only one of
which has degree
2n
. Hence the right-hand side of (
8.20
) gives
the part of degree
2n
in
y
of

\mathcal{L}\Bigl(\sum_{f\in S(n-1)}f+\sum_{S(n-2)}g\Bigr)\,.

Since all terms in
H
of degree
2n
in
y
arise from applying
\mathcal{L}

to terms of degree
2n-2
or degree
2n-4
, this shows by induction the
second assertion in the lemma.
∎

This lemma shows that for the surjectivity it suffices to show the
convergence of the formal sum in (
8.19
). We start with a
holomorphic function
\varphi
on

B_{\varepsilon}=\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{C}^{p+q}\;:\;|x_{i}|\leq\varepsilon\text{ for }1\leq i\leq p+q\bigr\}

for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. We want to find
\delta\in(0,\varepsilon]
,
\delta<1
, and

\eta\in(0,1)
such that
H
converges absolutely for

\bigl(x,y\bigr)
in
B_{\delta}\times[-\eta,\eta]
.

For
{\zeta}>0
and for holomorphic functions
f
on
B_{\zeta}
we put

(8.21)

\|f\|_{\zeta}=\sup_{x\in B_{\zeta}}|f(x)|\,.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
\varphi
is analytic on
B_{\varepsilon}
, then for

\delta\in(0,\varepsilon)

(8.22)

\|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi\|_{\delta}\leq\alpha!\,\varepsilon^{-|\alpha|}\,(1-\delta/\varepsilon)^{-|\alpha|-p-q}\,\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}\,.

Proof.

We have an expansion

\varphi(x)=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
. Then

|c_{\alpha}|\leq\varepsilon^{-|\alpha|}\,\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{p+q}

\displaystyle\partial^{\beta}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\geq\beta}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha-\beta}\frac{\alpha!}{(\alpha-\beta)!}=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}c_{\alpha+\beta}x^{\alpha}\frac{(\alpha+\beta)!}{\alpha!}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\varepsilon^{-|\alpha+\beta|}\delta^{|\alpha|}\frac{(\alpha+\beta)!}{\alpha!}\,\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-|\beta|}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{p+q}\sum_{\alpha_{j}\geq 0}\Bigl(\frac{\delta}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\alpha_{j}}\frac{(\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j})!}{\alpha_{j}!}\Bigr)\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}\,.

We have used that
\bigl|x^{\alpha}|\leq\delta^{|\alpha|}
. For each factor in
the product we use that

\sum_{k\geq 0}(\delta/\varepsilon)^{k}(k+b)!/k!=(1-\delta/\varepsilon)^{-1-b}\,b!\,.

Then

\displaystyle\partial^{\beta}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\ll\varepsilon^{-|\beta|}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{p+q}\bigl(1-\delta/\varepsilon)^{-1-\beta_{j}}\,\beta_{j}!\Bigr)\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-|\beta|}\bigl(1-\delta/\varepsilon\bigr)^{-|\beta|-p-q}\beta!\,\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}\,.\qed

We use this lemma to compute
\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi
. First we consider the action of

\mathcal{L}
on one basis function

f_{n-1}=t^{2h(n-1)}\,x^{\beta(n-1)}\,\partial^{\alpha(n-1)}\varphi
, with

h(n-1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and multi-indices
\alpha(n-1)
and
\beta(n-1)

in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{p+q}
. The image
\mathcal{L}f_{n-1}
is a linear
combination of many basis functions, arising from the many simple
differential operators
c_{\tau,\sigma}x^{\tau}\partial^{\sigma}
involved in

L_{1}
and
L_{2}
. That gives a finite number of terms, each of which we
can write as
c_{n}\,t^{2h(n)}\,x^{\beta(n)}\,\partial^{\alpha(n)}\varphi

with
h(n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\alpha(n),\beta(n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{p+q}
.
We fix
C
such that
|c_{\tau,\sigma}|\leq C
for all
(\sigma,\tau)
. The
total number of terms is bounded by a large number
N
obtained by
considering the number of terms
n_{\sigma,\tau}
arising for each

(\sigma,\tau)
. We use the abbreviations
{\bf a}(n)=|\alpha(n)|
,

{\bf b}(n)=|\beta(n)|
.

Lemma 8.8
.

In these notations:

(8.23)

\displaystyle h(n-1)+2

\displaystyle\geq h(n)\geq h(n-1)+1\,,

(8.24)

\displaystyle{\bf a}(n-1)

\displaystyle\leq{\bf a}(n)\leq{\bf a}(n-1)+2\,,

(8.25)

\displaystyle{\bf b}(n)

\displaystyle\leq{\bf b}(n-1)+2\,,\qquad{\bf b}(n)\leq{\bf a}(n)\,,

(8.26)

\displaystyle|c_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C{\zeta}_{\nu}\,d_{n}}{4n\,\bigl|\nu+n\bigr|}\,,

where

(8.27)

{\zeta}_{\nu}=\max\Bigl\{\frac{|\nu+1+m|}{|\nu+1+l|}\;:\;0\leq m\leq l\leq 2n\Bigr\}\,,

and

(8.28)

|d_{n}|\leq\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }{\bf a}(n)={\bf a}(n-1)+2\,,\\
\max(\beta(n-1))&\text{ if }{\bf a}(n)={\bf a}(n-1)+1\,,\\
\max(\beta(n-1))^{2}&\text{ if }{\bf a}(n)={\bf a}(n-1)\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

Definition (
8.18
) implies that

n\leq h(n)\leq 2n
. The positive quantity
{\zeta}_{\nu}
allows us to
estimate
\frac{1}{|\nu+n|}{\bigl|\nu+1+h(n-1)\bigr|}
by

\frac{{\zeta}_{\nu}}{n}
. We recall that we work under the assumption that

\nu\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

The differential operator
c_{\tau,\sigma}x^{\tau}\partial^{\sigma}
with

|\sigma|,|\tau|\leq 2
, gives the factor
C
in (
8.26
), and
increases
|\alpha|
and
|\beta|
by at most
2
. If
|\beta(n)|>0
, a
differentiation with respect to some
x_{i}
occurs. Then
|\alpha|

increases less,
|\beta|
decreases, and the differentiation gives a
factor estimated by
2d_{n}
.
∎

The function
\mathcal{L}^{k}\varphi
can be computed by making
k
choices of
operators
f_{j}
of the form
c_{\sigma,\tau}x^{\tau}\partial^{\sigma}
for

j=1,\ldots,k
. That gives at most
N^{k}
terms

f_{k}f_{k-1}\cdots f_{1}\varphi
of the form

(8.29)

g=D\,t^{2h(k)}\,x^{\beta(k)}\,\partial^{\alpha(k)}\varphi\,.

We aim at an estimate that is valid for all the terms arising in this
way. We have
h(n)\geq n
by (
8.23
), and get

(8.30)

D\leq\Bigl(\frac{C{\zeta}_{\nu}}{2}\Bigr)^{k}\frac{\prod_{n=1}^{k}d_{n}}{k!\;\bigl|(\nu+1)_{k}\bigr|}\,,

with the increasing Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{n}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(z+j)
.

Let
e_{1}
be the number of steps for which
{\bf a}(n)={\bf a}(n-1)+1

and
e_{2}
be the number of steps for which
{\bf a}(n)={\bf a}(n-1)
.
Since
{\bf b}(n)\leq{\bf a}(n)
by (
8.25
) we have

\max(\beta)\leq\max(\alpha)
. Furthermore,
\max(\alpha)\leq|\alpha|
. We have

{\bf a}(k)=2k-e_{1}-2e_{2}
by (
8.24
) and (
8.28
).

(8.31)

\prod_{n=1}^{k}d_{n}\leq|{\bf a}(k)|^{2e_{2}+e_{1}}\leq({\bf a}(k)+1)\cdots(2k)=\frac{(2k)!}{{\bf a}(k)!}\,.

We obtain with Lemma
8.7

\displaystyle\bigl\|g\bigr\|_{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq D\,|t|^{2h(k)}\,|x^{\beta}|\,\bigl\|\,\partial^{\alpha(k)}\varphi\bigr\|_{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(C{\zeta}_{\nu}/2)^{k}}{k!\,|(\nu+1)_{k}|}\frac{(2k)!}{{\bf a}(k)!}\eta^{2h(k)}\,\|x\|^{{\bf b}(k)}\,\alpha(k)!\,\varepsilon^{-{\bf a(k)}}(1-\delta/\varepsilon)^{-{\bf a}(k)-p-q}\,\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}\,.

By Stirling’s formula we have

\frac{(2k)!}{k!\;(\nu+1)_{k}}\ll 2^{2k}\,k^{-1/2-\re\nu}
. The
influence of
\nu
is in the implicit constant. We have

\frac{\alpha(k)!}{{\bf a}!}\leq 1
, and
\|x\|^{{\bf b}(k)}\ll 1
since

0<\delta<1
. Using
{\bf a}(k)\leq 2k
,
h(k)\leq 2k
, and

|t|\leq\eta
, we obtain

\|g\|_{\delta}\ll|t|^{2k}\Bigl(2C{\zeta}_{\nu}\varepsilon^{2}(1-\delta/\varepsilon)^{2}\Bigr)^{k}\,k^{-1/2-\re\nu}\,(1-\delta/\varepsilon)^{-p-q}\,\|\varphi\|_{\varepsilon}\,.

The estimates that we carried out were wasteful for a single term.
However they are uniform over the
{\mathrm{O}}(N^{k})
choices

f_{k}f_{k-1}\cdots f_{1}
of operators. With

0<\eta<\bigl(2NC{\zeta}_{\nu}(\varepsilon-\delta)^{2}\bigr)^{-1/2}
the norms

\|\mathcal{L}^{k}\varphi\|_{\delta}
have linear exponential decay in
k
on the
product
[-\delta,\delta]^{p+q}\times[-\eta,\eta]
, which gives absolute
convergence of the series
F
in (
8.17
), and hence analyticity.

This concludes the proof of the surjectivity of the restriction map

\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)_{\text{{{o}}}}\rightarrow\bigl(\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)_{\text{{{o}}}}
, and
ends the proof of Theorem
8.5
.
∎

9.
Cohomology with values in boundary germs

We proceed under the assumption that
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

The isomorphism of
G
-equivariant sheaves

\rho_{\nu}:\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
in Theorem
8.5

provides us with isomorphisms between
G
-modules of analytic functions
on the boundary
\partial S
with modules of
\nu
-analytic boundary
germs, on the condition
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

The module
\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
in (
6.20
) is isomorphic under

\rho_{\nu}
to

(9.1)

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}=\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{E}}}\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\bigl((\partial S)\smallsetminus E\bigr)\,,

where
E
runs over the finite sets of cusps. The submodule

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}=\rho_{\nu}^{-1}\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}
has no
independent description in the context of

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
.

Theorem
6.6
yields, under the condition

\nu\not\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, a linear map

(9.2)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}:{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

by composing
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
with the natural map corresponding to

\rho_{\nu}^{-1}
.

In this section we will see that
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
can be described directly by
integrals.

9.1.
Free space resolvent

We use results from the paper
[
34
]
by Miatello and Wallach:

Proposition 9.1
.

(
K
-bi-invariant functions)

There is a family
\mathbb{C}\ni\nu\mapsto Q_{\nu}
of functions on

G\smallsetminus K
such that

\mathrm{(1)}

Q_{\nu}
is holomorphic on
\re\nu>-\frac{1}{2}
, with
a meromorphic extension to
\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,

\mathrm{(2)}

Q_{\nu}(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)k_{2})=Q_{\nu}(\mathrm{a}(t))
for all
t>1
,

\mathrm{(3)}

R({\mathsf{Cas}})Q_{\nu}=-(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})Q_{\nu}
,

\mathrm{(4)}

The function
k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\mapsto Q_{\nu}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\bigr)

represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary germ with restriction equal to the
constant function
1
on
\partial S
.

\mathrm{(5)}

There is
d_{0}>0
such that

Q_{\nu}(z)={\mathrm{O}}\bigl({\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,\text{{{e}}})^{-d_{0}}\bigr)
as

z\rightarrow\text{{{e}}}
. If
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
, then we can take
d_{0}={\mathrm{d}}-2
.

We put
q_{\nu}(x_{1};x_{2})=Q_{\nu}(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2})
for

(x_{1},x_{2})\in S^{2}\smallsetminus\diag
,
g_{j}\in G
such that

x_{j}=g_{j}\,\text{{{e}}}
.

\mathrm{(6)}

q_{\nu}(gx_{1};gx_{2})=q_{\nu}(x_{1};x_{2})
for all
g\in G
,

\mathrm{(7)}

q_{\nu}(x_{2};x_{1})=q_{\nu}(x_{1};x_{2})
,

\mathrm{(8)}

q_{\nu}(\cdot,x_{2})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S\smallsetminus\{x_{2}\})

represents an element of
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
,

\mathrm{(9)}

\Delta q_{\nu}(\cdot,x_{2})=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})\,q_{\nu}(\cdot;x_{2})

and
\Delta q_{\nu}(x_{1};\cdot)=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})\,q_{\nu}(x_{1};\cdot)
,

\mathrm{(10)}

if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S)
, then

(9.3)

\int_{S}q_{\nu}(x;y)\,\bigl((\Delta-\rho^{2}+\nu^{2})f\bigr)(y)\;d\mu(y)=2\nu c(\nu)f(x)\,,

where
c
is the
c
-function of Harish-Chandra.

In the case of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
the function
Q_{\nu}
is given explicitly
as
Q_{\nu+1/2,0}
in
[
4
, (1.3b)]
.

Theorem 1.1 in
[
34
]
gives (

1

)–(

3

),
except the restriction of the singularities to
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.
See also
[
35
, Theorem 2.2]
and
[
16
, Proposition 3.2]
.
The relation in
[
34
, Theorem 1.1, b)]
with the spherical
function reduces the position of the singularities to zeros and
singularities of the Harish-Chandra
c
-function. Theorem 1.2 in

[
34
]
implies part (

4

). Part (d) in Theorem 1.1
implies a more precise result than (

5

). In fact we can take

d_{0}={\mathrm{d}}-2
if
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
, and any
d_{0}>0
if
{\mathrm{d}}=2
.

The resolvent relation (

10

) follows from
[
34
, Lemma
2.2]
. Actually, this result of Miatello and Wallach is valid
for more general elements of
C^{\infty}(S)
that satisfy a growth
condition at the boundary. For compactly supported functions we need
only local integrability, which is given by
[
34
, Lemma 2.1]

for all values of
\nu
at which
Q_{\nu}
is defined.

The
c
-function of
Harish-Chandra is given in
[
23
, (3.7)]
. For rank one we get

(9.4)

c(\nu)=\frac{2^{\rho-\nu}\,\Gamma\bigl((p+q+1)/2\bigr)\,\Gamma(\nu)}{\Gamma\bigl((p/2+1+\nu)/2\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl((p/2+q+\nu)/2\bigr)}\,,

with
p
and
q
as in (
2.9
). We see that it has a first order
singularity at
\nu=0
, and further it has zeros and poles in points of

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. The factor
\nu\,c(\nu)
in (
9.3
) in
the proposition is holomorphic and non-zero for

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. It seems sensible to
impose the further restriction
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

We form the
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-form

(9.5)

\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;x,\cdot)=\eta(f(.),q_{\nu}(x,\cdot)\bigr)\,,

on
S
depending on
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
and
