x\in S
, analogously to the
definition of
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in (
6.14
). See
Proposition
6.2
for
\eta(\cdot,\cdot)
. Like in
(
6.15
) we have for each
g\in G
:

(9.6)

\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;x,\cdot)\circ g^{-1}=\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f|g^{-1};gx,\cdot)\qquad(g\in G)\,.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, and let
U\subset S

be open. Let
D\subset U
be a compact manifold with a contractible
interior
\mathring{D}
, and with a piecewise smooth boundary

\partial D
.
Let
\partial D
be oriented according to the outward normal.

For each
h\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(U)
and
x\in S\smallsetminus D

(9.7)

\int_{\partial D}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(h;x,\cdot)=\begin{cases}2\nu\,c(\nu)\,h(x)&\text{ if }x\in\mathring{D}\,,\\
0&\text{ if }x\in S\smallsetminus D\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S)
. With (

9

) in
Proposition
9.1
and (
6.5
) we obtain, on all of

S\smallsetminus\{x\}
,

\displaystyle q_{\nu}(x,\cdot)\,(\Delta-\rho^{2}+\nu^{2})f=q_{\nu}(x,\cdot)\,\Delta f-f\,\Delta q_{\nu}(x,\cdot)=-{\mathrm{MS}}\bigl(f,q_{\nu}(x,\cdot)\bigr)\,.

Hence (
9.3
) takes the form

(9.8)

\displaystyle 2\nu\,c(\nu)\,f(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{y\in S}q_{\nu}(x,y)\,\bigl((\Delta-\rho^{2}+\nu^{2})f\bigr)(y)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{y,\;\varepsilon\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(y,x)\leq R}q_{\nu}(x,y)\,\bigl((\Delta-\rho^{2}+\nu^{2})f\bigr)(y)\,d\mu(y)\,,

for each
R
such that the support of
f
is contained in
\bigl\{y\in S\;:\;{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(y,x)<R\bigr\}
. Part (

a

) of
Proposition
6.2
allows us to formulate this as

(9.9)

\displaystyle 2\nu\,c(\nu)\,f(x)

\displaystyle=-I(\nu;x,R;f)+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}I(\nu;x,\varepsilon;f)\,,

\displaystyle I(\nu;x,r;f)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in H(x,r)}\eta(f,q_{\nu}(x,\cdot))\,,

with the
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-dimensional hypersurface
H(x,r)=\bigl\{y\in S\;:\;{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(y,x)=r\bigr\}
oriented according to the normal directed
away from
x
. See Figure
9.1
a). The hypersurface
H(x,R)

is outside the support of
f
. Hence

(9.10)

2\nu\,c(\nu)\,f(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}I(\nu;x,\varepsilon;f)\,.

Figure 9.1.
Illustrations for the proof of
Proposition
9.2
.

Under the assumptions in the proposition we take
f
equal to
h
on a
neighborhood
V
of
D
such that
V\subset U
, and extend it to an
element
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(S)
. Then

(\Delta f)(x)=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})f(x)

for all
x\in V
. Now the differential form
\eta(f,q_{\nu}(x,\cdot))

is closed on
V\smallsetminus\{x\}
.

If
x\in\mathring{D}
the integral
I(\nu;x,\varepsilon;f)
does not depend on

\varepsilon
, as long as
H(x,\varepsilon)
stays inside
D
.
\bigl(
See
Figure
9.1
b).
\bigl)
Deforming
H(x,\varepsilon)
into
\partial D

gives one part of the assertion, taking into account the minus sign
in (
9.8
). If
x
is outside
D
(case c) in the figure), then
the integral
\int_{D}\eta\bigl(f,q_{\nu}(x,\cdot)\bigr)

is zero, since then the differential form is closed on
D
.∎

9.2.
Cohomology with values in analytic boundary
germs

Theorem
8.5
implies that if

\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, then the linear map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
can
be lifted to a map from cusp forms to a cohomology space with values in
boundary germs. Here we give an integral transformation describing this
map, under the same assumption
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. The restriction morphism

\rho_{\nu}:\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
induces the following
relations:

\mathrm{(i)}

The kernel functions
r_{\nu}
in (
6.2
) and

q_{\nu}
in Proposition
9.1
satisfy

(9.11)

\rho_{\nu}q_{\nu}(\cdot;x)=r_{\nu}(\cdot;x)\qquad(x\in S)\,.

\mathrm{(ii)}

The differential forms
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;\cdot,x)
in
(
6.14
) and
\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;\cdot,x)
in (
9.5
) satisfy

(9.12)

\bigl(\rho_{\nu}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;\cdot,x)\bigr)(y)=\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}(f;y,x)\,.

Proof.

For part (

i

) we check in the definitions that for
t\geq 1

r_{\nu}({\boldsymbol{\infty}};\text{{{e}}})=\Phi_{0,\nu}(k^{-1})=1\,,\qquad q_{\nu}(\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\text{{{e}}})\;\sim\;t^{\rho+\nu}\,.

Hence
\rho_{\nu}q_{\nu}(\cdot,\text{{{e}}})({\boldsymbol{\infty}})=r_{\nu}(\infty;\text{{{e}}})
.

Each point
(b,x)\in\left(\partial S\right)\times S

can be written as
(g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}},g\,\text{{{e}}})
, by taking
g=kan\in KAN
:
first choose
k\in K
such that
k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}=b
, and then choose

an\in AN
such that
an\,\text{{{e}}}=k^{-1}\,x
. To complete the proof of
part (

i

) we use Theorem
8.5
and (
6.4
):

(9.13)

\displaystyle q_{\nu}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigl(|g\times|g\bigr)(\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=q_{\nu}(g\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},g\,\text{{{e}}})\,,

\displaystyle\bigl(q_{\nu}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigr)\bigl(|_{\nu}g\times|g\bigr)({\boldsymbol{\infty}},\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=\bigl((\rho_{\nu}^{-1}r_{\nu})(\cdot;g\,\text{{{e}}})\bigr)(g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})\,,

\displaystyle r_{\nu}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigl(|_{\nu}g\times|g)({\boldsymbol{\infty}},\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=r_{\nu}(g\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}};g\,\text{{{e}}})\,.

Part (

ii

) considers
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-forms in the
variable
x\in S
, with values in
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left(\partial S\right)
and

\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left(\partial S\right)
,
respectively. The restriction map involves only the parameter
y
.
∎

A consequence of this result is an explicit description of the map from
cusp forms to cocycles. The image of this map has even a more precise
description.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. The linear map

(9.14)

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}:{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

assigns to
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
the cohomology class of the cocycle
determined by

(9.15)

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\ni X\mapsto I_{X}(f;y)=\int_{x\in X}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;y,x)\,.

\mathrm{(i)}

The integral
I_{X}(f;y)
is well-defined for

y\in S\smallsetminus X
and satisfies

\mathrm{(a)}

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma

(9.16)

\int_{x\in\gamma^{-1}X}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;y,x)=\int_{x\in X}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;\gamma y,x)\,.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
X
contains no cusps (equivalently, if

X\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
), then
b\mapsto I_{X}(f;b)
represents an
element of
\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left(\partial S\right)
. If

X\cap{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}=\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
, then
b\mapsto I_{X}(f;b)
is in

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty}_{\!\!\nu}
and represents an element of

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left(\left(\partial S\right)\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}\}\right)
.

\mathrm{(c)}

For all
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
the integral is
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S\smallsetminus X)
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mathcal{T}}
have boundary

\partial Z=\sum_{X\in B(Z)}\varepsilon_{X}\,X
with
\varepsilon_{X}\in\{1,-1\}

oriented by the outward pointing normals. Then the function

(9.17)

y\mapsto\sum_{X\in B(Z)}\int_{x\in\varepsilon_{X}X}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(f;y,x)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }x\in S\smallsetminus Z\,,\\
2\nu\,c(\nu)\,f(x)&\text{ if }x\in\mathring{Z}\,.\end{cases}

on
S\smallsetminus\partial Z
represents the zero
\nu
-analytic boundary
germ. By
\mathring{Z}
we denote the interior of
Z
.

Proof.

Cells
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
are compact, and the integration over

X
poses no convergence problems. By Proposition
9.1

(

8

) the integrand represents an analytic boundary germ.
It is defined on
S\smallsetminus X
. If the cell
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}

contains a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
, then the convergence has to be checked with a
limit procedure, like in the discussion leading up to
Proposition
6.4
. Then the integral represents a

\nu
-analytic boundary germ on

\left(\partial S\right)\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
.
This gives part (i)(b). Part (i)(c) is clear, and part (i)(a) follows
from (
9.6
).

Part (

ii

) follows directly from Proposition
9.2

if
Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
. If
Y
contains a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
we first
apply Proposition
9.2
to a truncated version of
Y
, and
then use a limit procedure based on the quick decay at
{\mathsf{c}}
of
f
and
its derivatives.
∎

For
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells
X
containing a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
the integral in
(
9.15
) represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary germ on

(\partial S)\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
. This implies that for

k\,\infty\neq{\mathsf{c}}
the integral
h=I_{X}(f;\cdot)
satisfies

h\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})={\mathrm{O}}(t^{-\rho-\nu})
as
t\uparrow\infty
. We
need also to understand how
h(z)
behaves as
z\rightarrow{\mathsf{c}}

through
S\smallsetminus X
.

We use the norm on
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
given by

\|\xi\|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}\xi_{j}^{2}
, and recall
that
A_{Y}^{+}=\bigl\{\mathrm{a}(t)\;:\;t\geq Y\bigr\}
.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
\re\nu>-\rho
, and
suppose that
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
contains the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
. Then
there is a
({\mathrm{d}}-2)
-dimensional compact subset
C\subset N
such that

X\cap S=g_{\mathsf{c}}CA_{Y}^{+}
. For
\varepsilon>0
let

D_{\varepsilon}=\bigl\{\mathrm{n}(\xi)\;:\;\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q},\;\|\xi-x\|<\varepsilon
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}

such that
\mathrm{n}(x)\in C\bigr\}
.

For each cusp form
f\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
the integral
h=I_{X}(f;\cdot)
in
(
9.15
) satisfies the following bounds.

\mathrm{(a)}

The function
h
is bounded on

S\smallsetminus g_{\mathsf{c}}D_{\varepsilon}A^{+}_{a}
for each
\varepsilon>0
and for each

a\in(0,Y)
.

\mathrm{(b)}

h(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})={\mathrm{O}}\bigl(t^{-\rho-\nu}\bigr)
as

t\uparrow\infty
, uniformly for
n
in compact sets contained in

N\smallsetminus C

We note that if the cusp form
f
is non-zero, then
Proposition
3.8
implies that
\nu
satisfies the
stronger condition
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0
. It is worthwhile to keep
\nu

as general as we can. Then we can use Theorem
A
to conclude
that certain cohomology spaces are zero if
\nu
does not satisfy

\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0
.

The proof takes the remainder of this subsection.

Transformation to
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
and notation for coordinates.

Without loss of generality we can work with the cusp
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
. Then
X

has in horospherical coordinates the description
C\times[Y,\infty)

for some compact part
C
of a
({\mathrm{d}}-2)
-dimensional subvariety of
N
.
We use
w=\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
for the integration variable in

I_{X}(f,\cdot)
.
For
h
we use
h\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})
.

The kernel function.
The function
q_{\nu}
(introduced in
Proposition
9.1
)
satisfies

(9.18)

q_{\nu}(g_{1}\,\text{{{e}}},g_{2}\,\text{{{e}}})=Q_{\nu}(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2})\,,

and depends only on
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(g_{1}\,\text{{{e}}},g_{2}\text{{{e}}})
. The function

Q_{\nu}\bigl(k_{1}\mathrm{a}(t)k_{2}\bigr)
is equal to
t^{-\rho-\nu}
times an
analytic function of
t^{-2}
on a neighborhood of

\frac{1}{t}\in(-1,1)
. If the distance is large, then

(9.19)

\displaystyle q_{\nu}\bigl(z;w\bigr)

\displaystyle=e^{-(\rho+\nu){\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)}\,A_{\nu}\bigl(e^{-2{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=e^{-(\rho+\nu){\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z,w\bigr)}\Bigl(1+{\mathrm{O}}(e^{-2{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)})\Bigr)\qquad\text{as }{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)\rightarrow\infty\,,

with an analytic function
A_{\nu}
on a neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{C}
,
that satisfies
A_{\nu}(0)=1
.

As
t\downarrow 1
the function
Q_{\nu}(\mathrm{a}(t))
is
(t-1)^{2-d}
times
an analytic function in
t-1
. So the kernel function
q_{\nu}
can be
estimated by
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)^{1-c}
with
c={\mathrm{d}}-1
. In the case
{\mathrm{d}}=2

there is also a logarithmic term in the expansion as
t\downarrow 0
.
Then we can use any positive value of
c
in the exponent.

First derivative of the kernel function.
In the integral
representation

(9.20)

h(z)=\int_{w\in X}\eta\bigl(f(w),q_{\nu}(z,w\bigr)\bigr)

not only the function
q_{\nu}(z,w)
occurs, but also its first
derivatives with respect to
w
. See (
6.13
).

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
and
y>0
, and let
z\neq\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
. Then

(9.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\ll y^{-1}+\bigl(1+\|x^{(1)}\|\bigr)y^{-2}

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq p\,,

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{x_{j}}{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq y^{-2}

\displaystyle j\geq p+1\,,

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{y}{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(z,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq y^{-1}\,.

Proof.

Let
z\neq\text{{{e}}}
. The tangent vectors
\bigl(\partial_{x_{j}}\bigr)_{\text{{{e}}}}
and

\bigl(\partial_{y}\bigr)_{\text{{{e}}}}
at
e

form an orthonormal basis of

\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
. Let
v_{z}\in\mathrm{T}_{\!\text{{{e}}}}S
be the unit vector pointing to

z
. Then

\partial_{y}{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\bigr|_{x=0,y=1}=-\hbox{$g^{(R)}$}\bigl(v_{z},(\partial_{y})_{\text{{{e}}}}\bigr)\in[-1,1]\,.

The same holds for
(\partial_{x_{j}}\bigr)_{\text{{{e}}}}
.

For general
z\neq\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
the derivatives
m_{j}
in
(
2.27
) of the distance function have also values in
[-1,1]
. With
the inverse relations in (
2.28
) we arrive at the estimates in the
lemma.
∎

General approach to the estimations.
In the integral

(9.22)

h(z)=\int_{(n,y)\in C\times[Y,\infty)}\eta\bigl(f(n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}),q_{\nu}(z,n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\bigr)

the integrand is linear in
f
and its first derivatives in
horospherical coordinates, and also linear in
q_{\nu}(z,\cdot)
and its
first derivatives. Like in the proof of Proposition
6.4

we use a bound
C_{f,N}\,y^{-N}
for the influence
of
f
for each large
N
. To keep track of uniformity of estimates we
do not use
{\mathrm{O}}(\cdot)
and
\ll
, but indicate the constants
explicitly.

For each
z\in S\smallsetminus X
there exists

w_{0}=w_{0}(z)\in X
such that

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w_{0})\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)
for all
w\in X
. We
put

(9.23)

B(z,y)=C_{B}\cdot\begin{cases}\sup_{n\in C}e^{-(\rho+\nu){\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})}&\text{ if }{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w_{0})\geq 1\,,\\
\sup_{n\in C}{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})^{-c}&\text{ if }{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w_{0})<1\,,\end{cases}

with
c={\mathrm{d}}-1
if
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
, and
c>1
if
{\mathrm{d}}=2
. (The choice of
c

takes into account the first order derivatives of the kernel function.)
The constant
C_{B}
takes into account the implicit
constants in the estimates of the kernel function, and depends on

\re\nu
, on the volume of
C
, and on constants in the application
of (
6.13
). In this way we can estimate the integral
in (
9.22
) by

(9.24)

\bigl|h(z)\bigr|\leq C_{f,N}Y^{-N}\int_{y=Y}^{\infty}B(z,y)\,dy\,.

The factor
Y^{-N}
arises from the influence of the function
f
.

On the distance function.
The triangle inequality implies that

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\leq{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)+{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\,.

The first term on the right is easy. With (
2.35
)

(9.25)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=\bigl|\log(y/t)\bigr|\,.

The ‘horizontal distance’ in the second term is the length of a path
that we do not know precisely. This path can be parametrized as

p(\tau)=\mathrm{n}\bigl(\Xi(\tau)\bigr)\mathrm{a}\bigl(\eta(\tau)\bigr)\,\text{{{e}}}

where
\Xi(\tau)=x+\tau(\xi-x)
, and where
\eta
is a
C^{\infty}

function
[0,1]\rightarrow(0,\infty)
that satisfies

\eta(0)=\eta(1)=y
. The length of this path is given by the
integral in (
2.34
), which with help of (
2.32
) can be
written as

(9.26)

\int_{\tau=0}^{1}\sqrt{\frac{\|\xi^{(1)}-x^{(1)}\|^{2}}{\eta(\tau)^{2}}+\frac{Q(\Xi(\tau)^{(1)})[\xi-x]}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}+\frac{\eta^{\prime}(\tau)^{2}}{\eta(\tau)^{2}}}\,d\tau\,.

We denote by
Q(x)[\cdot]

the positive definite quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
with
p\times q

block matrix

(9.27)

\begin{pmatrix}C(x^{(1)})C(x^{(1)})^{t}&-C(x^{(1)})\\
-C(x^{(1)})^{t}&I_{q}\end{pmatrix}\,.

The matrix elements are at most quadratic in the coordinates of

x^{(1)}
. If
q=0
, then the middle term under the square root
in (
9.26
) vanishes.

The integral in (
9.26
) leads directly to the following bounds.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\xi,x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
,
y\geq 0
,
then

(9.28)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}},\,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(y^{-1}+y^{-2}b_{l}(\xi,x)\bigr)\bigl\|\xi-x\bigr\|\,,

\displaystyle\geq\bigl(\eta_{m}^{-1}+\eta_{m}^{-2}\,b_{s}(\xi,x)\bigr)\bigl\|\xi-x\bigr\|\,,

where

(9.29)

\displaystyle b_{l}(\xi,x)

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq\tau\leq 1}\sup_{\xi\neq 0}\frac{\bigl|Q(\Xi(\tau)^{(1)})[\xi-x]\bigr|}{\|\xi-x\|}\,,

\displaystyle b_{s}(\xi,x)

\displaystyle=\min_{0\leq\tau\leq 1}\inf_{\xi\neq 0}\frac{\bigl|Q(\Xi(\tau)^{(1)})[\xi-x]\bigr|}{\|\xi-x\|}\,,

and where
\eta_{m}=\max_{0\leq\tau\leq 1}\eta(\tau)
for the
function
\eta
describing the geodesic segment between

\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
and
\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
.

Lemma 9.8
.

With the notations introduced above:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)

\displaystyle\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\max\Bigl(\bigl|\log(y/t)\bigr|,2\log(\eta_{m}/y),\,\|\xi-x\|\,\bigl(\eta_{m}^{-1}+b_{s}(\xi,x)\eta_{m}^{-2}\bigr)\Bigr)\,.

Proof.

The triangle inequality implies that

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)
is
larger than
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigl(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=\bigl|\log(y/t)\bigr|
and also larger than

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}\bigr(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y),\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)
. In the shortest path
realizing the latter distance, the function
\eta
goes up from
y
to

\eta_{m}
and then down again to
y
. Working only with the last term
under the square root in (
9.26
) we get the second lower bound.
Lemma
9.7
gives the third lower bound.
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

We use the notations discussed above. Let

w=\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
with
y\geq Y
, and let

z=\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}
.

\mathrm{(a)}

If

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\leq 1
, then

(9.30)

e^{-1}t\leq y\leq et\quad\text{ and}\quad\eta_{m}\geq\|\xi-x\|\,(1+e^{-1/2}b_{s}(\xi,x)/y\bigr)\,.

\mathrm{(b)}

If the conditions in (
9.30
) hold, then

(9.31)

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\geq e^{-1/2}\|\xi-x\|y^{-1}\,.

Proof.

The assumption that the distance is at most
1
implies by
Lemma
9.8

e^{-1}t\leq y\leq et\,,\quad\eta_{m}\leq e^{1/2}y\,,\quad\eta_{m}\geq\|\xi-x\|\bigl(1+b_{s}(\xi,x)/\eta_{m}\bigr)\,.

This implies
directly the first two statements in (
9.30
). Furthermore, with

\eta_{m}\leq e^{1/2}y
we get

\eta_{m}\geq\|\xi-x\|\bigl(1+b_{s}(\xi,x)/\eta_{m}\bigr)\geq\|\xi-x\|\,\bigl(1+e^{-1/2}b_{s}(\xi,x)y^{-1}\bigr)\,.

This implies (
9.30
).

With Lemma
9.8

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}},\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\geq\|\xi-x\|\eta_{m}\,.

If the conditions in (
9.30
) hold, then we proceed with

\geq\|\xi-x\|(e^{1/2}y)^{-1}\,.\qed

Proof of part (

a

) of
Proposition
9.5
.

Let
z=\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\in D_{\varepsilon}
for
a given
\varepsilon>0
, with
D_{\varepsilon}
as defined in the proposition.

Suppose first that the distance
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w)\geq 1
for all
w\in X
.
Then we have in (
9.23
)

B(z,y)\leq C_{B}\,1=C_{B}\,.

With (
9.24
) we have for each
N\geq 2

(9.32)

\bigl|h(z)\bigr|\leq C_{f,N}\int_{y=Y}^{\infty}y^{-N}\,C_{B}\,dy=\frac{Y^{1-N}}{N-1}\,C_{f,N}\,C_{B}\,.

Suppose next that the minimal distance
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w_{0}(z)\bigr)
of
z
to

X
is smaller than
1
. By Lemma
9.9
this is relevant for

t/e\leq y\leq et
, and

{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\geq e^{-1/2}\,\bigl\|\xi-x\bigr\|\,y^{-1}\geq e^{-1/2}\,\varepsilon y^{-1}\,.

In (
9.23
) we can take

(9.33)

B(z,y)\geq C_{B}\,e^{c/2}\,\varepsilon^{-c}\,y^{c}\,.

We get the following contribution to a bound for
h(z)
:

(9.34)

C_{f,N}\int_{y=\max(Y,t/e)}^{et}y^{-N}\,C_{B}\,e^{c/2}\,\varepsilon^{-c}\,y^{c}\,dy\leq C_{f,N}C_{B}e^{c/2}\varepsilon^{-c}\,\frac{Y^{1+c-N}}{N-c+1}

for
N>2+c
. (Since
1+c-N<0
we could replace
\max(Y,t/e)
by
Y
.)
On the remainder of
[Y,\infty)
we integrate
C_{f,N}y^{-N}C_{B}
. This
gives

(9.35)

\bigl|h(z)\bigr|\leq C_{f,N}\,C_{B}\Bigl(\frac{Y^{1-N}}{N-1}+e^{c/2}\varepsilon^{-c}\frac{Y^{1+c-N}}{N-c-1}\Bigr)\,,

for any choice of
N>c+1
.

Hence
h(z)
is bounded on
S\smallsetminus D_{\varepsilon}A^{+}_{Y}
.∎

Proof of part (

b

) of
Proposition
9.5
.

We consider

z=\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\not\in X
with
t\geq eY
.

If the minimal distance
{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(z,w_{0})\leq 1
, then this happens for

t/e\leq y\leq et
. We put
\varepsilon=\min_{x\in C}\|\xi-x\|
. Like in
(
9.34
) this gives the contribution

(9.36)

C_{f,N}C_{B}e^{c/2}\varepsilon^{-c}\,\frac{(t/e)^{1+c-N}}{N-c+1}

to a bound of
h(z)
, where we can take any
N>2+c+\rho+\re\nu
.

For
y\in[Y,\infty]\smallsetminus[t/e,et]
we use

(9.37)

B(z,y)=C_{B}\,e^{-(\rho+\re\nu)\bigl|\log y/t\bigr|}\,.

In this way we arrive at the following contributions, with

N>1+\rho+\re\nu
.

\displaystyle\int_{y=et}^{\infty}

\displaystyle C_{f,N}y^{-N}\,C_{B}\,(y/t)^{-\rho-\re\nu}\,dy\leq C_{f,N}C_{B}\,t^{\rho+\re\nu}\frac{(et)^{1-N-\rho-\re\nu}}{N+\rho+\re\nu-1}

\displaystyle\ll e^{1-N-\rho-\re\nu}C_{f,N}\,C_{B}\,t^{1-N}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{y=Y}^{t/e}

\displaystyle C_{f,N}\,C_{B}\,y^{-N+\rho+\re\nu}\,t^{-\rho-\re\nu}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq e^{N-1-\rho-\re\nu}\,C_{f,N}\,C_{B}\,\frac{Y^{1-N-\rho-\re\nu}}{N-\rho-\re\nu-1}\,t^{-\rho-\nu}\,.

Combining these estimates with (
9.36
), we obtain the bound
in (

b

).∎

9.3.
Spaces of
\nu
-semi-analytic boundary
germs

Definition 9.10
.

By a Siegel domain for
\Gamma
at the
cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
we mean a set of the form

{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C,T)=g_{\mathsf{c}}CA^{+}_{T}\subset S
with some compact subset

C\subset N
and
A^{+}_{T}=\bigl\{\mathrm{a}(t)\;:\;t\geq T\bigr\}
for some

T>0
.

For
\gamma\in\Gamma
the translate
\gamma\,{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C,T)
is a Siegel domain
at the cusp
\gamma\,{\mathsf{c}}
. For each
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
containing
the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
, the set
X\cap S
is contained in a suitably chosen
Siegel domain at
{\mathsf{c}}
.

Definition 9.11
.

Let
E
be a finite set of cusps of
\Gamma
. We
call a neighborhood
U
of
\left(\partial S\right)\smallsetminus E
in

\hat{S}
an
excised
neighborhood
of
\left(\partial S\right)\smallsetminus E
if it is of the
form

(9.38)

U^{\prime}\smallsetminus\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in E}\Bigl({\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C_{\mathsf{c}},T_{\mathsf{c}})\cup j{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C_{\mathsf{c}},T_{\mathsf{c}})\Bigr)

with a usual neighborhood
U^{\prime}
of
\partial S
in
\hat{S}
, and Siegel
domains (as defined above) that depend on the cusp.
(We recall that
j
is the involution in
\hat{S}
that fixes

\partial S
and interchanges
S
and
S^{-}
.)

An excised neighborhood is a standard neighborhood of
\partial S
from
which we have cut out relatively small sets in
S
and
S^{-}
. See
Figure
9.2
for an illustration in case
{\mathrm{d}}=2
. (Adapted
from
[
4
, Figure 9.1]
.

Figure 9.2.
An excised neighborhood
U
of
\partial S\smallsetminus\{{\mathsf{c}}_{1},{\mathsf{c}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}
in the case
{\mathrm{d}}=2
. By
S_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
we
indicate the Siegel domain
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C_{\mathsf{c}},T_{\mathsf{c}})
in
Definition
9.11
.

In the context of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and of the embedding

{\mathfrak{H}}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\!\mathbb{C}}
the name
rounded neighborhood
is better
than the name
excised neighborhood
; see
[
4
, Figures 9.1 and
9.2]
. Here we keep the older terminology.

Definition 9.12
.

We define the following
\Gamma
-modules of semi-analytic boundary germs
contained in
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
.

\mathrm{(i)}

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{exc}}}_{\!\!\nu}
is the submodule of those
boundary germs
[u]\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
that can be represented by a
function
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}\bigl(U\cap S\bigr)
for an excised neighborhood

U
of
(\partial S)\smallsetminus E
for some finite set
E
of cusps.

\mathrm{(ii)}

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
is the submodule of the
boundary germs
[u]\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{exc}}}_{\!\!\nu}
represented by

u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}\bigl(U\cap S\bigr)
as in (

i

), that satisfy

\mathrm{(a)}

u
is bounded on
U\cap S
,

\mathrm{(b)}

u\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})={\mathrm{O}}(t^{-\rho-\nu})

as
t\uparrow\infty
uniformly for
n
in compact sets such that

g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\in U
for all sufficiently large values
of
t
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
is the submodule of the

[u]\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
such that
\rho_{\nu}[u]\in\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}

extends as an element of
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.

The restriction map gives
corresponding
\Gamma
-modules

\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\ast}_{\!\!\nu}=\rho_{\nu}\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\ast}_{\!\!\nu}
.

We use Definition
6.5
of boundary singularities for these
modules as well. For
h\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega,\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
the assertion

\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}(h)=\emptyset
is equivalent to the statement

f\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.

Propositions
9.4
(

i

:

i

b

)
and (
9.5
) imply that integration of cusp forms leads to
elements of
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
.

.

Let

10.
From cocycles to cusp forms

Proposition
9.13
shows that the image

\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
is contained in the cohomology space

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}
. We
turn to the construction of a linear map in the opposite direction.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
,

|\re\nu|<\rho
.

There is a linear map

(10.1)

\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}:H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)

such that
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}\circ\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is the identity on
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
,
for the linear map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
in Proposition
9.13
composed
with the natural map

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}\,.

This theorem is a direct consequence of Propositions
10.5
,

10.7
, and
10.8
.

10.1.
Modules of functions on
S
representing

\nu
-analytic boundary germs

We recall that semi-analytic boundary germs
[u]\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}

are represented by functions
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(U\cap S)
for some open
neighborhood
U
in
\hat{S}
of

(\partial S)\smallsetminus\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}\bigl([u]\bigr)
. The function

t\mapsto u\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t),\text{{{e}}})
should extend analytically as a
function of
t^{-1}
on a neighborhood of
t^{-1}=0

for those
k\in K

for which

k\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\not\in\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}([u])
,
in such a way that
there is an analytic function
A_{u}
on
U
such that

u\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=t^{-(\rho+\nu)}\,A_{u}\bigl(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})
for all

k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\in U\cap S
. The relation
\Delta u=(\rho^{2}-\nu^{2})u

on
U\cap S
is equivalent to
\tilde{\Delta}_{\nu}A_{u}=0
on
U
, with a
differential operator
\tilde{\Delta}_{\nu}
that we described in terms of
normalized horospherical coordinates in (
8.12
).

In (
8.8
) we saw an example in which we can choose the open set

U\subset\hat{S}
in such a way that it contains
S
. For

[u]\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
the set
U
is fairly large. Then
U
is an
excised neighborhood of

(\partial S)\smallsetminus\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}\bigl([u]\bigr)
,
for which
U\cap S
is the union of a compact set and a finite number of
Siegel domains. For our purpose it is convenient to work with
representatives of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs that are defined on
the whole symmetric space
S
.

Definition 10.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
be the space of
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
for which
there are a finite set
E\subset{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
and an excised neighborhood
U

of
(\partial S)\smallsetminus E
such that

\mathrm{(a)}

f
represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary germ in

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left((\partial S)\smallsetminus E\right)
,

\mathrm{(b)}

f
is a bounded function on
S
,

\mathrm{(c)}

for each
{\mathsf{c}}\in E

(10.2)

f\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)={\mathrm{O}}\bigl(t^{-(\nu+\rho)}\bigr)\qquad\text{ as }t\uparrow\infty

uniformly for
n
in compact sets in
N
.

Further we define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{f\in C^{\infty}(S)\;:\;\text{$f$
represents an element of $\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)$}\bigr\}\subset{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{f\in{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\;:\;\text{$f$
represents the zero boundary germ}\bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\omega}_{\nu}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}\cap\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\,.

So
{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}
is the case
E=\emptyset
in the definition of

{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
. The support of elements of
\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}

is the union of a compact subset of
S
and finitely many Siegel
domains. The space
\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
depends on
\nu
, since
\nu

occurs in the growth conditions at the cusps in
E
. The space

\mathcal{N}^{\omega}_{\nu}
is equal to
C_{c}^{\infty}(S)
, and it does not depend
on
\nu
.

Proposition 10.3
.

The spaces in Definition
10.2
are

\Gamma
-modules, related by

(10.3)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 94.0032pt\raise-33.12892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 158.8536pt\raise-33.12892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 158.8536pt\raise-33.12892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 226.13458pt\raise-33.12892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 226.13458pt\raise-33.12892pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

in which the rows are exact.

Proof.

We show the surjectivity in the lower exact sequence. The
other statements are clear.

Any element of
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
has a representative
f_{0}
in

{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}\left(U\cap S\right)
for some excised neighborhood
U
of

(\partial S)\smallsetminus E
for some finite set of cusps. It also
satisfies the conditions in part (

ii

) in
Definition
9.12
, which are similar to the conditions in parts
(

b

) and (

c

) in Definition
10.2
, but
concern only points in an excised neighborhood.

We have to extend a modification of
f_{0}
to all of
S
. Near a cusp

{\mathsf{c}}\in E
we have the situation sketched in Figure
10.1
.

Figure 10.1.
The Siegel domain
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C_{\mathsf{c}},Y)
transported to
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
.

If
C_{\mathsf{c}}
is chosen suitably, then

\Delta f_{0}=(\rho^{2}-\penalty\nu^{2})f_{0}
outside
{\mathfrak{S}}(C_{\mathsf{c}},Y)
and
outside the compact set
Q
. For each
{\mathsf{c}}\in E
we take a bounded
open set
A_{\mathsf{c}}\subset N
containing the compact set
C_{\mathsf{c}}
, and we
take a compact set
D_{\mathsf{c}}\subset N
containing
A_{\mathsf{c}}
. Transporting

{\mathsf{c}}
to
{\boldsymbol{\infty}}
we have the situation sketched in
Figure
10.2
.

Figure 10.2.
Inclusion of various Siegel domains.

Similarly, the compact set
Q\subset S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
is embedded into larger
compact sets. In this way we arrive at a transition zone in
U\cap S

on which we can multiply
f_{0}
by a smooth function
\chi
going from

1
to zero. Then
f=\chi\,f_{0}
extended by zero on the remaining
region is a smooth function representing the same
\nu
-analytic
boundary germ as
f_{0}
. Moreover the estimates in (

b

) and
(

c

) in Definition
10.2
are valid on each Siegel
domain
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(D_{\mathsf{c}},Y)
with
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.

We can add to
f
any smooth function with compact support not
intersecting the Siegel domains
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C_{\mathsf{c}},Y)
. The resulting
function represents the same boundary germ as
f_{0}
.
∎

Definition 10.4
.

For any function
f:S\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
the set of

\nu
-singularities

\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}(f)
of
f
is the complement of
the maximal open set
W\subset S
such that
f\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(W)
.

This concept of singularity is very wide. Any function that is not an
eigenfunction of the Laplace operator with eigenvalue
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}
on
a neighborhood of
x\in S
has a
\nu
-singularity at
x
.

10.2.
Construction of invariant eigenfunctions from cocycles

We
turn to the construction that will allow us to prove
Theorem
10.1
. Actually, the construction that we describe is
stronger than what we need for the present purpose. This construction
forms the essential step from cocycles back to automorphic forms. The
method is analogous to the approach in
[
4
]
for automorphic
forms on cofinite discrete subgroups of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. First cocompact
discrete subgroups are handled in §7.1. The presence of cusps needs
more work, carried out in §12.2.

Discussion.
If we deal with a cocycle
\psi
coming from a cusp
form, then Proposition
9.2
tells us what to do. On a union
of fundamental domains
Z
we integrate
\psi
over
\partial Z
.
We get back the cusp form on the interior of
Z
, and we can extend the
result to
S
in a
\Gamma
-invariant way. See Figure
10.3
.

Figure 10.3.
Two-dimensional illustration of the approach of the proof of
Proposition
9.2
. A union
Z
of fundamental
domains.

We have to modify this idea, to be able to apply it to cocycle that
might not come from cusp forms.

The difference between
\nu
-analytic boundary forms and their
representative functions on
S
is confusing. It is difficult to work
with cocycles with values in
\nu
-analytic boundary forms. More
convenient would be cocycles with values in the space
{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}
of
representatives, which are actual functions on
S
. This is not
possible with cocycles, however we can find cochains with values in

{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}
that represent the cocycle with which we start. If we have
an arbitrary cocycle it may happen that the value of its
representatives on
\partial Z
has singularities in the interior of

Z
, and the application of Proposition
9.2
breaks down.

A cocycle that satisfies the condition ‘exc’ has representatives on

\partial Z
that have no singularities in an excised neighborhood of

\partial S\smallsetminus E
, where the finite set
E
of cusps depend on
the components of
\partial Z
.
It turns out that if we take a very wide cycle
C
around
Z
a suitable
representative of the cocycle can be chosen such that its value on

\partial C

has no singularities on the interior of
Z
. The choice of
C
has to
take into account the set
Z
and the choice of the representative of
the cocycle. See Figure
10.4
.

Figure 10.4.
Two-dimensional illustration of the approach of the proof of
Proposition
9.2
. A cycle around
Z
.

Figure copied from
[
4
, p 75]
.

Our main task is to adapt the two-dimensional reasoning in
[
4
]

to the present
{\mathrm{d}}
-dimensional context.

Proposition 10.5
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
. There is an explicit
construction
\psi\mapsto u_{\psi}
that provides for each cocycle

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{exc}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
a
function
u_{\psi}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
such that

\mathrm{(a)}

u_{\psi}
depends linearly on
\psi
,

\mathrm{(b)}

u_{\psi}
depends only on the cohomology class of

\psi
in
Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{exc}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
,

\mathrm{(c)}

u_{\psi}|\gamma=u_{\psi}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

After a geometrical preparation we will describe the
construction, and prove it properties.

R
-neighborhoods of
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells.
Before
proving the proposition, we consider the influence of the condition
‘exc’ on cocycles. We define for each
C\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
the

R
-neighborhood
N_{R}(C)
for each positive real
parameter
R
.

If
C\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
we take

(10.4)

N_{R}(C)=\bigl\{x\in S\;:\;{\mathrm{d}_{S}\!}(x,C)\leq R\bigr\}\,.

This is a compact set, since
C
is a compact set in
S
.

If
C\not\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
then
C
contains a unique cusp

{\mathsf{c}}
, and
C
is contained in some Siegel domain
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(B,T)
with

B
compact in
N
and
T>0
. We define

B_{R}=\bigl\{\mathrm{n}(x)\;:\;\bigl|x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i}\bigr|\leq R\text{ for }1\leq i\leq p+q\text{ for all }\mathrm{n}(x^{\prime})\in B\bigr\}

in horospherical coordinates
x=(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
, with

n(x^{\prime})=n(x_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,x_{p+q}^{\prime})
.
We take

(10.5)

N_{R}(C)={\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}\bigl(B_{R},(T^{-1}+R)^{-1}\bigr)\,.

These
R
-neighborhoods satisfy the following statements:

Lemma 10.6
.

For any
C_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
there are only finitely many

C\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
such that
N_{R}(C_{0})\cap N_{R}(C)
is non-empty.

If
C_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
, then
there is a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}\in C_{0}
. For all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}
we have

{\mathsf{c}}\in\gamma C_{0}
. For any
C_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
there are only
finitely many
C\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
such that

(10.6)

N_{R}(C_{0})\cap N_{R}(C)\cap S\neq\emptyset\,.

Proof.

If
C_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
then
N_{R}(C_{0})
is a
compact set, and intersects only finitely many
\Gamma
-translates of the
fundamental domain
D(p_{0})
in (
4.17
) on which we based the
tessellation. Each
\Gamma
-translate of
D(p_{0})
intersects only finitely
many
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells in
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
.

If
C_{0}
contains a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
, then
N_{R}(C_{0})
is not compact, since it
contains a region in
S
with
{\mathsf{c}}
in its closure. The construction of

N_{R}(C_{0})
takes care that it intersects only finitely many
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells
containing
{\mathsf{c}}
.
∎

Construction.
Let

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
.
The cocycle is determined by its values
\psi(C_{j})
on a finite set of

({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
that generate
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
.

We use the exact sequences in (
10.3
) to choose
representatives
\tilde{\psi}(C_{j})\in{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. If

C_{j}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
we can take

\tilde{\psi}(C_{j})\in{\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{\nu}}
. If
C_{j}
contains a cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
, then

\tilde{\psi}(C_{j})\in{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
with

\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}\bigl(\tilde{\psi}(C_{j})\bigr)\subset\{{\mathsf{c}}\}
. We extend

\tilde{\psi}
in a
\Gamma
-equivariant way to a
\mathbb{C}[\Gamma]
-linear map

\tilde{\psi}:F_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}^{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
. This is
only a cochain, not a cocycle.

We arrange that
\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}\bigl(\tilde{\psi}(C_{j})\bigr)\subset N_{R}(C_{j})

for
1\leq j\leq n
by choosing
R
sufficiently large. This implies

\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}\bigl(\tilde{\psi}(C)\bigr)\subset N_{R}(C)
for all

C\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
, by
\Gamma
-equivariance.

To construct a function in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
we consider first a compact
region
Z
that is contained in the union
Z_{1}
of finitely many

{\mathrm{d}}
-cells
Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}
. The boundary
\partial Z_{1}
is a
cycle in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}]
. We take a much larger union

\tilde{Z}\supset Z_{1}
of finitely many
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells

Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{d}}
such that its boundary

\partial\tilde{Z}=\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}D_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}]

satisfies for all
i

(10.7)

Z\cap N_{R}(D_{i})\cap S=\emptyset\,.

Hence

(10.8)

\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}\bigl(\tilde{\psi}(\partial\tilde{Z})\bigr)\subset\bigcup_{i}\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}\bigl(d\tilde{\psi}(D_{i})\bigr)

does not intersect
Z\cap S
. The function

(10.9)

u_{\tilde{Z},\tilde{\psi}}=\frac{1}{2\nu c(\nu)}\tilde{\psi}(\partial\tilde{Z})=\frac{1}{2\nu c(\nu)}\sum_{i}(d\tilde{\psi})(D_{i})

on
Z\cap S
determines an element of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}\bigl(\mathring{Z}\bigr)
.
We do not need factors in
\{1,-1\}
to fix the orientation. The
notation of the
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells
D_{i}
is automatically positive, and

\partial\tilde{Z}
gets its orientation from
\tilde{Z}
. We note that
on
Z\cap S
the functions
\tilde{\psi}(D_{i})
are representatives of

\psi(D_{i})
. The division by
\nu\,c(\nu)
causes no problems.
See (
9.4
).

If
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
is another
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cycle satisfying the same
conditions, then
\partial\tilde{Z}^{\prime}-\partial\tilde{Z}
is the
boundary of a sum of
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells far away from
Z
, and on
Z\cap S

u_{\tilde{Z}^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}}-u_{\tilde{Z},\tilde{\psi}}=\frac{1}{2\nu\,c(\nu)}\tilde{\psi}\bigl(\partial(\tilde{Z}^{\prime}-\tilde{Z})\bigr)=\frac{1}{2\nu\,c(\nu)}\psi\bigl(\partial(\tilde{Z}^{\prime}-\tilde{Z})\bigr)=0\,

since
\psi
is a cocycle. This shows that we can write

u_{Z,\tilde{\psi}}
, independently of the choice of
\tilde{Z}
.

If
Z_{1}\supset Z
is a union of more
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells, then a suitable choice
of
\tilde{Z}_{1}
works fine for
Z
, and we have

u_{\tilde{Z}_{1},\tilde{\psi}}=u_{Z,\tilde{\psi}}\qquad\text{on }Z\cap S\,.

This implies that we can define

(10.10)

u_{\tilde{\psi}}(z)=\lim_{Z}u_{Z,\tilde{\psi}}(z)\,,

where
Z
runs over larger and larger sums of finitely many
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells.
This defines
u_{\tilde{\psi}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
.

Properties.
We have still to show that the constructed
eigenfunction
u_{\tilde{\psi}}
does not depend on the choice of the
cochain
\tilde{\psi}
and that it has properties
(

a

)–(

c

).

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
. For
z\in S
we take
Z
such that it contains
\gamma z

and
z
, and we take
\tilde{Z}
suitable for
Z
.

\displaystyle 2\nu\,c(\nu)

\displaystyle\,u_{\tilde{\psi}}(\gamma z)=\tilde{\psi}(\partial\gamma\tilde{Z})(\gamma z)=\tilde{\psi}(\gamma\partial\tilde{Z})|\gamma^{-1}\,(z)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}(\partial\tilde{Z})(z)=2\nu\,c(\nu)\,u_{\tilde{\psi}}(z)\,.

So
u_{\tilde{\psi}}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(\Gamma\backslash S)
.

Two different choices of cochains
\tilde{\psi}
representing
\psi

differ by a
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cochain
b
with values in
\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
.
In the situation of (
10.9
) this gives on
Z\cap S
the zero
function. So we can write

(10.11)

u_{\tilde{\psi}}=u_{\psi}\,.

This gives a
\Gamma
-invariant function
u_{\psi}
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
. This is
part (

c

). For assertion (

a

) we note that we
can take lifts of cocycles in a linear way.

Let

\psi=d\eta\in B^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu})\cap Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu})^{\mathrm{bdc}}
.
Choose
\tilde{\eta}(D)\in{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
with image

\eta(D)\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
for representatives of the
\Gamma
-orbits
in
X^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-2}
, and extend
\tilde{\eta}
as a cochain. Then

d\tilde{\eta}
can play the role of
\tilde{\psi}
. Since

\tilde{\psi}(\partial C)=d\tilde{\psi}(C)=d^{2}\tilde{\eta}(C)=0
,
we conclude that
u_{\tilde{\psi}}=0
, and hence
u_{\psi}=0
. This gives
assertion (

b

).
∎

The construction gives for classes

[\psi]\in H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigr(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{exc}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}
a
specific element of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(\Gamma\backslash S)
without condition on the
growth at the cusps. In Theorem
10.1
we deal with cocycles
with values in the smaller module
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
. So there
is further work to do. Furthermore, we have no guarantee that the
construction might not give zero functions.

10.3.
Cocycles and cusp forms

Proposition 10.7
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
|\re\nu|<\rho
. If

\psi\in Z^{d-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
,
then
u_{\psi}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
.

Proof.

The additional assumption concerning the cocycle
\psi

implies that we can use condition (

c

) in
Definition
10.2
. By Definition
3.6
we need quick decay
for
u\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
at the cusps. Actually, any decay of size

{\mathrm{O}}\bigl(t^{\rho-|\re\nu|-\varepsilon}\bigr)
,
\varepsilon>0
, suffices by
Lemma
3.12
.

For a given cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
it suffices to give an estimate of
u_{\psi}
on a
Siegel domain
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C,T)
, where
C
contains a fundamental domain
of
\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}\backslash N
. We take
\tilde{Z}
as the boundary of a union
of
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells covering
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C,T)
such that

N_{R}(D_{i})\cap{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C,T)=\emptyset
for all the finitely many

({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cells occurring in
\tilde{Z}
.

For boundary components
D_{i}
for which
{\mathsf{c}}\not\in D_{i}
the
contribution
\tilde{\psi}(D_{i})
represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary germ
on a neighborhood
U_{i}
of
{\mathsf{c}}
in
\partial S
. Then we have

\tilde{\psi}(D_{i})\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(t)\bigr)=t^{-\rho-\nu}A\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(t)\bigr)
for all

g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(t)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{c}}(C,T)
, for some analytic function on
a neighborhood of
U_{i}
in
\hat{S}
. The analytic function
A
is
bounded on
U_{i}
, and

(10.12)

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}t^{\rho+\nu}\tilde{\psi}(D_{i})\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(t)\bigr)=A_{u}(g_{\mathsf{c}}\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})=A_{u}({\mathsf{c}})\,.

This gives an estimate of the desired strength of the contribution of

D_{i}
.

The remaining boundary components
D_{i}
contain the cusp
{\mathsf{c}}
. The
growth condition (

c

) in Definition
10.2
gives for
the contribution of these components the behavior

{\mathrm{O}}\bigl(t^{-(\rho+\nu)}\bigr)
as well.

To satisfy the condition in Lemma
3.12

we need
-\rho-\re\nu<\rho-\bigl|\re\nu\bigr|
. Under the additional
condition
|\re\nu|<\rho
we conclude that
u_{\psi}
is a cusp form.
∎

Proposition 10.8
.

Suppose that
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
satisfies

\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
and
|\re\nu|<\rho
.

Let
u\in{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
and let the
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cocycle
\psi
be
determined by
\psi(C)=I_{C}(u;\cdot)
for all
C\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}-1}
,
as in (
9.15
). Then
u_{\psi}=u
.

Proof.

We consider a
{\mathrm{d}}
-cell
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{\mathrm{d}}
. Let

V_{\varepsilon}
be the open set of points
z\in X
with distance to
\partial X

larger than
\varepsilon
. By taking
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, we arrange that

V_{\varepsilon}
has a non-empty interior, which is open.

The equality
I_{C}(u;y)=\int_{x\in C}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(u;y,x)
and the
inclusion
\mathsf{Sing}\bigl(I_{C}(u;\cdot)\bigr)\subset C
hold for each
boundary component
C\in B(X)
. We take a lift
\tilde{\psi}
of
\psi

like in the proof of Proposition
10.5
, and now can take the
parameter
R
smaller than
\varepsilon
. Then
\tilde{\psi}(C)(y)=\psi(C)(y)
for

y\in\mathring{V}_{\varepsilon}
. This gives for
y\in V_{\varepsilon}

(10.13)

\displaystyle 2\nu\,c(\nu)

\displaystyle u_{\psi}(y)=\tilde{\psi}(\partial X)(y)=\psi(\partial X)(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\partial X}\omega^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}(u;y,x)=2\nu\,c(\nu)\,u(y)\,,

with use of Proposition
9.2
in the last step. Since

\nu\,c(\nu)\neq 0
under the condition on
\nu
, we conclude that

u_{\psi}=u
on the non-empty open set
\mathring{V}_{\varepsilon}
. By analyticity,

u_{\psi}=u
on
S
.
∎

11.
Injectivity of the map from cocycles to cusp
forms

Theorem
10.1
provides a linear map
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
from

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
that is only a
one-sided inverse to the linear map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
in
Proposition
9.13
.

In the case
{\mathrm{d}}=2
the map
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is a bijection. See

[
4
, Proposition 12.6]
. For
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
we do not know yet
whether the kernel of
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
is zero or not. In this section we
describe the kernel as the image of a cohomology space with values in a
specific space of representatives of
\nu
-analytic boundary forms.
This leads, for
\nu
satisfying
|\re\nu|<\rho
and

\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
, to the result in
part (

i

) of Theorem
B
stated in the
introduction. The less complicated statement in (

ii

)
will be considered in the next section.

In Subsection
11.1
we transform the construction of

u_{\psi}
in Subsection
10.2
into a description as a
locally finite sum. This description is used in
Subsection
11.2
to relate the subspace of

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
that we
have to divide out, to the image of the cohomology space

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}})
. With help of sheaf cohomology
we will show that

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}})=H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)\bigr)
. This leads to
completion of the proof of part (

i

) of
Theorem
B
.

11.1.
Coinvariants and infinite sums

The
construction in the proof of Proposition
10.5
of the cusp
form
u_{\psi}
associated to a cocycle

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

is based on a lift

\tilde{\psi}\in C^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{exc}}^{0}}_{\nu}}\bigr)
,
and on evaluation of
\tilde{\psi}
on
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-cycles. The next
proposition gives an alternative formulation, which will be important
later on in this section.

Proposition 11.1
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
\re\nu>-\rho
. Let

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu})^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}

and let

\tilde{\psi}\in C^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}})^{\mathrm{bdc}}

be a lift as in the proof of Proposition
10.5
. Then

(11.1)

u_{\psi}(x)=\sum_{D\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{d}}d\tilde{\psi}(D)(x)

represents
u_{\psi}
as a locally finite sum of elements of

{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
.

The cancellation of the
\nu
-singularities is remarkable. Since

u_{\psi}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
we have
\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}(u_{\psi})=\emptyset
. However, the
choice of the lift
\psi
implies that in general

\mathsf{Sing}_{\nu}\bigl(d\tilde{\psi}(D)\bigr)\neq\emptyset
, and hence that
singularities of neighboring cells
D
have to cancel each other.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\psi}
be a cochain representing
\psi
, like in
the proof of Proposition
10.5
. Since
d\psi(D)=0
for each

{\mathrm{d}}
-cell, we have
d\tilde{\psi}(D)\in\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
.

We recall that the union of the
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells is equal to
S^{\ast}
, with
overlap only in boundary components. We consider a finite union

U_{E}=\bigcup_{D\in E}D
with a finite subset
E\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{d}
.
For suitable choice of
E
we can use

\tilde{Z}=\sum_{D\in E}\partial D
as a
({\mathrm{d}}-1)
-dimensional set
surrounding a given set
Z
, as in the proof of
Proposition
10.5
. Then

u_{\psi}=\frac{1}{2\nu\,c(\nu)}\,\tilde{\psi}\bigl(\partial U_{E}\bigr)

on
Z
. If we add more and more
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells to
E
we get a
representation of
u_{\psi}
as a sum over larger and larger sets
D
.
Since the sets
\mathsf{Sing}(\tilde{\psi}(D))
have only finitely many non-empty
pairwise intersections, the sum
\sum_{D\in E}d\tilde{\psi}(D)(x)

becomes locally constant in
x
as
E\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{d}
expands, and
the infinite sum in (
11.1
) converges absolutely on
S
and
represents
u_{\psi}
.
∎

The group
\Gamma
acts freely on the set
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
of
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells,
with finitely many orbits. We can generate these orbits by

{\mathfrak{F}}_{0}\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},Y}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
and
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}
where
{\mathsf{c}}
runs over a
set
R(\Gamma)

of representatives of
\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
. So we can write the
assertion (
11.1
) as

(11.2)

u_{\psi}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L(\gamma)\Bigl(d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})+\sum_{{\mathsf{c}}\in R(\Gamma)}d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}})\Bigr)\,.

By Proposition
4.4
the space

H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu})
is isomorphic to the space of
coinvariants
(\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu})_{\Gamma}
, which is

\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigm/\left(1-L(\Gamma)\right)\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
,
where we denote by
\left(1-L(\Gamma)\right)\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
the
space generated by the elements
L(\gamma)v-v
, where
\gamma
runs through

\Gamma
and
v
runs through
\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
. The coinvariant
associated to the cohomology class of
d\tilde{\psi}
can be represented
by

(11.3)

c\bigl(d\tilde{\psi})=d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})+\sum_{{\mathsf{c}}\in R(\Gamma)}d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}})\,.

Other choices of generating
{\mathrm{d}}
-cells lead to the same quantity modulo
the space
\left(1-L(\Gamma)\right)\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
\re\nu>-\rho
. Let

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu})^{{\mathrm{bdc}}}

and let

\tilde{\psi}\in C^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}})^{\mathrm{bdc}}

be a lift as in the proof of Proposition
10.5
.

The cusp form
u_{\psi}
is zero if and only if the cohomology class of

d\tilde{\psi}
is equal to the trivial coinvariant

\left(1-L(\Gamma)\right)\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
.

Proof.

We construct a partition of unity on
S
. This is a
function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(S)
such that

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\chi(\gamma\,x)=1
for all
x\in S
, as a locally finite
sum.

We choose
a>Y
. Then

S_{\!\Gamma}(a)=S\smallsetminus\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}}B_{\mathsf{c}}(a)
is a
neighborhood of
S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)
. We choose
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S)
such that

(11.4)

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\chi_{0}(\gamma\,x)=1\qquad\text{for }x\in S_{\!\Gamma}(a)\,.

For each
{\mathsf{c}}\in R(\Gamma)
we take
\kappa_{\mathsf{c}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(N)
such that

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}}}\kappa_{\mathsf{c}}(\lambda^{-1}n)=1
for
n\in N
, and then put
for
g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\in B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)

(11.5)

\chi_{\mathsf{c}}\bigl(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=\kappa_{\mathsf{c}}(n)\,.

Then

(11.6)

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}}\chi_{\mathsf{c}}(\gamma^{-1}x)=1\qquad\text{for }x\in B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)\,.

We note that
\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}}
is a lattice in
N
, and that

\Gamma_{\!{\mathsf{c}}}=g_{\mathsf{c}}\Lambda_{\mathsf{c}}g_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}\subset\Gamma
. We define

\chi_{\mathsf{c}}=0
on
S\smallsetminus B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
.

We glue
\chi_{0}
and the functions
\chi_{\mathsf{c}}
together with a function

\vartheta\in C^{\infty}(S)
determined by

(11.7)

\vartheta(x)=1\text{ for }x\in S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\,,\quad\vartheta(g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=\vartheta_{0}(t)\text{ for }g_{\mathsf{c}}n\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\in B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)\,,

with
\vartheta_{0}\in C^{\infty}\bigl([Y,\infty)\bigr)
satisfying

\vartheta_{0}\geq 0
,
\vartheta_{0}(Y)=1
and
\vartheta_{0}(t)=0
for
t\geq a
, where

a>y
has been chosen at the start of the proof. Then we obtain

\chi\in C^{\infty}(S)
by

(11.8)

\displaystyle\chi(x)

\displaystyle=\chi_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\text{for }x\in S_{\!\Gamma}(Y)\,,

\displaystyle=\vartheta(x)\,\chi_{0}(x)+\sum_{{\mathsf{c}}\in\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}}\bigl(1-\vartheta(x)\bigr)\chi_{\mathsf{c}}(x)

\displaystyle\text{for }x\in\gamma B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y),\;\gamma\in\Gamma\,.

The resulting function
\chi
is in
\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
, and

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L(\gamma)\chi=1
, as a locally finite sum.

We put

f=d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})+\sum_{{\mathsf{c}}\in R(\Gamma)}d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}})
.
With (
11.2
) and (
11.3
) we have

(11.9)

u_{\psi}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L(\gamma)f\,.

We apply the following computation, with locally finite sums. We use
that left translation of products satisfies

L(\gamma)(a\cdot b)=\bigl(L(\gamma)a\bigr)\cdot\bigl(L(\gamma)b\bigr)
.

\displaystyle f-\chi\cdot u_{\psi}

\displaystyle=f\cdot\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L(\gamma^{-1})\chi

partition of unity

\displaystyle\qquad\hbox{}-\chi\cdot\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L(\gamma)f

with (
11.9
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}f\cdot L(\gamma^{-1})\chi-\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L(\gamma)\Bigl(f\cdot L(\gamma^{-1})\chi\Bigr)

(11.10)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Bigl(1-L(\gamma)\Bigr)\Bigl(f\cdot L(\gamma^{-1})\chi\Bigr)\,.

The function
f\cdot\bigl(L(\gamma^{-1})\chi\bigr)
can be non-zero only if

\supp(f)\cap\bigl(\gamma^{-1}\supp(\chi)\bigr)\neq\emptyset
. This
happens only for finitely many
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Hence
f-\chi\cdot u_{\psi}

is an element of
\left(1-L(\Gamma)\right)\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
.

Under the assumption that
u_{\psi}=0
the function

f=d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{0})+\sum_{{\mathsf{c}}\in R(\Gamma)}d\tilde{\psi}({\mathfrak{F}}_{\mathsf{c}})

represents the trivial coinvariant in
(\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu})_{\Gamma}
.
Conversely, if
f
represents the trivial coinvariant, then the sum of
its
\Gamma
-translates vanishes.
∎

11.2.
The kernel of the map from cohomology to
cocycles

Under the conditions
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,

|\re\nu|<\rho
we now have the following scheme of linear maps

(11.11)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
