3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\Bigr)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

See Proposition
9.13
and Theorem
10.1
. The upper
vertical map is the natural map associated to the injective linear map

\mathbf{i}:\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
.
Proposition
10.8
states that the composition of the three maps
in the triangle starting at
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
is the identity. This
implies that
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
and the composition
\mathbf{i}\circ\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
are
injective.

The bottom row is part of the long exact sequence associated to the
short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\rightarrow{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\rightarrow 0\,.

See Remark
4.6
. The space

H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\Bigr)
is isomorphic to the
space of coinvariants
(\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu})_{\Gamma}
; see
Proposition
4.4
. So a class in

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}
represented by

\psi\in Z^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(F^{\mathcal{T}}_{\bullet};\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

is in the kernel of
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
if its image
[d\tilde{\psi}]
in

H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)
vanishes. See
Proposition
11.2
. The part of the long exact sequence in
the diagram (
11.11
) shows that this happens if
[\psi]
is in the
image of
H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\bigr)
under the
natural map associated to

{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
. In this way, we
arrive at the following result.

Proposition 11.3
.

If

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
|\re\nu|<\rho
then the
kernel of the surjective map

\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}:H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
is equal to the
intersection

(11.12)

\displaystyle I^{W,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}

\displaystyle:=H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cap\im\Bigl(H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\bigr)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)\Bigr)\,.

In the case
{\mathrm{d}}=2
we have

H^{1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\bigr)=\{0\}
by

[
4
, Proposition 12.5]
.

We recall that
\mathcal{N}^{\omega}_{\nu}=C^{\infty}_{c}(S)
, and that
\mathcal{N}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}

is the space of functions
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
with support contained in
the union of a finite number of Siegel domains and a compact subset of

S
satisfying the growth conditions in parts (

b

)
and (

c

) in Definition
10.2
.

We abbreviate

\Delta_{\nu}=\Delta-\rho^{2}+\nu^{2}
,
and define
\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}
as the image
\Delta_{\nu}{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
. The
elements of
\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}
have support in the union of a finite number of
Siegel domains and a compact subset of
S
. The differentiation will in
general destroy the growth conditions that elements of

{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
satisfy.

The kernel of
\Delta_{\nu}:{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}
is the

\Gamma
-module
{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}={\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)\cap{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}
. We have the exact sequence

(11.13)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{\nu}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}\longrightarrow 0\,.

Lemma 11.4
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
|\re\nu|<\rho
. For each

h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(S)
there are
f\in C^{\infty}(S)
such that

\Delta_{\nu}f=h
.

Proof.

Since
Q_{\nu}
is locally integrable,
[
34
, Lemma
2.1]
, the function

f_{1}(x)=\int_{y\in S}q_{\nu}(x;y)h(y)\,d\mu(y)

is well-defined.
We test against
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(S)
. So

\Delta_{\nu}\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(S)
, and with use of (
9.3
) in
Proposition
9.1
,

\displaystyle\int_{x\in S}(\Delta_{\nu}\varphi)(x)\,f_{1}(x)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in S}(\Delta_{\nu}\varphi)(x)\int_{y\in S}q_{\nu}(x;y)\,h(y)\,d\mu(y)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in S}\int_{x\in S}q_{\nu}(x;y)\Delta_{\nu}\varphi(x)\,d\mu(x)\,h(y)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=2\nu\,c(\nu)\int_{y\in S}\varphi(y)\,h(y)\,d\mu(y)\,.

So
f_{1}
is a weak solution of the differential equation

\Delta_{\nu}f=2\nu\,c(\nu)\,h
. Since
\Delta
is elliptic and

\nu\,c(\nu)\neq 0
the equation
\Delta_{\nu}f=h
has a smooth solution.
∎

11.3.
Sheaf cohomology

We recall Proposition
4.5
,
which states that parabolic cohomology spaces with values in a

\Gamma
-module
V
are isomorphic to sheaf cohomology spaces on

\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
with values in a locally constant sheaf
associated to
V
. By

{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]=\Gamma\backslash\left(\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}\times S^{\!\ast}\right)
we
denote the locally constant sheaf associated to
\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty}
denote the sheaf of

C^{\infty}
-functions on
\Gamma\backslash S
, and let
j
denote the
embedding
\Gamma\backslash S\rightarrow\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
. We put

{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}=j_{\ast}{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty}:U\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty}\bigl(j^{-1}U\bigr)
.

Lemma 11.5
.

The restriction of
{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]
to

\Gamma\backslash S
is isomorphic to the sheaf
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

It suffices to check that the stalks at all points

x\ \in\Gamma\backslash S
are isomorphic. By

\pi:S^{\!\ast}\rightarrow\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
we denote the natural
projection. Since
\Gamma
is torsion-free, each point
x\in S
has small
open neighborhoods
U_{0}
that are relatively compact in
S
and for
which
\gamma\,U_{0}\cap U_{0}=\emptyset
for all

\gamma\in\Gamma\smallsetminus\{e\}
. Then
\bigsqcup\gamma\,U_{0}
is open in
S

and the image
U=\pi U_{0}=\pi\bigl(\bigsqcup\gamma\,U_{0}\bigr)
is a
neighborhood of
x\in\Gamma\backslash S
.

Each element
h\in\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}
is a
C^{\infty}
-function on
S
with
support contained in the union of a compact set and a finite number of
Siegel domains. Taking the compact set large so that it contains
U_{0}
,
we see that the sections in
{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}](U_{0})
are precisely the
sections of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty}
on
U_{0}
. Hence for
p\in\Gamma\backslash S
we
have equality of stalks

{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]_{p}=({\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty})_{p}\,.\qed

Lemma 11.6
.

Let

p\in(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast})\smallsetminus(\Gamma\backslash S)=\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.
Then

\mathrm{(a)}

the stalk
{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]_{p}
is zero;

\mathrm{(b)}

the stalk
({\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast})_{p}
is non-zero, and each germ
in
({\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast})_{p}
is given by a function in
C^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash S)

with support in
\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in p}B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
.

In this statement,
p
denotes a class in
\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.
So
p
is a point on the quotient
\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
, and
{\mathsf{c}}\in p

runs through the cusps in the class
p
.

Proof.

The extended horoballs
B^{\ast}_{\mathsf{c}}(a)
with
a>Y
form a neighborhood
basis of
{\mathsf{c}}
in
S^{\!\ast}
. Each germ in
({\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast})_{p}
can be
represented by a
\Gamma
-invariant
C^{\infty}
-function
f
on the union

\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in p}B_{\mathsf{c}}(a)
for some
a>Y
. Let
{\mathsf{c}}\in p
. The
restriction of
f
to
B_{\mathsf{c}}(a)
is
\Gamma_{\mathsf{c}}
-invariant, and
multiplication by a suitable
N_{\mathsf{c}}
-invariant cut-off function
\chi

gives another representative
\chi f
with compact support in the
interior of
B_{\mathsf{c}}(a)
. This determines a
\Gamma
-invariant function in

C^{\infty}(S)
with support in the union
\bigcup_{{\mathsf{c}}\in p}B_{\mathsf{c}}(Y)
.
This gives part (

b

).

If we start with a non-zero element
h\in{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]_{p}
its
restriction to
B_{\mathsf{c}}(a)
cannot be periodic, since the support of
h

is contained in a Siegel domain at
{\mathsf{c}}
. This gives
part (

a

).
∎

We recall that for a sheaf
A
and a subsheaf
B
we get a presheaf

U\mapsto A(U)/B(U)
that may not be a sheaf. The quotient sheaf

A\bigm/B
is the sheaf associated to this presheaf. See for instance

[
19
, Chap. I, §1, Proposition-Definition 1.2]
.

A sheaf
F
on
X
is a skyscraper sheaf at
p\in X
if for some vector
space
V

F(U)=V\quad\text{ if }p\in U,\quad F(U)=\{0\}\quad\text{ if }p\not\in U\,.

Proposition 11.7
.

The quotient sheaf

\,{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}\bigm/{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]
is a finite direct sum of skyscraper
sheaves on the finitely many points of

\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}\subset\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
.

Proof.

If
\Gamma
has only one
\Gamma
-orbit of cusps, then Lemmas

11.5
and
11.6
show that we get a sky-scraper
sheaf as the quotient. Suppose that there are more than one
\Gamma
-orbit
of cusps. The description of the support of representatives in
part (

b

) of Lemma
11.6
shows that we can split
up representatives of the quotient into representatives corresponding
to the different points of
\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Cu}(\Gamma)}
.
∎

Corollary 11.8
.

If
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
, then

H^{i}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast},{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]\bigr)\cong H^{i}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}\bigr)=\{0\}
for
i\geq 2
.

Proof.

We use the long exact sequence of cohomology spaces on

\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast}
associated to the short exact sequence

(11.14)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\longrightarrow 0

with
{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}\bigm/{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]
.

The sheaves
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
are soft, which means that all germs in
all stalks are represented by global sections. See
[
15
, Chap. A,
§4]
. The sheaf
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\infty}
of smooth functions is soft, and the
definition of the direct image implies that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}
is soft. A finite
direct sum of skyscraper sheaves is soft as well.

For soft sheaves, the sheaf cohomology spaces vanish in all dimensions

i\geq 1
. In the long exact sequence associated to (
11.14
) we
have for
i\geq 2
the part

H^{i-1}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{Y}}\bigr)\rightarrow H^{i}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]\bigr)\rightarrow H^{i}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\backslash S}^{\ast}\bigr)\,.

The two outer spaces are zero, hence

H^{i}\bigl(\Gamma\backslash S^{\!\ast};{\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}]\bigr)
vanishes as well.
We use Proposition
4.5
for the relation with parabolic
cohomology spaces.
∎

Remarks.
Taking
i={\mathrm{d}}
in the corollary we get

H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}\bigr)=\{0\}
. With
Proposition
4.4
we conclude that the space of coinvariants

(\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu})_{\Gamma}
is zero. This is in contrast with the fact that

H^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{N}^{{\mathrm{excg}}}\bigr)=(\mathcal{N}^{{\mathrm{excg}}})_{\Gamma}
is
non-zero. Otherwise the construction of
u_{\psi}
in
Proposition
10.5
would not work.

A consequence for the case
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
is that the exact sequence in
(
11.13
) implies that

(11.15)

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\bigr)\cong H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)\,.

11.4.
The image of spaces of cusp forms in parabolic cohomology
spaces

We have the morphisms of
\Gamma
-modules

(11.16)

{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\rightarrow{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\,,\quad\text{
and }{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\,.

The former morphism is injective and the latter one surjective. The
composition is injective, and we can consider
{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
as
a submodule of
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
. Similarly, we have

{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
as a submodule of

\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
.

Proposition 11.9
.

Let

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
|\re\nu|<\rho
. We have
the direct sum decompositions of vector spaces

(11.17)

\displaystyle H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\oplus H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,,

\displaystyle H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{i}\,\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\oplus H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,,

where
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\cong\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)\cong\mathbf{i}\,\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
.

Proof.

The isomorphism in (
11.15
) and
Proposition
11.3
show that the kernel of
\alpha^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
in the
diagram
11.11
is equal to the image of

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}\bigl(\Gamma;{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)
in

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu})
intersected with

H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu})^{\mathrm{bdc}}
. The condition ‘bdc’
concerns the position of the boundary singularities, and can be applied
to
{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
as well as to
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}
. Together
with Proposition
10.8
this gives the second decomposition.
Pulling back along
\mathbf{i}
in diagram (
11.11
) we obtain the first
decomposition.
∎

Proof of part (

i

) of Theorem
B
.

The recapitulation in §
9.3.1
shows that for

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}

\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\subset\mathcal{V}^{\omega_{0}}_{\!\!\nu}=\mathop{\lim}_{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left((\partial S)\smallsetminus E\right)\,.

In this direct limit
E
runs over finite sets of cusps. Elements of

{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
represent boundary germs in

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\left(\partial S\smallsetminus E\right)
for such a finite set

E
. We indicate by
\rho_{\nu}{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
the space of
restrictions of these boundary germs. (See Definition
8.4
and
Theorem
8.5
for the restriction maps
\rho_{\nu}
.)

Application of the restriction map
\rho_{\nu}
to (
11.17
) leads for

\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
to

(11.18)

\displaystyle\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{i}\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}:{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma,\nu)\rightarrow H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\qquad\text{(injective)}\,,

\displaystyle H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\!\!\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}=\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma,\nu)\oplus H^{{\mathrm{d}}-1}_{\mathrm{pb}}\bigl(\Gamma;\rho_{\nu}{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\bigr)^{\mathrm{bdc}}\,.

This gives the injectivity of
\beta_{\nu}
in Theorem
B
, and
the assertion in part (

i

).
∎

12.
Global eigenfunctions of the Laplace operator

We defined in (
11.13
) the space

{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}\subset{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
as the kernel of

\Delta_{\nu}:{\mathcal{G}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\Delta}_{\nu}
. The subspace

{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},\infty,{\mathrm{excg}}}
is characterized by the condition

\rho_{\nu}f\in\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.
To complete the proof of the main result, Theorem
B
, stated
in the introduction, we prove in this section the following result.

Theorem 12.1
.

If
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and

|\re\nu|<\rho
, then
{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}=\{0\}
.

Proof of part (

ii

) of
Theorem
B
.

Immediate from Theorem
12.1
, together
with part (

i

) of Theorem
B
.
∎

We will use only part of the properties of functions in

{\mathcal{E}}^{\omega^{0},{\mathrm{excg}}}_{\nu}
, and will actually prove

Proposition 12.2
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and
|\re\nu|<\rho
. If

f\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
is bounded and represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary
germ on
\partial S\smallsetminus E
for some finite subset

E\subset\partial S
, then
f=0
.

This implies Theorem
12.1
, and will be a consequence of
Proposition
12.4
.

12.1.
Poisson transformation

Central in this
section is the Poisson transformation. It sends functions

\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial S)
to elements of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
:

(12.1)

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi(g\,\text{{{e}}})=\int_{b\in\partial S}r_{\nu}(b;g\,\text{{{e}}})\,\varphi(b)\,db=\int_{k\in K}\Phi_{\nu}\bigl(k^{-1}g\bigr)\,\varphi(k\,\infty)\,dk\,.

This transformation makes sense more generally: we may replace
\varphi
by
a distribution on
\partial S
, or even by a linear form on

C^{\omega}(\partial S)
.

Distributions.
We recall that a distribution on
\partial S
is a
linear form on
C^{\infty}(\partial S)
that is continuous for the
topology determined by the supremum norm of all derivatives on compact
sets.
(Since
\partial S
is compact we can work with the supremum norms of
derivatives on
\partial S
.) A function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial S)

determines the distribution
\varphi:\chi\mapsto\langle\varphi,\chi\rangle=\int_{k\in K}\varphi(k\,\infty)\,\chi(k\,\infty)\,dk
. By

C^{-\infty}(\partial S)
we indicate
the space of distributions on
\partial S
.

The action
\tau_{\nu}
on
C^{\infty}(\partial S)
satisfies

\bigl\langle\tau_{\nu}(g)\psi,\varphi\bigr\rangle=\bigl\langle\psi,\tau_{-\nu}(g^{-1})\varphi\bigr\rangle
. This relation
extends to distributions. Providing
C^{-\infty}(\partial S)
with the
action
\tau_{-\nu}
we obtain the dual
\mathcal{V}^{-\infty}_{\!\!-\nu}(\partial S)

of the representation
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
.

For a distribution
\beta
on
\partial S
we use the notation
\beta(f)
if

f\in C^{\infty}(\partial S)
. If we have a function given by some
expression
E
, then we use the notations

\beta\left(b\mapsto E(b)\right)
and
\beta\bigl(E(\cdot)\bigr)
.

Poisson transformation.
The Poisson transform
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
is the function on
S

obtained by applying
\beta
to

b\mapsto r_{\nu}(b;x)
:

(12.2)

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta(x)=\beta\bigl(r_{\nu}(\cdot;x)\bigr)\,.

Property (

ii

-

ii

b

) in Proposition
6.1

implies that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
. The Poisson transformation
intertwines the action
\tau_{-\nu}
with the action
L
on

{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
. Lewis has shown
[
31
, Theorem 5.3]
that the image

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}C^{-\infty}(\partial S)
is equal to the space of all
elements
f\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
with polynomial growth at the boundary. He
works under the condition that
\nu\not\in\mathbb{Z}
and that
\lambda=-i\nu

is “simple”. He discusses that these two conditions lead to the
requirements
\nu\not\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\nu\not\in-p/2+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. So
the use of this theorem forces us to add
\nu\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
to
the conditions on the spectral parameter.

The support
\supp(\varphi)

of a function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial S)
is the closure of the set

x\in\partial S
such that
\varphi(x)\neq 0
. The support of a
distribution
\beta
on
\partial S
consists of the points

x\in\partial S
such that
\beta(\varphi)\neq 0
for some

\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial S)
with
x\in\supp\varphi
.

A large part of this section will be needed to establish the following
result.

Proposition 12.3
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}

and
|\re\nu|<\rho
. Let
\beta\in C^{-\infty}(\partial S)
. The set

\partial S
is the disjoint union of the sets

\supp(\beta)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bigl\{x\in\partial S\;:\;\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta\in(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{x}\bigr\}\,.

The statement that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
is an element of the stalk

(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{p}
means that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
represents a
\nu
-analytic
boundary germ on a neighborhood of
p
in
\partial S
.

Proposition
12.2
will follow from
Proposition
12.3
and the following result:

Proposition 12.4
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
satisfy
\rho+\re\nu>0

and
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Suppose that
the support of
\beta\in C^{-\infty}(C)
consists of finitely many
elements of
\partial S
.
If
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
is bounded on
S
, then
\beta=0
.

We give a proof in the next subsection.

12.2.
Distributions on
N\,\text{{{o}}}

Functions

\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\partial S)
determine a function
\varphi^{\mathrm{prj}}
on

N\,\text{{{o}}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
by

\varphi^{\mathrm{prj}}(x)=\varphi\bigl(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{o}}(\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{w})\,{\boldsymbol{\infty}})
.
If
\varphi
has support in
N\,\text{{{o}}}
, then integration over
\partial S

can be formulated as integration over

(\partial S)\smallsetminus\{{\boldsymbol{\infty}}\}=N\,\text{{{o}}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{p+q}

(12.3)

\int_{\partial S}\varphi(b)\,db=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p+q}}\varphi^{\mathrm{prj}}(x)\,a_{0}\,\bigl((1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{-2\rho}\,dx\,.

with the standard Lebesgue measure
dx
on
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
, and where

a_{0}>0
is chosen in accordance with the total volume
1
of

\partial S
for the invariant measure
db
on
K/M
.
(Compare
[
21
, (3) p 1, Theorem 1.14 p 52]
.)
We have seen in Proposition
2.1
that

(12.4)

(1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}=\mathbf{t}\bigl(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x)\bigr)^{-2}\,.

In this way we can work with functions on
\partial S
in terms of
functions on
N\,\text{{{o}}}
. In the context of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
this is called
the
projective model
in
[
4
, §2.1]
.

Helgason gives in
[
21
, Corollary 1.18 p 65]
an explicit formula
for the Poisson kernel on
N\,\text{{{o}}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
. Helgason works with

\mathbf{t}_{\mathrm{o}}\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y)^{-1}k\bigr)
, and we take

\mathbf{t}\bigl(k^{-1}\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)
. For
x=(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}

and
y>0
we obtain

(12.5)

r_{\nu}^{\mathrm{prj}}\bigl(x;\,\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})=y^{\rho+\nu}\Bigl(\frac{(1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}}{(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}}\Bigr)^{(\rho+\nu)/2}\,.

The positive constant
c
is not normalized here in the same way as in

[
21
]
; see the proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Although conceptually nice, the projective model is less convenient than
the
line model
of the functions

\varphi^{\mathrm{lnm}}(x)=\bigl((1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\varphi^{\mathrm{prj}}(x)
. In the line model
the kernel of the Poisson transformation takes the form

(12.6)

\displaystyle r_{\nu}^{\mathrm{lnm}}(x;\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=y^{\rho+\nu}\Bigl(\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2})^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t}\bigl(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\bigr)^{\rho+\nu}\,.

See (
2.23
).

If
n,n_{1}\in N
, then

(12.7)

r_{\nu}^{\mathrm{lnm}}(n;n_{1}\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})=r_{\nu}^{\mathrm{lnm}}(n_{1}^{-1}n;\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\,.

Proof of Proposition
12.4
.

Let
\beta
be a
distribution with finite support. With a partition of unity we can
write it as a sum of distributions with support consisting of one
point. The action of
K
enables us to consider distributions with

\supp(\beta)=\{\text{{{o}}}\}
.

In this way we have a distribution
\beta
on
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}\cong N\,\text{{{o}}}

with support
\{0\}
. Such distributions are a linear combination of
finitely many derivatives of the
\delta
-distribution

\delta:\varphi\mapsto\varphi(0)
. We use multi-indices

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p+q})
to describe derivatives as

\partial^{\alpha}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{p+q}}^{\alpha_{p+q}}
,
and put
|\alpha|=\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}\alpha_{j}
. We put
N(x,y)=N(x^{(1)},x^{(2)},y)=\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}
and write
\rho+\nu=2b
. By (
12.7
) we have

(12.8)

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\bigl(\partial^{\alpha}\delta\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{n}(-x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}\frac{y^{2b}}{N(x,y)^{b}}\,.

Since
\partial^{\alpha}\delta
is a non-zero distribution, its Poisson
transform is non-zero, and is analytic on
S
. So

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\bigl(\partial^{\alpha}\delta\bigr)
is not identically zero
near
e
. Hence the polynomial
p_{\alpha}\bigl(x,y\bigr)
such that

(12.9)

(-1)^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}\frac{y^{2b}}{N(x,y)^{b}}=y^{2b}p_{\alpha}(x,y)N(x,y)^{-b-|\alpha|}

is not the zero polynomial. In each differentiation step the total
degree of
p_{\alpha}
increases at most by
3
.

If
p_{\alpha}(0,y)\neq 0
the function

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\bigl(\partial^{\alpha}\delta\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{n}(-x)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)
is given by

p_{\alpha}(0,y)y^{-2b-4|\alpha|}
with
3|\alpha|+\re(-2b-4|\alpha|)<0
, since the
total degree of
p_{\alpha}
is smaller than
3|\alpha|
, and since
N(x,y)

has degree
4
in
y
. It is unbounded as
y\downarrow 0
. If

p_{\alpha}(0,y)=0
we take a curve

(12.10)

y\mapsto\bigl(a_{1}y^{d_{1}},\ldots,a_{p+q}y^{d_{p+q}}\bigr)

with
d_{j}=\frac{1}{2}
for
1\leq j\leq p
and
d_{j}=1
for

p+1\leq j\leq p+q
, and
a_{j}
such that
p_{\alpha}
is not identically
zero along this curve by similar degree arguments. Then the Poisson
transform is unbounded along this curve. So the Poisson transforms of
the simple derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}\delta
are unbounded.

So let us consider a finite linear combination

\beta=\sum_{i}c_{i}\partial^{\alpha(i)}\delta
. Its Poisson transform at

\mathrm{n}(x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}
is the linear combination

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta=\sum_{i}c_{i}y^{2b}p_{\alpha(i)}(x,y)N(x,y)^{-b-|\alpha(i)|}\,.

We can write
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta\bigl(\mathrm{n}(-x)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})
as

y^{2b}P(x,y)\,N(x,y)^{-b-M}
, with
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and a
polynomial
P
with total degree smaller than
M
. This new function
cannot be identically zero if
\beta
is not the zero linear combination,
by Lewis’s theorem. We again construct a curve of the same form as in
(
12.10
) through
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}\times(0,\infty)
approaching

0\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q+1}
along which
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
is unbounded.

So a bounded function in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(S)
cannot be of this form.
∎

12.3.
Approximation of distributions

We still have to prove
Proposition
12.3
. It can be localized to a neighborhood
of
o
in
N\,\text{{{o}}}
. First we discuss the approximation of
distributions by functions.

The convolution of functions
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(N)
is defined by

(12.11)

\psi\ast\varphi(n)=\int_{N}\psi(nn_{1}^{-1})\varphi(n_{1})\,dn_{1}=\int_{N}\psi(n_{1})\varphi(n_{1}^{-1}n)\,dn_{1}\,.

The linear map
\varphi\mapsto\psi\ast\varphi
is continuous for the supremum
norm of all derivatives of
\varphi
up to a given order. For

\chi\in C^{\boldsymbol{\infty}}(N)
we have

(12.12)

\int_{N}\chi(n)(\psi\ast\varphi)(n)\,dn=\int_{N}(\check{\psi}\ast\chi)(n)\,\varphi(n)\,dn\,,

with
\check{\psi}(n)=\psi(n^{-1})
. This shows how to deal with the
convolution of
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(N)
with a distribution
\beta
with
compact support:

(12.13)

(\psi\ast\beta):\chi\mapsto\beta\bigl(\check{\psi}\ast\chi\bigr)\,.

The distribution
\check{\psi}\ast\beta
is given by a function in

C_{c}^{\infty}(N)
; see eg.
[
37
, 17-10, Thm 7.5]
.

A Dirac sequence with shrinking support is a sequence

\bigl(\psi_{m}\bigr)_{m}
of elements in
C_{c}^{\infty}(N)
such that

\psi_{m}\geq 0
, and
\int_{N}\psi_{m}(n)\,dn=1
for all
m
, for which the
supports
\supp\psi_{m}
form a shrinking sequence of compact sets with
intersection
\{e\}
. See
[
29
, Chap VIII, §3]
. We can choose
the Dirac sequence such that
\check{\psi}_{m}=\psi_{m}
. The important
property is that
\psi_{m}\ast\chi
converges uniformly to

\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(N)
, and the same holds for all derivatives
(under right differentiation).

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
\beta
be a distribution on
N
with
compact support. If
\psi_{m}
is a Dirac sequence with shrinking support,
then

\mathrm{(a)}

For each
\chi\in C^{\infty}(N)

\beta(\chi)=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\int_{n\in N}(\psi_{m}\ast\beta)(n)\,\chi(n)\,dn\,.

\mathrm{(b)}

Let
n\in N
. Then
n\in\supp(\beta)
if and only
if
n\in\supp(\psi_{m}\ast\beta)
for all sufficiently large
m
.

\mathrm{(c)}

If
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
represents an element of the
stalk
(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{x}
, then
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}(\psi_{m}\ast\chi)
represents an
element of this stalk for all sufficiently large
m
.

We note that part (

b

) gives an equivalence, whereas part
(

c

) gives only one implication.

Proof.

With the relation

\int_{N}(\psi_{m}\ast\beta)(n)\chi(n)\,dn=\int_{N}\beta(n_{1})\,(\check{\psi}_{m}(n_{1})\ast\chi)(n_{1})\,dn_{1}\,,

part (

a

) follows directly from the convergence of

\psi_{m}\ast\chi
to
\chi
.

For part (

b

) we consider
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}
. Part
(

a

) shows that
\beta(\chi)\neq 0
if and only if

(\psi_{m}\ast\beta)(\chi)\neq 0
for all large values of
m
.

For part (

c

) suppose that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta(n\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

is
\nu
-analytic on an open set
I\subset N\,\text{{{o}}}
containing
x
.
Then there is an analytic function
B
on an open set
U\subset\hat{S}

containing
I
such that

(12.14)

\beta\bigl(r^{\mathrm{lnm}}_{\nu}(\cdot,\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\bigr)=y^{\rho+\nu}B(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

for
\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\in S\cap U
. Since the support of
\psi_{m}

shrinks to
e
, we have for all large values of
m

(12.15)

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}(\psi_{m}\ast\beta)(\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=y^{\rho+\nu}\int_{N}\psi_{m}(n_{1})B\bigl(n_{1}^{-1}\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)\,dn_{1}

for all
\mathrm{n}(\xi)\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\in S\cap U_{m}
for some neighborhood

U_{m}
of
x
in
\hat{S}
.
∎

12.4.
Proof of Proposition
12.3

To prove
Proposition
12.3
we have to show that for all

b\in\partial S

(12.16)

b\not\in\supp(\beta)\;\Leftrightarrow\;\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta\text{ represents an element of
}(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{b}\,.

We first consider the forward implication.

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
,
and
\beta\in\mathcal{V}^{-\infty}_{\!\!\nu}(\partial S)
. If

b\in(\partial S)\smallsetminus\supp(\beta)
, then
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta

represents an element of the stalk
(\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu})_{b}
.

Proof.

If
\supp(\beta)=\partial S
the lemma holds trivially. So
let
\supp(\beta)
be a compact subset of
\partial S
not containing a
given point
b
. The Poisson transform

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=\beta\Bigl(b_{1}\mapsto r_{\nu}(b_{1};k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\Bigr)

depends only on the values of
r_{\nu}(b_{1};k\mathrm{a}\,\text{{{e}}})
for

b_{1}\in\supp(\beta)
. We write

r_{\nu}(b_{1};k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})=t^{-\rho-\nu}A(b_{1};k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\,,

since the factor
t^{-\rho-\nu}
is left out of consideration in the
definition of
\nu
-analytic boundary germs. So for
k\in K
such that

k\,\infty
varies through a neighborhood of
b
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta(k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=\beta\Bigl(b_{1}\mapsto t^{-\rho-\nu}A(b_{1},k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=t^{-\rho-\nu}\,\beta\Bigl(b_{1}\mapsto A(b_{1},k\mathrm{a}(t)\,\text{{{e}}})\Bigr)\,.

The function
A(b_{1},x)
extends analytically from
x\in S
near
b
to
a neighborhood of
b
in
\hat{S}
. So the Poisson transform

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\beta
represents a
\nu
-analytic boundary germ on a
neighborhood of
b
in
\partial S
.∎

We are left with the converse implication in (
12.16
).

Reduction to functions.
We can localize
\beta
such that

\supp(\beta)\subset N\,\text{{{o}}}
, and
b=\text{{{o}}}
. Parts (

c

) and
(

b

) in Lemma
12.5
implies that there are
functions
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(N\,\text{{{o}}})
approximating
\beta
such that
the converse implication in (
12.16
) is implied by the
analogous implication with
\beta
replaced by

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(N\,\text{{{o}}})
. So we have to prove:

Lemma 12.7
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
,

|\re\nu|<\rho
. If
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi
represents a
\nu
-analytic
boundary germ on an open set
I\subset N\,\text{{{o}}}
, then
\varphi(b)=0
for
all
b\in I
.

Proof.

With the action of
N
we can transform any
b\in I
to
b=\text{{{o}}}
. Then
Lemma
12.8
below implies that
\varphi(\text{{{o}}})=0
. We can carry
out the transformation of
b
to
o
for any
b\in I
. Hence each

b\in I
is not in
\supp\varphi
.∎

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}\smallsetminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and

|\re\nu|<\rho
. If

y\mapsto y^{-\rho-\nu}\,\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

extends analytically to
y=0
, then
\varphi(\text{{{o}}})=0
.

The proof requires auxiliary lemmas, and is completed at the end of this
subsection.

Explicit Poisson integral.
To prove Lemma
12.8
we use
the explicit formula in (
12.6
), which is based on

N\,\text{{{o}}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
.

(12.17)

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=y^{\rho+\nu}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}}\varphi\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\,dx\,.

We have
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p+q})
and
y>0
. Convergence is
guaranteed by the compactness of
\supp(\varphi)
. The integral is analytic
in
y\in(0,\infty)
.

The transformation of variables
x^{(1)}\mapsto yx^{(1)}
,

x^{(2)}\mapsto y^{2}x^{(2)}
, implies
dx\mapsto y^{2\rho}\,dx
.
This leads to the alternative explicit formula

(12.18)

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle=y^{\rho-\nu}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}}\varphi\bigl(yx^{(1)},y^{2}x^{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\,dx\,.

For our purpose it is important to leave the factor
y^{\rho+\nu}
out
of consideration. We study the following
function

(12.19)

\displaystyle A(\varphi

\displaystyle;\rho+\nu,y)=y^{-\rho-\nu}\,\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}}\varphi\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)}\bigr)\Bigl(\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\,dx

\displaystyle=y^{-2\nu}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}}\varphi\bigl(yx^{(1)},y^{2}x^{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(1+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\,dx

modulo the subspace
\mathsf{An}(0)
of functions on
(0,\infty)

that extend analytically to a neighborhood of
[0,\infty)
in
\mathbb{R}
.

We split up the Poisson integral by integration, separately over the
region

(12.20)

\ell(\delta)=\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}\;:\;\|x^{(1)}\|\leq\delta\text{ and }\|x^{(2)}\|\leq\delta^{2}\bigr\}\,,

and over its complement in
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
, for
\delta>0
.

Lemma 12.9
.

For
\delta>0

put

(12.21)

\displaystyle I_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\ell(\delta)}\varphi(x^{(1)},x^{(2)})

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\,dx\,.

Then
\nu\mapsto I_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)
is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}
, and

(12.22)

y^{-\rho-\nu}\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}(\varphi)\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})\equiv\,I_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)\bmod\mathsf{An}(0)\,.

Proof.

We have

(12.23)

\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})=y^{\rho+\nu}I_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)+y^{\rho+\nu}A_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)\,,

where

(12.24)

\displaystyle A_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\|x^{(1)}\|>\delta\text{ or }\|x^{(2)}\|>\delta^{2}}\varphi\bigl(x^{(1)},x^{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}\Bigr)^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\,dx\,.

The compact support of
\varphi
takes care of convergence of the integrals
for
I_{\delta}
and
A_{\delta}
, and implies that both depend holomorphically
on
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. Furthermore, the quantity

\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x^{(1)}\|^{2}\bigr)^{2}+4c\|x^{(2)}\|^{2}
is larger than

y^{4}
. Hence the integral for
A_{\delta}
depends analytically on

y\in\mathbb{R}
and
A_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)\in\mathsf{An}(0)
.
∎

Lemma 12.10
.

Let
\re\nu>-\rho
. Suppose that

\varphi(x)={\mathrm{O}}\bigl(\|x\|\bigr)
on its domain. If
0<\delta\leq y\leq 1
, then

(12.25)

\displaystyle I_{\delta}\bigl(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)

\displaystyle\ll\delta^{2\rho+1}y^{-2(\rho+\nu)}

\displaystyle\ll y^{-2\nu+1}\qquad\text{ if }\delta=y\,.

Proof.

On the domain of integration the integrand is estimated by

{\mathrm{O}}\left(\bigl(\|x^{(1)}\|+\|x^{(2)}\|\bigr)y^{-2(\rho+\nu)}\right)\,.

The integral of
x^{(1)}
over a ball in dimension
p
is, up to a
factor, the integral of
r_{1}=\bigl\|x^{(1)}\|
against the measure

r_{1}^{p_{1}-1}\,dr_{1}
, and similarly for the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. In this
way, we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{\ell(\delta)}

\displaystyle\bigl(\|x^{(1)}\|+\|x^{(2)}\|\bigr)\,dx

\displaystyle\ll\int_{r_{1}=0}^{\delta}\int_{r_{2}=0}^{\delta^{2}}\,(r_{1}+r_{2})r_{1}^{p-1}r_{2}^{q-1}\,dr_{1}\,dr_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{1}=0}^{\delta}r_{1}^{p}\,dr_{1}\,\int_{r_{2}=0}^{\delta^{2}}r_{2}^{q-1}\,dr_{2}+\int_{r_{1}=0}^{\delta}r_{1}^{p-1}\,dr_{1}\,\int_{r_{2}=0}^{\delta^{2}}r_{2}^{q}\,dr_{2}

\displaystyle\ll\delta^{p+1}\delta^{2q}+\delta^{p}\delta^{2(q+1)}\ll\delta^{2\rho+1}\,.

This gives the estimate

I_{\delta}\ll\delta^{2\rho+1}y^{-2(\rho+\nu)}\ll y^{1-2\nu}\,.

In the last step we use
\delta\leq y
.
∎

We can compute
I_{\delta}(\varphi;\rho+\nu,y)
explicitly if
\varphi
is polynomial
on the domain of integration. To carry this out for
\varphi(x)=1
on

\ell(\delta)
, we first consider the
integral

(12.26)

P_{\!n,\delta}(h,F):t\mapsto\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\|x\|\leq\delta}F(t^{2}+h\|x\|^{2})\,dx

for
F
analytic on
(0,\infty)
. We take
\delta>0
and

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and denote by
c_{n}
the positive factor such that

dx=c_{n}\,r^{n-1}\,dr
for
r=\|x\|
. To avoid long
notations we indicate the power function
u\mapsto|u|^{-w}
as

|\cdot|^{-w}
.

Proposition 12.11
.

The integral
P_{\!n,\delta}
for some
choices of
F
.

\mathrm{(i)}

Let
F(\xi)=\xi^{w}
with
\re w>0
.

\mathrm{(a)}

If
w-\frac{n}{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
then

(12.27)

\displaystyle P_{\!n,\delta}\bigl(h,|\cdot|^{-w}\bigr)(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{n}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{n}{2}\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(w-\frac{n}{2}\bigr)}{2\,h^{n/2}\,\Gamma(w)}t^{n-2w}

\displaystyle\hbox{}+\frac{c_{n}\,\delta^{n-2w}\,h^{-w}}{n-2w}{}_{2}F_{\!1}\Bigl(w,w-\frac{n}{2},w-\frac{n}{2}+1;-\frac{t^{2}}{h\delta^{2}}\Bigr)\,.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
w=\frac{n}{2}
then

(12.28)

P_{\!n,\delta}\bigl(h,|\cdot|^{-w}\bigr)(t)=-c_{n}h^{-n/2}\log t+\bigl(c_{n}h^{-n/2}\,\log\delta+(\mathrm{cst})\bigr)\,,

where
(\mathrm{cst})
is a real constant, not depending on
\delta
.

\mathrm{(c)}

If
w=\frac{n}{2}-k
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,n/2-1]
then

(12.29)

\displaystyle P_{\!n,\delta}\bigl(h,|\cdot|^{-w}\bigr)(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{n}\,(-1)^{k}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{n}{2}\bigr)}{h^{n/2}\,k!\;\bigl(\frac{n}{2}-k-1\bigr)!}\,t^{2k}\,\log t

\displaystyle\hbox{}+\frac{c_{n}\,(-1)^{k}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{n}{2}\bigr)}{h^{n/2}\,k!\;\bigl(\frac{n}{2}-k-1\bigr)!}\bigl(\log\delta+(\mathrm{cst})\bigr)\,t^{2k}\,.

\mathrm{(ii)}

If
F\in\mathsf{An}(0)
, then
P_{\!n,\delta}(h,F)\in\mathsf{An}(0)
.

Proof.

We rewrite (
12.26
) as

(12.30)

P_{\!n,\delta}(h,F)(t)=c_{n}\int_{r=0}^{\delta}F(t^{2}+hr^{2})\,r^{n-1}\,dr\,.

Applied with
F(\xi)=\xi^{-w}
we get

(12.31)

=\frac{c_{n}\delta^{n}}{n}{}_{2}F_{\!1}\Bigl(w,\frac{n}{2};\frac{n}{2}+1;-\frac{\delta^{2}h}{t^{2}}\Bigr)\,.

This hypergeometric function can be written as a linear combination of
hypergeometric functions that are holomorphic at
0
. We use the
relation in
[
32
, Chap II, 2.9]
expressing
u_{1}
in terms of

u_{3}
and
u_{4}
(in the notations used there). This relation writes the
holomorphic function
P_{\!n,\delta}
in
w
as the sum of two meromorphic
terms. The singularities occur at

w\in\frac{n}{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. We use that

{}_{2}F_{\!1}(a,0;c;z)=1
, and arrive at (
12.27
).

If
w\in\frac{n}{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
we have to work with another basis of the
solutions of the hypergeometric differential equation. The expression
in (
12.31
) is holomorphic in
w
for
w\in\mathbb{C}
. Figuring out how
the singularities in both terms in the right-hand side cancel each
other, we arrive at (

i

-

i

b

) and (

i

-

i

c

).

In part (

ii

) we get the integral

\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\|x\|\leq\delta}F(t^{2}+h\|x\|^{2})\,dx=c_{n}\int_{r=0}^{\delta}F(t^{2}+hr^{2})r^{n-1}\,dr

of an analytic function on a compact region, which results in an
analytic function of
t>0
. If
F
extends analytically to a
neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
, then the integral is analytic in
t
on a
neighborhood of
0
.
∎

In the three cases in part (

i

) of this proposition the
integral
P_{\!n,\delta}
is the sum of two terms. The first term is a
multiple of a simple function of
t
, which is not analytically
extendable to
t=0
. The second term is analytically extendable to a
neighborhood of
t=0
. (We note that
k
is positive in
(

i

-

i

c

).)
In each of the three cases, the first term does not depend on the
truncation parameter
\delta
.

Proof of Lemma
12.8
for
q=0
.

In
this case
\rho=\frac{p}{2}
, and
|\re\nu|<\frac{p}{2}
.

We write the function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
on the region

\|x\|\leq\delta
as the constant function
\varphi(0)
and

\varphi_{1}(x)=\varphi(x)-\varphi(0)
. The Poisson integral in (
12.17
)
simplifies to

(12.32)

\displaystyle y^{-\rho-\nu}

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\varphi(x)\,\bigl(y^{2}+c\|x\|^{2}\bigr)^{-\rho-\nu}\,dx

\displaystyle\equiv\;I_{\delta}(\varphi(0)+\varphi_{1};\rho+\nu,y)\bmod\mathsf{An}(0)

by Lemma
12.9
. We take
0\leq\delta\leq y\leq 1
. The main term
is given by
P_{\!p,\delta}(c,|\cdot|^{-p/2-\nu})
. So in
Proposition
12.11
(

i

) we use
n=p
and

w=\frac{p}{2}+\nu
. Hence
w-\frac{n}{2}=\nu
. The parts (

i

b

) and
(

i

c

) occur for values of
\nu
in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
which
we have to exclude anyhow, since we use Lewis’s theorem in
§
12.1
. The main term is

(12.33)

\varphi(0)\Bigl(b_{0}y^{-2\nu}+\delta^{-2\nu}a_{0}(t^{2}/\delta^{2})\Bigr)\,,

with
b_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
a_{0}\in\mathsf{An}(0)
. The dependence on
\delta
is
explicit.

Lemma
12.10
gives

(12.34)

I_{\delta}(\varphi_{1};\rho+\nu,y)={\mathrm{O}}\bigl(\delta^{p+1}y^{-p-2\nu}\bigr)\,.

The assumption that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi(\mathrm{a}(y))\,\in\mathsf{An}(0)
extends
analytically to
y=0
implies that modulo
\mathsf{An}(0)
with

0<\delta\leq y\leq 1

(12.35)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{P}_{\!\nu}\varphi\bigl(\mathrm{a}(y)\,\text{{{e}}})

\displaystyle\equiv I_{\delta}(\varphi(0)+\varphi_{1};\rho+\nu;y)

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma~\ref{lem-Idt}}

\displaystyle\equiv\varphi(0)b_{0}y^{-2\nu}+I_{\delta}(\varphi_{1};\rho+\nu;y)

\displaystyle\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop-PI}}

\displaystyle=\varphi(0)b_{0}y^{-2\nu}+{\mathrm{O}}\bigl(y^{1-2\nu})

\displaystyle\delta=y\text{ in
Lemma~\ref{lem-I-estm}}\,.

For
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
the non-analytic factor
y^{-2\nu}
cannot
cancel with an element of
\mathsf{An}(0)
. As
y\downarrow 0
it is larger than
the
{\mathrm{O}}
-term. Since
b_{0}\neq 0
we need that
\varphi(0)=0
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
12.8
for
q\geq 1
.

The idea of the proof is the same as for
q=0
, except that now the
operators in (
12.26
) have to be applied twice.

We start with
P_{\!q,\delta^{2}}(4c,|\cdot|^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\bigr)(t)
for

t>0
. We have
w=\frac{\rho+\nu}{2}
and
n=\frac{q}{2}
. We use
\delta^{2}
,
in view of the condition
\|x^{(2)}\|\leq\delta^{2}
in the definition of

\ell(\delta)
in (
12.20
). So
w-\frac{n}{2}=\frac{p}{4}+\frac{\nu}{2}
.
Since we work under the condition
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
the
exceptional values in part (

i

-

i

a

) of Proposition

12.11
are avoided, and we get

(12.36)

P_{\!q,\delta^{2}}(4c,|\cdot|^{-(\rho+\nu)/2}\bigr)(t)\equiv b_{1}t^{-\nu-p/2}\bmod\mathsf{An}(0)\,,

with a nonzero quantity
b_{1}
.

We apply Proposition
12.11
(

i

-

i

a

) to

I_{p,\delta}(c,F)
with
F(\xi)=\xi^{-p/2-\nu}
. So
w-n=\nu
and we
get

(12.37)

P_{\!p,\delta}\bigl(c,|\cdot|^{-p/2-\nu}\bigr)(y)\equiv b_{2}y^{-2\nu}\bmod\mathsf{An}(0)\,,

with
b_{2}\neq 0
if
\nu\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. Part (

ii

)
shows that the other term from the first stage, which is in
\mathsf{An}(0)
, is
sent to an element of
\mathsf{An}(0)
. Now we can proceed like in the case

q=0
.∎

13.
Concluding remarks

Comparison with
[
4
]
.
The case of cofinite discrete
subgroups of
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
acting on the complex upper half-plane is
treated in
[
4
]
. We followed its approach as far as possible.
We restricted the generalization to symmetric spaces of non-compact Lie
groups of real rank one in several ways:

•

We did not consider cocompact discrete groups.

•

We simplified our work by assuming that the cofinite discrete
subgroup
\Gamma
has no torsion.

•

We considered only cusp forms, whereas in
[
4
]
more
general invariant eigenfunctions were considered.

•

We did not use mixed parabolic cohomology; as in
[
4
, Definition
10.1]
. It seemed simpler to work with cohomology spaces
satisfying the condition “
{\mathrm{bdc}}
”; see (
6.23
). The integrals in
Propositions
6.4
(

i

) and
9.4

give directly cocycles that satisfy this condition.

Theorem B in
[
4
]
states that spaces of Maass cusp forms are
isomorphic to several mixed parabolic cohomology spaces, with values in
various modules, without a condition “bdc” that we use here.
Proposition 13.1 in
[
4
]
may be called ‘separation of singularities’. It implies
that
if the boundary singularities of an element
f\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
form a
finite set
E
, we can decompose
f
as
\sum_{e\in E}f_{e}
with

f_{e}\in\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
with
\mathsf{bdSing}_{\nu}(f_{e})\subset\{e\}
.
In the context of groups of real rank we did not establish a similar result.
Hence the
concept of mixed parabolic cohomology groups in
[
4
]
had to be
replaced by the condition “
{\mathrm{bdc}}
” determining a subspace of
parabolic cohomology spaces related to
\mathcal{W}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
and

\mathcal{V}^{\omega^{0}}_{\!\!\nu}
. Various steps are much more complicated than
in
[
4
]
. Compare, for instance, the proof of the isomorphism

\mathcal{W}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}\cong\mathcal{V}^{\omega}_{\!\!\nu}
in Theorem
8.5
with the proof of

[
3
, Theorem 5.6]
, or the discussion in §
11
with

[
3
, Proposition 5.3 and Lemma 5.4]
.

The spectral parameter.
The spectral parameter
\nu
parametrizes
the characters of
A
, the spherical principal series representations,
and the eigenvalues of the Laplace operator.

Proposition
3.8
states that cusp forms can occur only for

\nu\in i\mathbb{R}\cup[-\rho,\rho]
. Proposition
5.2
cites a
result of Kostant on the values of
\nu
for which the spherical
principal series is irreducible.

The main result Theorem
B
concerns values of
\nu\in\mathbb{C}

that satisfy
|\re\nu|<\rho
and
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
,
with the nicest result for
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. These restrictions
arise in the following way.

•

The linear map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
in Theorem
6.6
for the space

{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(\Gamma;\nu)
of cusp forms to a parabolic cohomology space can be
constructed for all
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. It is based on the Poisson kernel

r_{\nu}
in Proposition
6.1
. Of course,
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}}}_{\nu}
is the
zero map if
\nu
does not satisfy
\rho^{2}-\nu^{2}\geq 0
.

•

The
\nu
-analytic boundary germs in Section
8
can be
defined for all
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. They are useful for our purpose only if
we have the isomorphism in Theorem
8.5
, for which

\re\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
is required.

•

In Section
9
we construct a map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
from spaces
of cusp forms to cohomology spaces with values in a module of

\nu
-analytic boundary germs. We need the kernel function
q_{\nu}
in
Proposition
9.1
, which, like Theorem
8.5
, requires

\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}
.

•

Proposition
9.5
shows that under the additional
condition
\re\nu>-\rho
the map
\beta^{\!\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}}_{\nu}
has image in a module of

\nu
-analytic boundary germs with a special behavior at some cusps. We
need the condition
\re\nu>-\rho
in the proof of Proposition
10.5
to characterize cusp forms with help of Lemma
3.12
.

•

Section
10
constructs cusp forms from given cocycles. In
the proof of Proposition
10.5
we have to show that the
eigenfunction of
\Delta
that we have constructed is a cusp form.
We use Lemma
3.12
to show this,
now needing
\re\nu<\rho
as well.

The conditions
|\re\nu|<\rho
and
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}

allow us to prove in Section
11

part (

i

) in Theorem
B
.

•

To show part (

ii

) of the main result we use the
Poisson transformation in Section
12
. We use a result of Lewis in

[
31
]
that needs
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
We have worked under the condition
\nu\not\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, thus
avoiding complicated case distinctions. If one is able to extend
Lewis’s result to
\nu=0
it might be worthwhile to attempt to try to
show that Proposition
10.5
works for
\nu=0
as well.

Allowing torsion.
The restriction to torsion-free discrete groups

\Gamma
of motions in
S
is practical. Several technical complications
are avoided. By going over to vector-value cusp forms, our results
probably can be extended to more general discrete groups that contain a
torsion-free subgroup of finite index.

Transfer operator.
For
\PSL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
a parabolic cocycle
\psi
can
be determined by the value on
S=\left[{\hfill 0\@@atop\hfill 1}{\hfill-1\@@atop\hfill 0}\right]
, which gives an
analytic function on
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\!\mathbb{R}}\smallsetminus\{0,\infty\}
. The cocycle
relations give rise to a three term relation satisfied by the
restriction of
\psi_{S}
to the interval
(0,\infty)
. In the study of
the associated transfer operator, see Mayer
[
33
]
, it is
important that this relation is contracting on the functions
on
(0,\infty)
.

In the case
{\mathrm{d}}\geq 3
removal of a finite number of cusps from

\partial S
leaves us with a connected set. It remains to be seen
whether our result can help in the study of discretization of the
geodesic flow on
S
.
